 Abstract 
 We update the constraints on the fraction of the Universe that may have gone into primordial black holes (PBHs) over the mass range 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 50 ​ g 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{50}\,\mathrm{g} .
Those smaller than ∼ 10 15 ​ g \sim 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} would have evaporated by now due to Hawking radiation, so their abundance at formation is constrained by the effects of evaporated particles on big bang nucleosynthesis, the cosmic microwave background (CMB), the Galactic and extragalactic γ \gamma -ray and cosmic ray backgrounds and the possible generation of stable Planck mass relics.
PBHs larger than ∼ 10 15 ​ g \sim 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} are subject to a variety of constraints associated with gravitational lensing, dynamical effects, influence on large-scale structure, accretion and gravitational waves.
We discuss the constraints on both the initial collapse fraction and the current fraction of the CDM in PBHs at each mass scale but stress that many of the constraints are associated with observational or theoretical uncertainties.
We also consider indirect constraints associated with the amplitude of the primordial density fluctuations, such as second-order tensor perturbations and μ \mu -distortions arising from the effect of acoustic reheating on the CMB, if PBHs are created from the high- σ \sigma peaks of nearly Gaussian fluctuations.
Finally we discuss how the constraints are modified if the PBHs have an extended mass function, this being relevant if PBHs provide some combination of the dark matter, the LIGO/Virgo coalescences and the seeds for cosmic structure.
Even if PBHs make a small contribution to the dark matter, they could play an important cosmological role and provide a unique probe of the early Universe.

 I Introduction 
 I.1 Overview 
 Primordial black holes (PBHs) have been a source of intense interest for more than 50 years Zel’dovich and Novikov 1967 , despite the fact that there is still no evidence for them.
One reason for this interest is that only PBHs could be small enough for Hawking radiation to be important Hawking 1974 .
This has not yet been confirmed experimentally but this discovery is generally recognised as one of the key developments in 20th century physics and Hawking was only led to it through contemplating the properties of PBHs.
Indeed, those smaller than about 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} would have evaporated by now but could still have many interesting cosmological consequences. 
 PBHs much larger than 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} are unaffected by Hawking radiation but have also attracted interest because of the possibility that they provide the dark matter (DM) which comprises 25 % of the critical density Ade et al. 2016 , an idea that goes back to the earliest days of PBH research Chapline 1975 .
If PBHs formed at all, they would probably have done so before the end of the radiation-dominated era and are not subject to the well-known big bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) constraint that baryons can have at most 5 % of the critical density Cyburt et al. 2003 .
They should therefore be classed as non-baryonic and from a dynamical perspective they behave like any other cold dark matter (CDM) candidate.
There is still no compelling evidence that PBHs provide the DM, but nor is there for any of the more traditional CDM candidates. 
 Despite the lack of evidence for them, PBHs have been invoked to explain numerous cosmological features.
For example, evaporating PBHs have been invoked to explain the extragalactic Page and Hawking 1976 and Galactic Lehoucq et al. 2009 γ \gamma -ray backgrounds, antimatter in cosmic rays Barrau 2000 , the annihilation line radiation from the Galactic centre Okele and Rees 1980 , the reionisation of the pregalactic medium Belotsky and Kirillov 2015 and some short-period gamma-ray bursts Cline et al. 1997 .
Non-evaporating PBHs – even if they do not provide the DM – have been invoked to explain lensing effects Hawkins 1993 , the heating of the stars in our Galactic disc Lacey and Ostriker 1985 , the origin of massive compact halo objects (MACHOs) Yokoyama 1997 , the seeds for the supermassive black holes in galactic nuclei Bean and Magueijo 2002 , the generation of large-scale structure through Poisson fluctuations Afshordi et al. 2003 , effects on the thermal and ionisation history of the Universe Ricotti et al. 2008 and the LIGO/Virgo gravitational wave events Bird et al. 2016 .
Here we only cite the original paper on each topic, even if this has been superseded by later work. 
 There are usually other possible explanations for these features, so there is still no definitive evidence for PBHs.
This should be born in mind throughout this review since – in discussing the possible consequences of PBHs – we will not repeatedly stress that they may not exist at all.
Nevertheless, studying each of these effects allows one to place interesting constraints on the number of PBHs of mass M M and this in turn places constraints on the cosmological models which would generate them.
We will be discussing all these constraints in this review.
For evaporating PBHs these are usually expressed as constraints on the fraction of the Universe collapsing into PBHs at their formation epoch, denoted as β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) .
Indeed, this must be less than 10 − 18 10^{-18} over the entire mass range 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 15 ​ g 10^{9}\text{--}10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} .
For non-evaporating ones they are most usefully expressed as constraints on the fraction of the DM in PBHs, denoted as f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) .
It was already clear a decade ago that there were only a few mass windows where PBHs could provide all the dark matter Barrau et al. 2004 : the asteroid range ( 10 16 ​ – ​ 10 17 ​ g 10^{16}\text{--}10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} ), the sublunar range ( 10 20 ​ – ​ 10 26 ​ g 10^{20}\text{--}10^{26}\,\mathrm{g} ) and the intermediate-mass range ( 10 ​ – ​ 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 10\text{--}10^{3}\,M_{\odot} ).
Since then, interest in PBHs as dark matter has increased, primarily because of the LIGO/Virgo events.
The lowest and highest mass windows have now narrowed and it is sometimes argued that they are excluded, while the middle mass window has shifted to 10 17 ​ – ​ 10 23 ​ g 10^{17}\text{--}10^{23}\,\mathrm{g} , with the both mass limits having decreased.
However, we stress that most of the limits assume that PBH mass spectrum is quasi-monochromatic (i.e. having a width Δ ​ M ≲ M \Delta M\lesssim M ) and it could well be extended Carr et al. 2016a .
As discussed later, whether this makes it easier or more difficult for PBHs to provide the DM in the various mass windows has been the subject of some dispute. 
 In this review, we discuss all the PBH constraints, summarizing our results as excluded regions in the β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) and f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) planes.
Similar diagrams have been produced by numerous authors – indeed almost every paper on PBHs now includes such a diagram.
However, we hope this review will be more comprehensive and up-to-date than previous ones.
We should stress at the outset that the limits are constantly changing as a result of both observational and theoretical developments, so our claim for comprehensiveness may be short-lived.
We will also discuss some limits which are no longer believed because this is historically illuminating.
Even wrong calculations are worth recording because this may avoid their being repeated in the future.
In fact, all of the limits come with caveats and few can be regarded as 100 % secure.
(For example, the evaporation constraints assume the validity of Hawking radiation, even though there is still no direct observational evidence for this.)
While we emphasize which limits have gone away or are questionable, we are not always able to specify the confidence levels precisely.
Since our primary purpose is to discuss the constraints, there will be little attempt to describe the more positive aspects of PBHs (i.e. the ways in which they can probe the early Universe or explain various cosmological conundra Carr et al. 2021a ).
However, such positive aspects are covered in another recent PBH review  Carr and Kühnel 2020 and this complements the present work. 
 I.2 PBH formation 
 Black holes with a wide range of masses could have formed in the early Universe as a result of the great compression associated with the big bang Hawking 1971 ; Carr and Hawking 1974 .
A comparison of the cosmological density at a time t t after the big bang with the density associated with a black hole of mass M M suggests that such PBHs would have a mass of order 
 M ∼ c 3 ​ t G ∼ 10 15 ​ ( t 10 − 23 ​ s ) ​ g . M\sim\frac{c^{3}\,t}{G}\sim 10^{15}\,\left(\frac{t}{10^{-23}\,\mathrm{s}}\right)\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (1) 
 This roughly corresponds to the Hubble mass at time t t .
PBHs could thus span an enormous mass range:
those formed at the Planck time ( 10 − 43 ​ s 10^{-43}\,\mathrm{s} ) would have the Planck mass ( 10 − 5 ​ g 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{g} ), whereas those formed at 1 ​ s 1\,\mathrm{s} would be as large as 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}\,M_{\odot}  , comparable to the mass of the holes thought to reside in galactic nuclei.
By contrast, black holes forming at the present epoch could never be smaller than about 1 ​ M ⊙ 1\,M_{\odot}  .
The high density of the early Universe is a necessary but not sufficient condition for PBH formation.
One possibility is that they formed from large inhomogeneities – either fed into the initial conditions of the Universe or arising spontaneously in an initially smooth universe (e.g. through quantum effects during inflation).
Another possibility is that some sort of phase transition may have enhanced PBH formation from primordial inhomogeneities or triggered it even if there were none.
We now briefly discuss the various formation scenarios, although the references are incomplete and papers are cited without the authors being named. 
 I.2.1 Collapse from inhomogeneities during radiation-dominated era 
 Many PBH formation scenarios depend on the development of inhomogeneities of some kind.
Overdense regions could then stop expanding and recollapse Carr and Hawking 1974 .
Whatever the source of the fluctuations, they would need to be larger than the Jeans length at maximum expansion in order to collapse against the pressure and this is w ​ c ​ t \sqrt{w}\,ct for equation of state p = w ​ ρ ​ c 2 p=w\,\rho\,c^{2} .
A simple analytic argument then implies that the overdensity δ \delta when the region enters the horizon must exceed a critical value δ c ≈ w \delta_{\mathrm{c}}\approx w   Carr 1975 .
This gives 1 / 3 1/3 in the radiation era and early numerical calculations Nadezhin et al. 1978 ; Bicknell and Henriksen 1979 for overdense spherically symmetric regions surrounded by a compensating void roughly confirmed this prediction.
If the fluctuations on a given mass scale have Gaussian distribution, with a root-mean-square amplitude σ \sigma at the horizon epoch, then the collapse fraction for that scale should be roughly exp ⁡ [ − ( δ c / 2 ​ σ ) 2 ] \exp[-(\delta_{\mathrm{c}}/\sqrt{2}\sigma)^{2}]   Carr 1975 , which is expected to be tiny.
However, there is some ambiguity in how one defines fluctuations on scales larger than the horizon, especially when they are non-linear.
These issues were later addressed in Ref.  Shibata and Sasaki 1999 , this leading to estimates for δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} in the range 0.3 ​ – ​ 0.5 0.3\text{--}0.5   Green et al. 2004 .
A more precise analytical expression for δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} was later given in Ref.  Harada et al. 2013 and this corresponded to 0.4 0.4 in the radiation era.
However, this did not allow for pressure gradient effects and an improved calculation, allowing for the sharpness of the transition in the compensated model, gave a larger value  Harada et al. 2015 .
As discussed below, further insights came with the realisation that PBH formation is an application of critical phenomena  Niemeyer and Jedamzik 1998 ; Niemeyer and Jedamzik 1999 , with subsequent studies indicating that the value of δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} depends on the form of the density profile in the collapsing region  Musco et al. 2005 ; Musco et al. 2009 ; Polnarev and Musco 2007 .
Later numerical calculations  Nakama et al. 2014 identified the radial integral of the curvature profile in the central region and the transition scale as the most important features of the density profile and indicated a value for δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} in the range 0.37 ​ – ​ 0.43 0.37\text{--}0.43 .
Subsequent work considered the relationship between the threshold and curvature profile more precisely and obtained 0.4 ​ – ​ 2 / 3 0.4\text{--}2/3   Musco 2019 , with an analytic expression being obtained in Ref.  Escrivà et al. 2020 .
In the context of inflationary scenarios, it was also shown that the collapse fraction depends on the shape of the peak in the power spectrum Germani and Musco 2019 .
Recent work  Musco et al. 2021 uses numerical simulations to derive a simple expression for δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} which accounts for the non-linear relation between the curvature perturbation and the density contrast and for non-linear effects arising at horizon crossing.
The value of δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} could also be affected by non-Gaussianity, this slightly changing the relationship between the curvature perturbation and the overdensity  Saito et al. 2008 ; Kehagias et al. 2019 . 
 I.2.2 Critical collapse 
 When the density perturbation approaches the threshold value δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} required for PBH formation, a critical phenomenon occurs in which the black hole mass scales as ( δ − δ c ) γ (\delta-\delta_{\mathrm{c}})^{\gamma} and therefore extends down to arbitrarily small scales  Niemeyer and Jedamzik 1998 ; Niemeyer and Jedamzik 1999 ; Musco et al. 2005 ; Musco et al. 2009 .
Here the exponent γ \gamma is independent of the density profile and just depends on the equation of state ( 0.35 0.35 in the radiation case).
Most of the density is in PBHs with the horizon mass but there is also a power-law tail at lower masses Yokoyama 1998a ; Green and Liddle 1999 ; Kribs et al. 1999 .
The PBHs much smaller than the horizon are expected to have large spins  Harada et al. 2021 .
Because δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} is sensitive to the equation of state parameter w w , even a slight reduction in this can enhance PBH production.
For example, this may happen at the Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) era Jedamzik 1997 ; Widerin and Schmid ; Jedamzik and Niemeyer 1999 and the critical collapse analysis then predicts the mass function very precisely Byrnes et al. 2018 . 
 I.2.3 Collapse from single-field inflation 
 The most natural source of the fluctuations would be quantum effects during inflation.
Although any PBHs formed before the end of inflation (i.e. with mass exceeding about 1 ​ g 1\,\mathrm{g} ) would be diluted exponentially, the inflationary fluctuations themselves could generate PBHs on scales larger than this.
In the simplest (single scalar field) scenario, the inflationary fluctuations would depend upon the form of the inflaton potential V ⁡ ( ϕ ) V(\phi) and is expected to have a power-law form.
Since the fluctuations are only 10 − 5 10^{-5} on the CMB scale, they would then need to be “blue” (i.e. increasing on smaller scales) for PBH formation, with most PBH production occurring shortly after reheating  Carr and Lidsey 1993 .
However, the observed fluctuations on the CMB scale are “red”, so one needs a more complicated scenario, with a running of the spectral index or some feature in the power spectrum at the PBH scale.
Since the horizon fluctuation scales as ( d ​ V / d ​ ϕ ) − 1 (dV/d\phi)^{-1} in slow-roll inflation, one expects a peak in the power spectrum at an inflection point of V ⁡ ( ϕ ) V(\phi) , so this was a feature of an early model of PBH production  Ivanov et al. 1994 .
Similar models were subsequently studied for a polynomial potential with Yokoyama 1998b or without García-Bellido and Ruiz Morales 2017 a logarithmic correction, a non-minimally coupled scalar field with a quartic potential  Ballesteros and Taoso 2018 and a quintic potential fine-tuned so that a local minimum is followed by a small maximum (so that the field is slowed down but can still exit this region)  Hertzberg and Yamada 2018 .
Ultra-slow roll inflation occurs in the limit of a flat potential  Kinney 2005 and curvature perturbations then grow rapidly on superhorizon scales, rather than remaining constant, so the standard expression for the power spectrum is not valid and a more precise numerical calculation is required  Germani and Prokopec 2017 .
Rapid growth of superhorizon fluctuation is not limited to the case of ultra-slow roll, as it also happens if the first and second Hubble slow-roll parameters satisfy a certain condition Saito et al. 2008 .
Whenever the predicted fluctuations are large, one expects quantum corrections to the standard analysis to be important, so this has led to a study of the link between quantum diffusion and PBH production  Pattison et al. 2017 ; Ezquiaga and García-Bellido 2018 ; Biagetti et al. 2018 .
Other single-field proposals are the running-mass model  Stewart 1997 , in which the power spectrum grows sufficiently for PBHs to form without violating constraints on cosmological scales  Kohri et al. 2008 , and the hill-top model  Alabidi and Kohri 2009 , in which inflation occurs as the field evolves away from a local maximum towards a minimum.
However, in both of these cases, an auxiliary mechanism is required to terminate inflation.
Reheating at the end of inflation, where the inflaton oscillates around its minimum and decays, may also generate PBHs  Green and Malik 2001 ; Bassett and Tsujikawa 2001 , with perturbations being enhanced by a resonant instability  Martin et al. 2020a . 
 I.2.4 Collapse from multi-field inflation 
 A different mechanism for PBH production arises in multi-field scenarios  Randall et al. 1996 ; García-Bellido et al. 1996 .
In hybrid inflation one of the fields, ϕ \phi , initially slow-rolls while the accelerated expansion is driven by a second field ψ \psi .
At a critical value of ϕ \phi , ψ \psi undergoes a waterfall transition to a global minimum and inflation ends, with quantum fluctuations at the transition being large.
For some parameter values, however, the transition can be mild, so that there is a second inflationary phase as ψ \psi evolves to the minimum of its potential.
In this case, isocurvature perturbations are generated during the waterfall transition, leading to a broad peak in the power spectrum  Kawasaki et al. 1998 ; Clesse and García-Bellido 2015 .
Perturbations on cosmological scales are generated during the first period of inflation, while those on PBH scales are generated during the second period.
Another model invokes a curvaton field, which is dynamically unimportant during inflation but generates curvature perturbations after the inflaton has decayed  Lyth and Wands 2002 .
This makes it easier to produce PBHs than in standard single field models.
The first model of PBH formation with such a field was proposed even before the term curvaton was introduced Yokoyama 1997 .
Other examples are the axion-like curvaton  Kawasaki et al. 2013 and the inflating curvaton  Kohri et al. 2013 . 
 I.2.5 Collapse from inhomogeneities during matter-dominated era 
 Whatever the source of the inhomogeneities, PBH formation would be enhanced if some phase transitions led to a sudden reduction in the pressure – for example, if the early Universe went through a dustlike phase at early times as a result of being dominated by non-relativistic particles for a period Khlopov and Polnarev 1980 ; Polnarev and Khlopov 1981 ; Polnarev and Khlopov 1982 or undergoing slow reheating after inflation Khlopov et al. 1985 ; Carr et al. 1994 ; Carr et al. 2018 ; Martin et al. 2020b ; Allahverdi et al. 2020 .
In such cases, the effect of pressure in stopping collapse is unimportant and the probability of PBH formation just depends upon the fraction of regions which are sufficiently spherical to undergo collapse.
If a perturbation is not sufficiently spherically symmetric, it will collapse to form a pancake or cigar.
The mass of PBHs formed in this case may be much smaller than the horizon mass.
For a given spectrum of primordial fluctuations, the mass range over which the PBHs form is determined by the period of the soft equation of state, extending from the horizon mass at the start of the matter-dominated period to the mass of the regions binding at the end of that period (this being much smaller than the horizon mass then).
The fraction of regions which collapse to PBHs is the product of the fractions of regions which satisfy the inhomogeneity and anisotropy criteria.
The first fraction can be derived from the condition that the fluctuation must collapse within its Schwarzschild radius before a caustic can form at its centre and was originally estimated as σ 3 / 2 \sigma^{3/2}   Polnarev and Khlopov 1981 .
However, taking into account the finite propagation speed of information leads to a larger estimate 3.7 ​ σ 3 / 2 3.7\,\sigma^{3/2}   Kokubu et al. 2018 .
The original expression for second fraction was 0.02 ​ σ 5 0.02\,\sigma^{5}   Khlopov and Polnarev 1980 but this was later revised to 0.056 ​ σ 5 0.056\,\sigma^{5}   Harada et al. 2016 .
The total collapse fraction is therefore 0.2 ​ σ 13 / 2 0.2\,\sigma^{13/2} and PBHs form more abundantly during matter-domination than radiation-domination if σ < 0.05 \sigma<0.05 .
Note that PBHs can form with large spins during matter-domination, with the exact value depending on σ \sigma and the duration of the matter-dominated period  Harada et al. 2017 . 
 I.2.6 Collapse of cosmic string loops 
 In the cosmic string scenario, one expects some strings to self-intersect and form cosmic loops.
A typical loop will be larger than its Schwarzschild radius by the factor ( G ​ μ ) − 1 (G\mu)^{-1} , where μ \mu is the string mass per unit length.
Observations of the CMB imply that G ​ μ G\mu must be less than about 10 − 7 10^{-7} Ade et al. 2014 , while a comparison of the gravitational wave background expected from loop oscillations with pulsar timing array (PTA) data gives a limit of 10 − 11 10^{-11}   Blanco-Pillado et al. 2018 .
However, as discussed by many authors Hawking 1989 ; Polnarev and Zembowicz 1991 ; Hansen et al. 2000 ; Hogan 1984 ; Nagasawa 2005 ; Honma and Minakata 1991 ; James-Turner et al. 2020 , there is still a small probability that a cosmic loop will get into a configuration in which every dimension lies within its Schwarzschild radius, numerical simulations  Caldwell and Allen 1992 indicating a collapse fraction 10 4.9 ​ ( G ​ μ ) 4.1 10^{4.9}\,(G\mu)^{4.1} .
Since the PBHs form with equal probability at every epoch, they should have an extended mass spectrum  MacGibbon et al. 1998 .
It has recently been argued that PBHs would form more abundantly from the collapse of cosmic string cusps  Jenkins and Sakellariadou .
Critical phenomena may arise in loop collapse, with the PBH mass scaling as a power of the difference between the loop and Schwarzschild radius  Helfer et al. 2019 .
PBHs might also form through the collapse of string necklaces Matsuda 2006 ; Lake et al. 2009 . 
 I.2.7 Collapse from bubble collisions 
 Bubbles of broken symmetry might arise at any spontaneously broken symmetry epoch and many people have suggested that PBHs could form as a result of bubble collisions Crawford and Schramm 1982 ; Hawking et al. 1982 ; La and Steinhardt 1989 ; Moss 1994 ; Konoplich et al. 1998 ; Konoplich et al. 1999 .
The production of PBHs from bubble collisions associated with a first-order phase transition at the end of the old inflation model Sato 1981 ; Guth 1981 has also been studied Kodama et al. 1982 .
However, this happens only if the bubble formation rate per Hubble volume is finely tuned:
if it is much larger than the Hubble rate, the entire Universe undergoes the phase transition immediately and there is not time to form black holes;
if it is much less than the Hubble rate, the bubbles are very rare and never collide.
The holes should have a mass of order the horizon mass at the phase transition, so PBHs forming at the GUT (Grand Unified Theory) epoch would have a mass of 10 3 ​ g 10^{3}\,\mathrm{g} , those forming at the electroweak unification epoch would have a mass of 10 28 ​ g 10^{28}\,\mathrm{g} , and those forming at the QCD phase transition would have mass of around 1 ​ M ⊙ 1\,M_{\odot}  .
The production of PBHs through the nucleation of false vacuum bubbles during inflation has recently been reconsidered in Ref.  Kusenko et al. 2020 . 
 I.2.8 Collapse of scalar field 
 A scalar condensate can form in the early Universe and collapse into Q-balls before decaying Cotner and Kusenko 2017a .
If the Q-balls dominate the energy density for some period, the statistical fluctuations in their number density can lead to PBH formation Cotner and Kusenko 2017b .
For a general charged scalar field, this can generate PBHs over the mass range allowed by observational constraints and with sufficient abundance to account for the dark matter and the LIGO observations.
If the scalar field is associated with supersymmetry, the mass range must be below 10 23 ​ g 10^{23}\,\mathrm{g} .
The fragmentation of the inflaton into oscillons might also lead to PBH production in the sublunar range Cotner et al. 2018 ; Cotner et al. 2019 . 
 I.2.9 Collapse of domain walls 
 The collapse of sufficiently large closed domain walls produced at a 2nd order phase transition in the vacuum state of a scalar field, such as might be associated with inflation, could lead to PBH formation Rubin et al. 2000 ; Rubin et al. 2001 ; Dokuchaev et al. 2005 .
These PBHs would have a small mass for a thermal phase transition but they could be much larger if one invoked a non-equilibrium scenario.
Indeed, they could then span a wide range of masses, with a fractal structure of smaller PBHs clustered around larger ones Khlopov et al. 2000 .
PBHs in the sublunar range could form through the collapse of closed domain walls in QCD axion models, together with a string-wall network  Ge 2020 .
It has been argued that bubbles formed during inflation would (depending on their size) form either black holes or baby universes connected to our Universe by wormholes Garriga et al. 2016 ; Deng et al. 2017 .
In this case, the PBH mass function would be very broad and extend to very high masses Deng and Vilenkin 2017 ; Liu et al. 2020 . 
 In some of these scenarios, the PBH mass spectrum is expected to be narrow and centred around the mass given by Eq. ( 1 ) with t t corresponding to the time at which the PBH scale re-enters the horizon in the inflationary model or to the time of the relevant cosmological phase transition otherwise.
However, there are some circumstances in which the spectrum would be extended and the constraint on one mass-scale would then imply a constraint on neighbouring scales.
For example, PBHs may be much smaller than the horizon if they form as a result of critical phenomena or during a matter-dominated phase and their spectrum could then extend well below the horizon mass.
It used to be claimed that a PBH could not be much larger than the value given by Eq. ( 1 ) at formation else it would be a separate closed universe rather than part of our Universe Carr and Hawking 1974 .
This interpretation is misleading because the PBH mass necessarily goes to zero when the size of the overdense region reaches a certain limit  Kopp et al. 2011 but there is still a maximum mass for a PBH forming at a given epoch and this is of order the horizon mass  Harada and Carr 2005 . 
 I.3 Mass and density of PBHs 
 In the following discussion, we assume that the standard Lambda Cold Dark Matter ( Λ \Lambda CDM) model applies, with the age of the Universe being t 0 = 13.8 ​ Gyr t_{0}=13.8\,\mathrm{Gyr} and the Hubble parameter being H 0 ≡ 100 ​ h ​ km ​ s − 1 ​ Mpc − 1 H_{0}\equiv 100\,h\,\mathrm{km}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1} with h = 0.67 h=0.67 Akrami et al. 2020 .
We also put c = ℏ = k B = 1 c=\hbar=k_{\mathrm{B}}=1 .
The Friedmann equation implies that the density ρ \rho and temperature T T during the radiation era are given by 
 H 2 = 8 ​ π ​ G 3 ​ ρ = 4 ​ π 3 ​ G 45 ​ g ∗ ​ T 4 , H^{2}=\frac{8\pi\,G}{3}\,\rho=\frac{4\pi^{3}\,G}{45}\,g_{*}\,T^{4}\,, 
 (2) 
 where g ∗ g_{*} counts the number of relativistic degrees of freedom.
This can be integrated to give 
 t ≈ 0.738 ( g ∗ 10.75 ) − 1 / 2 ( T 1 ​ MeV ) − 2 s , t\approx 0.738\,\left(\frac{g_{*}}{10.75}\right)^{-1/2}\,\left(\frac{T}{1\,\mathrm{MeV}}\right)^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}\,, 
 (3) 
 where g ∗ g_{*} and T T are normalised to their values at the start of the BBN epoch.
For PBHs which form during the radiation era (the ones generated before inflation being diluted to negligible density), the initial PBH mass M M is close to the Hubble horizon mass M H M_{\mathrm{H}} and therefore given by 
 M = γ ​ M H = 4 ​ π 3 ​ γ ​ ρ ​ R H − 3 = γ ​ c 3 ​ t G ≈ 2.03 × 10 5 ​ γ ​ ( t 1 ​ s ) ​ M ⊙ . M=\gamma\,M_{\mathrm{H}}=\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\gamma\,\rho\,R_{\mathrm{H}}^{-3}=\frac{\gamma c^{3}t}{G}\approx 2.03\times 10^{5}\,\gamma\,\left(\frac{t}{1\,\mathrm{s}}\right)\,M_{\odot}\,. 
 (4) 
 Here γ \gamma is a numerical factor (somewhat below 1 1 ) which depends on the details of gravitational collapse and the penultimate expression applies exactly in a radiation Universe (for which R H = 2 ​ c ​ t R_{\mathrm{H}}=2ct ).
However, Eq. ( 4 ) does not apply for PBHs which form in an early matter-dominated (dust-like) era or for the low mass PBHs forming through critical collapse.
Note that the normalisation in Eq. ( 4 ) is sometimes taken to be the particle horizon mass; that is inappropriate if there is an inflationary phase, although the two masses are the same in a non-inflationary radiation-dominated model.
For much of the following discussion, we will assume that the PBHs all have the same mass or at least a mass width Δ ​ M \Delta M no larger than M M .
This simplifies the analysis considerably and suffices providing we only require limits on the PBH abundance at particular values of M M .
If the PBHs have an extended mass function, the analysis of the constraints is more complicated and we discuss this situation later. 
 Assuming adiabatic cosmic expansion after PBH formation, the ratio of the PBH number density to the entropy density, n PBH / s n_{\mathrm{PBH}}/s , is conserved.
Using the relation ρ = 3 ​ s ​ T / 4 \rho=3sT/4 , the fraction of the Universe’s mass in PBHs at their formation time is then related to their number density n PBH ​ ( t ) n_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t) during the radiation era by 
 β ( M ) ≡ ρ PBH ​ ( t i ) ρ ⁡ ( t i ) = 4 ​ M 3 ​ T i n PBH ​ ( t ) s ⁡ ( t ) ≈ 7.99 × 10 − 29 γ − 1 / 2 ( g ∗ i 106.75 ) 1 / 4 ( M M ⊙ ) 3 / 2 ( n PBH ​ ( t 0 ) 1 ​ Gpc − 3 ) , \beta(M)\equiv\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t_{\mathrm{i}})}{\rho(t_{\mathrm{i}})}=\frac{4M}{3T_{\mathrm{i}}}\,\frac{n_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t)}{s(t)}\\
\approx 7.99\times 10^{-29}\,\gamma^{-1/2}\,\left(\frac{g_{*\mathrm{i}}}{106.75}\right)^{1/4}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{3/2}\,\left(\frac{n_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t_{0})}{1\,\mathrm{Gpc}^{-3}}\right)\,, 
 (5) 
 where the subscript “ i \mathrm{i} ” indicates values at the epoch of PBH formation and we have assumed s = 8.54 × 10 85 ​ Gpc − 3 s=8.54\times 10^{85}\,\mathrm{Gpc}^{-3} today.
 g ∗ i g_{*\mathrm{i}} is now normalised to the value of g ∗ g_{*} at around 10 − 5 ​ s 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{s} since it does not increase much before that in the Standard Model and most PBHs are likely to form before then.
We can also express this as 
 β ( M ) ≈ 7.06 × 10 − 18 γ − 1 / 2 ( h 0.67 ) 2 ( g ∗ i 106.75 ) 1 / 4 Ω PBH ( M ) ( M 10 15 ​ g ) 1 / 2 , \beta(M)\approx 7.06\times 10^{-18}\,\gamma^{-1/2}\,\left(\frac{h}{0.67}\right)^{2}\,\left(\frac{g_{*\mathrm{i}}}{106.75}\right)^{1/4}\,\Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M)\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{15}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{1/2}\,, 
 (6) 
 where Ω PBH ≡ ρ PBH ​ ( t 0 ) / ρ crit \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t_{0})/\rho_{\mathrm{crit}} is the current density parameter of the PBHs.
A less precise form of this equation can be obtained from the relation 
 Ω PBH = β Ω CMB ( 1 + z ) ∼ 10 6 β ( t s ) − 1 / 2 ∼ 10 9 β ( M M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 , \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}=\beta\,\Omega_{\mathrm{CMB}}\,(1+z)\sim 10^{6}\,\beta\,\left(\frac{t}{\mathrm{s}}\right)^{-1/2}\sim 10^{9}\,\beta\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1/2}\,, 
 (7) 
 where Ω CMB ≈ 5 × 10 − 5 \Omega_{\mathrm{CMB}}\approx 5\times 10^{-5} is the density parameter of the CMB and we have used Eq. ( 1 ).
The ( 1 + z ) (1+z) factor can be understood as arising because the radiation density scales as ( 1 + z ) 4 (1+z)^{4} , whereas the PBH density scales as ( 1 + z ) 3 (1+z)^{3} . 
 Since β \beta always appears in combination with γ 1 / 2 g ∗ i − 1 / 4 h − 2 \gamma^{1/2}\,g_{*\mathrm{i}}^{-1/4}\,h^{-2} , it is convenient to define a new parameter 
 β ′ ( M ) ≡ γ 1 / 2 ( g ∗ i 106.75 ) − 1 / 4 ( h 0.67 ) − 2 β ( M ) , \beta^{\prime}(M)\equiv\gamma^{1/2}\,\left(\frac{g_{*\mathrm{i}}}{106.75}\right)^{-1/4}\,\left(\frac{h}{0.67}\right)^{-2}\,\beta(M)\,, 
 (8) 
 where g ∗ i g_{*\mathrm{i}} and h h can be specified very precisely but γ \gamma is rather uncertain.
Note that the relationship between β \beta and Ω PBH \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}} must be modified if the Universe ever deviates from the standard radiation-dominated behaviour – for example, if there is a dust-like stage, a second inflationary phase, a change of dimensionality Sendouda et al. 2006 ; Johnson 2020 or a variation in the gravitational constant at early times Barrow 1992 ; Barrow and Carr 1996 ; Harada et al. 2002 . 
 Any limit on Ω PBH \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}} places a constraint on β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) .
For non-evaporating PBHs with M > 10 15 ​ g M>10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} , one constraint comes from requiring that Ω PBH \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}} be less than the CDM density, Ω CDM = 0.264 ± 0.006 \Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}=0.264\pm 0.006 with h = 0.67 h=0.67 Akrami et al. 2020 , so the 3 ​ σ 3\sigma upper limit is Ω PBH < Ω CDM < 0.282 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}<\Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}<0.282 .
Much stronger constraints are associated with PBHs smaller than 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} since these would have evaporated by now.
For example, the γ \gamma -ray background limit implies β ⁡ ( 10 15 ​ g ) ≲ 10 − 29 \beta(10^{15}\,\mathrm{g})\lesssim 10^{-29} and this is the strongest constraint on β \beta over all mass ranges.
Other ones are associated with the generation of entropy, modifications to the cosmological production of light elements and the CMB anisotropies.
There are also constraints below 10 6 ​ g 10^{6}\,\mathrm{g} based on the (uncertain) assumption that evaporating PBHs leave stable Planck mass relics, an issue which is discussed later. 
 The constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) were first brought together by Novikov et al. Novikov et al. 1979 40 years ago.
Besides the entropy, BBN, γ \gamma -ray background and density limit, they included a strong constraint above 10 15 ​ M ⊙ 10^{15}M_{\odot} associated with the upper limit on the CMB dipole anisotropy.
An updated version of this diagram appeared in Ref.  Carr et al. 1994 about 15 years later but was essentially the same, apart from the omission of the dipole constraint (which was outside the considered mass range) and the addition of a ‘relics’ constraint.
Because of their historical interest, both diagrams are shown in Fig.  1 , although the later one is slightly adapted.
Subsequently, this diagram has frequently been revised as the relevant effects have been studied in greater detail.
For example, Josan et al. Josan et al. 2009 produced a comprehensive version a decade ago and we produced a version at about the same time in Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a (henceforth CKSY), covering both the evaporating and non-evaporating PBHs.
However, there have been many further developments since then, on both the observational and theoretical fronts.
An updated review of the constraints was given in Ref.  Carr et al. 2016a several years ago and the present discussion might be regarded as another (albeit more comprehensive) update.
The important qualitative point of all such diagrams is that the value of β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) must be tiny over every mass range, even if the PBH density is large today, so any cosmological model which would entail more than a tiny fraction of the Universe going into PBHs is immediately excluded. 
 Figure 1: Constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) from Ref.  Novikov et al. 1979 in 1979 (left) and Ref.  Carr et al. 1994 in 1994 (right). 
 I.4 Evaporation of PBHs 
 The realisation that PBHs might be small prompted Hawking to study their quantum properties.
This led to his famous discovery Hawking 1975 that black holes radiate thermally with a temperature 
 T BH = ℏ ​ c 3 8 ​ π ​ G ​ M ​ k B ∼ 10 − 7 ​ ( M M ⊙ ) − 1 ​ K , T_{\mathrm{BH}}=\frac{\hbar\,c^{3}}{8\pi\,G\,M\,k_{\mathrm{B}}}\sim 10^{-7}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1}\,\mathrm{K}\,, 
 (9) 
 so they evaporate completely on a timescale 
 τ ⁡ ( M ) ∼ G 2 ​ M 3 ℏ ​ c 4 ∼ 10 64 ​ ( M M ⊙ ) 3 ​ yr . \tau(M)\sim\frac{G^{2}\,M^{3}}{\hbar\,c^{4}}\sim 10^{64}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{3}\,\mathrm{yr}\,. 
 (10) 
 More precise expressions are given below but this implies that only PBHs smaller than about 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} would have evaporated by the present epoch, so Eq. ( 1 ) implies that this effect could be important only for ones which formed before 10 − 23 ​ s 10^{-23}\,\mathrm{s} .
Since PBHs with a mass of around 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} would be producing photons with energy of order 100 ​ MeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV} at the present epoch, the observational limit on the γ \gamma -ray background intensity at 100 ​ MeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV} immediately implies that their density could not exceed about 10 − 8 10^{-8} times the critical density Page and Hawking 1976 .
This suggests that there is little chance of detecting their final explosive phase at the present epoch, at least in the Standard Model of particle physics.
Nevertheless, the γ \gamma -ray background limit does not preclude PBHs having important cosmological effects Carr 1976 . 
 I.4.1 Mass loss and evaporation timescale 
 From Eq. ( 9 ) the temperature of a black hole with mass M ≡ M 10 × 10 10 ​ g M\equiv M_{10}\times 10^{10}\,\mathrm{g} is 
 T BH ≈ 1.06 ​ M 10 − 1 ​ TeV . T_{\mathrm{BH}}\approx 1.06\,M_{10}^{-1}\,\mathrm{TeV}\,. 
 (11) 
 This assumes that the hole has no charge or angular momentum, since charge and angular momentum are usually assumed to be lost through quantum emission on a shorter timescale than the mass, although this may fail at the Planck scale  Lehmann et al. 2019 .
The emission is not exactly black-body but depends upon the spin and charge of the emitted particle, the average energy for neutrinos, electrons and photons being 4.22 ​ T BH 4.22\,T_{\mathrm{BH}}  , 4.18 ​ T BH 4.18\,T_{\mathrm{BH}} and 5.71 ​ T BH 5.71\,T_{\mathrm{BH}}  , respectively Page 1976 . 
 The mass loss rate of an evaporating black hole can be expressed as 
 d ​ M 10 d ​ t = − 5.34 × 10 − 5 f ( M ) M 10 − 2 s − 1 . \frac{\mathrm{d}M_{10}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-5.34\times 10^{-5}\,f(M)\,M_{10}^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,. 
 (12) 
 Here f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) is a measure of the number of emitted particle species, normalised to unity for a black hole with M ≫ 10 17 ​ g M\gg 10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} , this emitting only particles which are (effectively) massless: photons, three generations of neutrinos and antineutrinos, and gravitons.
The contribution of each relativistic degree of freedom to f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) is MacGibbon 1991 
 f s = 0 = 0.267 , f s = 1 = 0.060 , f s = 3 / 2 = 0.020 , f s = 2 = 0.007 , \displaystyle f_{s=0}=0.267\,,\quad f_{s=1}=0.060\,,\quad f_{s=3/2}=0.020\,,\quad f_{s=2}=0.007\,, 
 (13) 
 f s = 1 / 2 = 0.147 (neutral) , f s = 1 / 2 = 0.142 (charge  ± e ) . \displaystyle f_{s=1/2}=0.147~\text{(neutral)}\,,\quad f_{s=1/2}=0.142~\text{(charge $\pm e$)}\,. 
 Holes in the mass range 10 15 ​ g < M < 10 17 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} emit electrons but not muons, while those in the range 10 14 ​ g < M < 10 15 ​ g 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} also emit muons, which subsequently decay into electrons and neutrinos.
The latter range is relevant for the PBHs which are completing their evaporation at the present epoch. 
 Once M M falls to around 10 14 ​ g 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} , a black hole can also begin to emit hadrons.
However, hadrons are composite particles made up of quarks held together by gluons.
For temperatures exceeding the QCD confinement scale, Λ QCD = 250 ​ – ​ 300 ​ MeV \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}=250\text{--}300\,\mathrm{MeV} , one would expect these fundamental particles to be emitted rather than composite particles.
Only pions would be light enough to be emitted below Λ QCD \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}  .
Above this temperature, the particles radiated can be regarded as asymptotically free, leading to the emission of quarks and gluons MacGibbon and Webber 1990 .
Since there are 12 12 quark degrees of freedom per flavour and 16 16 gluon degrees of freedom, one would expect the emission rate (i.e., the value of f f ) to increase suddenly once the QCD temperature is reached.
If one includes just u u , d d and s s quarks and gluons, Eq. ( 13 ) implies that their contribution to f f is 3 × 12 × 0.14 + 16 × 0.06 ≈ 6 3\times 12\times 0.14+16\times 0.06\approx 6 , compared to the pre-QCD value of about 2 2 .
Thus the value of f f roughly quadruples, although there will be a further increase in f f at somewhat higher temperatures due to the emission of the heavier quarks.
After their emission, quarks and gluons fragment into further quarks and gluons until they cluster into the observable hadrons when they have travelled a distance Λ QCD − 1 ∼ 10 − 13 ​ cm \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}^{-1}\sim 10^{-13}\,\mathrm{cm} .
This is much larger than the size of the hole, so gravitational effects can be neglected. 
 If we sum up the contributions from all the particles in the Standard Model up to 1 ​ TeV 1\,\mathrm{TeV} , corresponding to M 10 ∼ 1 M_{10}\sim 1 , this gives f ⁡ ( M ) = 15.35 f(M)=15.35 .
Integrating the mass loss rate over time then gives a lifetime 
 τ ≈ 407 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) 15.35 ) − 1 ​ M 10 3 ​ s . \tau\approx 407\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{15.35}\right)^{-1}\,M_{10}^{3}\,\mathrm{s}\,. 
 (14) 
 The mass of a PBH evaporating at time τ \tau after the big bang is then 
 M ≈ 1.35 × 10 9 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) 15.35 ) 1 / 3 ​ ( τ 1 ​ s ) 1 / 3 ​ g . M\approx 1.35\times 10^{9}\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{15.35}\right)^{1/3}\,\left(\frac{\tau}{1\,\mathrm{s}}\right)^{1/3}\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (15) 
 The critical mass for which τ \tau equals the age of the Universe is denoted by M ∗ M_{*}  .
For the currently favoured age of 13.8 ​ Gyr 13.8\,\mathrm{Gyr} , one finds 
 M ∗ ≈ 1.02 × 10 15 ​ ( f ∗ 15.35 ) 1 / 3 ​ g ≈ 5.1 × 10 14 ​ g , M_{*}\approx 1.02\times 10^{15}\,\left(\frac{f_{*}}{15.35}\right)^{1/3}\,\mathrm{g}\approx 5.1\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}\,, 
 (16) 
 where the last step assumes f ∗ = 1.9 f_{*}=1.9 , the value associated with the temperature T BH ​ ( M ∗ ) = 21 ​ MeV T_{\mathrm{BH}}(M_{*})=21\,\mathrm{MeV} .
At this temperature muons and some pions are emitted, so the value of f ∗ f_{*} accounts for this.
Although QCD effects are initially small for PBHs with M = M ∗ M=M_{*} , only contributing a few percent, they become important once M M falls to 
 M q ≈ 0.4 ​ M ∗ ≈ 2 × 10 14 ​ g , M_{\mathrm{q}}\approx 0.4\,M_{*}\approx 2\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}\,, 
 (17) 
 since the peak energy becomes comparable to Λ QCD \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}} then.
This means that an appreciable fraction of the time-integrated emission from the PBHs evaporating at the present epoch goes into quark and gluon jet products. 
 It should be stressed that the above analysis is not exact because the value of f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) in Eq. ( 15 ) should really be the weighted average of f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) over the lifetime of the black hole.
The more precise calculations of MacGibbon MacGibbon and Webber 1990 ; MacGibbon 1991 ; MacGibbon et al. 2008 give the slightly smaller value M ∗ = 5.00 × 10 14 ​ g M_{*}=5.00\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} .
However, the weighted average is well approximated by f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) unless one is close to a particle mass threshold.
For example, since the lifetime of a black hole of mass 0.4 ​ M ∗ 0.4\,M_{*} is roughly 0.25 × ( 0.4 ) 3 = 0.016 0.25\times(0.4)^{3}=0.016 times that of an M ∗ M_{*} black hole, one expects the value of M ∗ M_{*} to be overestimated by a few percent.
This explains the small difference from MacGibbon’s calculation. 
 I.4.2 Primary and secondary emission 
 Particles injected from PBHs have two components: the primary component, which is the direct Hawking emission, and the secondary component, which comes from the decay of gauge bosons or heavy leptons and the hadrons produced by fragmentation of primary quarks and gluons.
For example, the photon spectrum can be written as 
 d ​ N ˙ γ d ​ E γ ​ ( E γ , M ) = d ​ N ˙ γ pri d ​ E γ ​ ( E γ , M ) + d ​ N ˙ γ sec d ​ E γ ​ ( E γ , M ) , \frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(E_{\gamma},M)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}^{\mathrm{pri}}_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(E_{\gamma},M)+\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(E_{\gamma},M)\,, 
 (18) 
 where an overdot denotes a time derivative, with similar expressions for other particles.
In order to treat QCD fragmentation, CKSY use the PYTHIA code (version 6), a Monte Carlo event generator constructed to fit hadron fragmentation for centre-of-mass energies s ≲ 200 ​ GeV \sqrt{s}\lesssim 200\,\mathrm{GeV} .
The pioneering work of MacGibbon and Webber used the Herwig code but obtained similar results MacGibbon and Webber 1990 . 
 The spectrum of secondary photons is peaked around E γ ≃ m π 0 / 2 ≈ 68 ​ MeV E_{\gamma}\simeq m_{\pi^{0}}/2\approx 68\,\mathrm{MeV} , because it is dominated by the 2 ​ γ 2\gamma -decay of soft neutral pions which are practically at rest.
The peak flux can be expressed as 
 d ​ N ˙ γ sec d ​ E γ ​ ( E γ = m π 0 / 2 ) ≃ 2 ​ ∑ i = q , g ℬ i → π 0 ​ ( E ¯ , E π 0 ) ​ E ¯ m π 0 ​ d ​ N ˙ i pri d ​ E i ​ ( E i ≃ E ¯ ) , \frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{sec}}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(E_{\gamma}=m_{\pi^{0}}/2)\simeq 2\,\sum_{i=q,g}\mathcal{B}_{i\to\pi^{0}}(\overline{E},E_{\pi^{0}})\,\frac{\overline{E}}{m_{\pi^{0}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{i}^{\mathrm{pri}}}{\mathrm{d}E_{i}}(E_{i}\simeq\overline{E})\,, 
 (19) 
 where ℬ q , g → π 0 ​ ( E jet , E π 0 ) \mathcal{B}_{q,g\to\pi^{0}}(E_{\mathrm{jet}},E_{\pi^{0}}) is the fraction of the jet energy E jet E_{\mathrm{jet}} going into neutral pions of energy E π 0 E_{\pi^{0}}  .
This is of order 0.1 0.1 and fairly independent of jet energy.
If we assume that most of the primary particles have the average energy E ¯ ≈ 4.4 ​ T BH \overline{E}\approx 4.4\,T_{\mathrm{BH}}  , the last factor becomes d ​ N ˙ i pri / d ​ E i ≈ 1.6 × 10 − 3 ​ ℏ − 1 \mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{i}^{\mathrm{pri}}/\mathrm{d}E_{i}\approx 1.6\times 10^{-3}\,\hbar^{-1} .
Thus the energy dependence of Eq. ( 19 ) comes entirely from the factor E ¯ \overline{E} and is proportional to the Hawking temperature.
The emission rates of primary and secondary photons for four typical temperatures are shown in Fig.  2 . 
 Figure 2: Instantaneous emission rate of photons for four typical black hole temperatures, from Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a .
For each temperature, the curve with the right (left) peak represents the primary (secondary) component and the thick curve denotes their sum. 
 It should be noted that the time-integrated ratio of the secondary flux to the primary flux increases rapidly once M M goes below M ∗ M_{*}  .
This is because a black hole with M = M ∗ M=M_{*} will emit quarks efficiently once its mass gets down to the value M q M_{\mathrm{q}} given by Eq. ( 17 ) and this corresponds to an appreciable fraction of its original mass.
On the other hand, a PBH with somewhat larger initial mass, M = ( 1 + μ ) ​ M ∗ M=(1+\mu)\,M_{*}  , will today have a mass Carr et al. 2010a 
 m ≡ M ⁡ ( t 0 ) ≈ ( 3 ​ μ ) 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ( μ ≪ 1 ) . m\equiv M(t_{0})\approx(3\,\mu)^{1/3}M_{*}\quad(\mu\ll 1)\,. 
 (20) 
 Here we have assumed f ⁡ ( M ) ≈ f ∗ f(M)\approx f_{*}  , which should be a good approximation for m > M q m>M_{\mathrm{q}} since the value of f f only changes slowly above the QCD threshold.
However, m m falls below M q M_{\mathrm{q}} for μ < 0.02 \mu<0.02 and if we assume that f f jumps discontinuously from f ∗ f_{*} to α ​ f ∗ \alpha\,f_{*} at this mass, then Eq. ( 20 ) must be reduced by a factor α 1 / 3 \alpha^{1/3} .
The fact that this happens only for μ < 0.02 \mu<0.02 means that the fraction of the black hole mass going into secondaries falls off sharply above M ∗ M_{*}  .
The ratio of the energies at which the spectra peak is 
 E ¯ S / E ¯ P ≈ ( 68 ​ MeV ) / ( 600 ​ m 14 − 1 ​ MeV ) ≈ 0.6 ​ ( m / M ∗ ) , \bar{E}^{\mathrm{S}}/\bar{E}^{\mathrm{P}}\approx(68\,\mathrm{MeV})/(600\,m_{14}^{-1}\,\mathrm{MeV})\approx 0.6\,(m/M_{*})\,, 
 (21) 
 while the flux ratio is 
 ( d ​ N ˙ S d ​ E ) E ¯ S / ( d ​ N ˙ P d ​ E ) E ¯ P ≈ 1.4 ( m M ∗ ) − 1 e − χ m / M q \biggl.\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}^{\mathrm{S}}}{\mathrm{d}E}\biggr)_{\bar{E}^{\mathrm{S}}}\biggr/\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}^{\mathrm{P}}}{\mathrm{d}E}\biggr)_{\bar{E}^{\mathrm{P}}}\approx 1.4\,\left(\frac{m}{M_{*}}\right)^{-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\chi\,m/M_{\mathrm{q}}} 
 (22) 
 for some constant χ \chi .
The time-integrated ratios of the peak energies and fluxes are shown in Fig.  3 .
These relations are important when calculating the effects of PBHs evaporating at the present epoch (e.g. the γ \gamma -ray background generated by PBHs in the Galactic halo). 
 Figure 3: Ratios of secondary to primary peak energies (solid) and fluxes (dotted), from Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a . 
 II Constraints on evaporating PBHs 
 In this section we discuss the constraints on evaporating PBHs.
The results are summarised in Fig.  4 , which is an update of Fig. 6 of Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a .
The important point is that the BBN, CMB and extragalactic γ \gamma -ray background (EGB) limits are the most stringent ones over almost the entire mass range 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 17 ​ g 10^{9}\text{--}10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} .
There is just a small range between 10 14.7 ​ g 10^{14.7}\,\mathrm{g} and 10 16.1 ​ g 10^{16.1}\,\mathrm{g} where the Galactic γ \gamma -ray background (GGB) and Galactic electron and positron limits dominate.
Obviously none of these constraints would apply if there were no Hawking radiation.
In this case, the only constraint would come from the condition Ω PBH < 1 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}<1 , as indicated by the broken line in Fig.  4 .
In this context, Raidal et al. have suggested that large primordial curvature fluctuations could collapse into horizonless exotic compact objects instead of PBHs Raidal et al. 2018 .
In this case, they either do not evaporate at all or they do so much more slowly. 
 Figure 4: Combined BBN (pink) and EGB (red) limits Carr et al. 2010a , compared to other constraints on evaporating PBHs from LSP relics Lemoine 2000 (dotted grey since dependent on LSP mass), the CMB spectral distortions Acharya and Khatri 2020a ; Chluba et al. 2020 and anisotropies Acharya and Khatri 2020a (yellow), extragalactic antiprotons Carr et al. 2010a and neutrinos Carr et al. 2010a ; Dasgupta et al. 2020 (green), the GGB Carr et al. 2016b and the electrons and positrons observed by Voyager 1 Boudaud and Cirelli 2019 (blue).
Less secure limits come from observations of primary photons  Laha et al. 2020 ; Coogan et al. 2021 , electron-positron annihilation  DeRocco and Graham 2019 ; Laha 2019 and radio emission from the GC  Chan and Lee 2020 (blue since Galactic) and Leo T  Kim (green since extragalactic).
The dashed line is the only constraint for no Hawking radiation. 
 II.1 Big bang nucleosynthesis 
 PBHs with M ∼ 10 10 ​ g M\sim 10^{10}\,\mathrm{g} and T BH ∼ 1 ​ TeV T_{\mathrm{BH}}\sim 1\,\mathrm{TeV} have a lifetime τ ∼ 10 3 ​ s \tau\sim 10^{3}\,\mathrm{s} and therefore evaporate at the epoch of cosmological nucleosynthesis.
The effect of these evaporations on BBN has been a subject of long-standing interest.
Injection of high-energy neutrinos and antineutrinos Vainer and Naselskii 1978 changes the weak interaction freeze-out time and hence the neutron-to-proton ratio at the onset of BBN, which changes He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} production.
Since PBHs with M = 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 13 ​ g M=10^{9}\text{--}10^{13}\,\mathrm{g} evaporated during or after BBN, the baryon-to-entropy ratio at nucleosynthesis would be increased, resulting in overproduction of He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} and underproduction of D \mathrm{D} Miyama and Sato 1978 .
Emission of high-energy nucleons and antinucleons Zel’dovich et al. 1977 increases the primordial deuterium abundance due to capture of free neutrons by protons and spallation of He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} .
The emission of photons by PBHs with M > 10 10 ​ g M>10^{10}\,\mathrm{g} dissociates the deuterons produced in nucleosynthesis Lindley 1980 ; Ellis et al. 1992 .
The limits associated with these effects are shown in Fig.  1 . 
 Observational data on both the light element abundances and the neutron lifetime have changed since these early papers.
Much more significant, however, have been developments in our understanding of the fragmentation of quark and gluon jets from PBHs into hadrons.
Most of the hadrons created decay almost instantaneously compared to the timescale of nucleosynthesis but long-lived ones (such as pions, kaons and nucleons) remain long enough in the ambient medium to leave an observable signature on BBN.
These effects were first discussed by Kohri and Yokoyama Kohri and Yokoyama 1999 for the relatively low mass PBHs evaporating in the early stages of BBN but the analysis was later extended to incorporate the effects of heavier PBHs evaporating after BBN Carr et al. 2010a , the hadrons and high energy photons from these PBHs further dissociating synthesised light elements. 
 II.1.1 Modern studies 
 High energy particles emitted by PBHs modify the standard BBN scenario in three different ways:
(1) high energy mesons and antinucleons induce extra interconversion between background protons and neutrons even after the weak interaction has frozen out in the background Universe;
(2) high energy hadrons dissociate light elements synthesised in BBN, thereby reducing He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} and increasing D \mathrm{D} , T \mathrm{T} , He 3 {}^{3}\mathrm{He} , Li 6 {}^{6}\mathrm{Li} and Li 7 {}^{7}\mathrm{Li} ;
(3) high energy photons generated in the cascade further dissociate He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} to increase the abundance of lighter elements even more. 
 The PBH constraints depend on three parameters: the initial baryon-to-photon ratio η i \eta_{\mathrm{i}}  , the PBH initial mass M M or (equivalently) its lifetime τ \tau , and the initial PBH number density normalised to the entropy density, Y PBH ≡ n PBH / s Y_{\mathrm{PBH}}\equiv n_{\mathrm{PBH}}/s .
From Eq. ( 5 ) this is related to the initial mass fraction β ′ \beta^{\prime} by 
 β ′ = 5.4 × 10 21 ​ ( τ 1 ​ s ) 1 / 2 ​ Y PBH . \beta^{\prime}=5.4\times 10^{21}\,\left(\frac{\tau}{1\,\mathrm{s}}\right)^{1/2}\,Y_{\mathrm{PBH}}\,. 
 (23) 
 The parameters β ′ \beta^{\prime} , τ \tau and Y PBH Y_{\mathrm{PBH}} all depend on M M but we suppose a monochromatic mass function in what follows.
The initial baryon-to-photon ratio is set to the present one after allowing for entropy production from PBH evaporations and photon heating due to e + ​ e − e^{+}e^{-} annihilations. 
 Figure 5: Upper bounds on β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) at 95 % confidence level required for success of BBN model, using data from Refs.  Kohri and Yokoyama 1999 ; Kawasaki et al. 2018 ; Kawasaki et al. 2020 .
The broken line gives the earlier limit of Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a . 
 Figure  5 shows the 95 % confidence-level limits which result from these calculations (see CKSY for further details).
PBHs with lifetime shorter than 10 − 2 ​ s 10^{-2}\,\mathrm{s} are free from BBN constraints because they evaporate well before weak freeze-out and leave no trace.
PBHs with M ≈ 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 10 ​ g M\approx 10^{9}\text{--}10^{10}\,\mathrm{g} and lifetime τ ≈ 10 − 2 ​ – ​ 10 2 ​ s \tau\approx 10^{-2}\text{--}10^{2}\,\mathrm{s} are constrained by the extra interconversion between protons and neutrons due to emitted mesons and antinucleons, which increases the n / p n/p freeze-out ratio as well as the final He 4 {}^{4}\mathrm{He} abundance.
For τ ≈ 10 2 ​ – ​ 10 7 ​ s \tau\approx 10^{2}\text{--}10^{7}\,\mathrm{s} , corresponding to M ≈ 10 10 ​ – ​ 10 12 ​ g M\approx 10^{10}\text{--}10^{12}\,\mathrm{g} , hadro-dissociation becomes important and the debris deuterons and non-thermally produced Li 6 {}^{6}\mathrm{Li} put constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) .
Finally, for τ ≈ 10 7 ​ – ​ 10 12 ​ s \tau\approx 10^{7}\text{--}10^{12}\,\mathrm{s} , corresponding to M ≈ 10 12 ​ – ​ 10 13 ​ g M\approx 10^{12}\text{--}10^{13}\,\mathrm{g} , energetic neutrons decay before inducing hadro-dissociation.
Instead, photodissociation processes are operative and the most stringent constraint comes from overproduction of He 3 {}^{3}\mathrm{He} .
However, even these effects become insignificant after 10 12 ​ s 10^{12}\,\mathrm{s} . 
 These computations involved Monte-Carlo simulations and included experimental and observational uncertainties in the baryon number and reaction and decay rates to obtain error bars in the light element abundances.
The bounds should therefore be much more conservative than the ones obtained without these uncertainties.
We adopted the upper bound on the abundance of He 3 / D {}^{3}\mathrm{He}/\mathrm{D} rather than He 3 / H {}^{3}\mathrm{He}/\mathrm{H} since it is more reliable.
Because He 3 {}^{3}\mathrm{He} is both produced and destroyed in low-mass stars, the observed local value of He 3 / H {}^{3}\mathrm{He}/\mathrm{H} does not imply an upper bound on the primordial component  Kawasaki et al. 2005a .
However, the He 3 / D {}^{3}\mathrm{He}/\mathrm{D} observed in the solar system should give a reasonable upper bound on the primordial value because this increases with cosmic time  Kawasaki et al. 2005b .
In addition, we included both hadronic and radiative emissions;
these can occur together and cancel each other, which reduces the constraint in overlapping regions.
Reference  Acharya and Khatri 2020a claims stronger BBN constraints on evaporating PBHs but does not consider these effects.
For comparison, Fig.  5 shows as a broken line the earlier BBN limit obtained in Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a .
The deuterium limit gives the strongest bound for M ≳ 10 10 ​ g M\gtrsim 10^{10}\,\mathrm{g} because hadro-dissociation of helium produces more deuterium, which is severely constrained by more precise recent observational data  Kawasaki et al. 2018 ; Kawasaki et al. 2020 . 
 Keith et al.   Keith et al. 2020 have revisited the BBN constraints, making use of more recent deuterium and helium measurements and considering how physics beyond the Standard Model could impact the constraints.
They discuss scenarios which feature large hidden sectors, as well as models of TeV-scale supersymmetry.
Although their constraints seem to be stronger than ours, they depend on limited data taken from figures of Kawasaki et al.   Kawasaki et al. 2018 and this neglects a lot of relevant information, so we do not include them in Fig.  5 . 
 II.1.2 Lithium-7 
 Overproduction of Li 7 {}^{7}\mathrm{Li} by a factor ∼ 3 \sim 3 is the most serious issue with the standard cosmological model Coc 2017 .
Astrophysical solutions have so far failed Korn et al. 2006 ; Fu et al. 2015 .
Particle decay models reduce lithium via neutron injection but generically overproduce D \mathrm{D} Coc et al. 2014 , for which there are increasingly precise constraints.
Therefore more exotic particle decays – involving neutron-triggered destruction of Be 7 {}^{7}\mathrm{Be} , followed by γ \gamma -ray destruction of excess D \mathrm{D} – have been suggested, notably those associated with a leptophilic metastable massive particle of mass ∼ 10 ​ MeV \sim 10\,\mathrm{MeV} Goudelis et al. 2016 .
However, PBHs evaporating at ∼ 10 2 ​ – ​ 10 4 ​ s \sim 10^{2}\text{--}10^{4}\,\mathrm{s} might provide a plausible alternative.
For example, PBHs with an extended mass function around 10 12 ​ g 10^{12}\,\mathrm{g} could provide an early injection of neutrons, followed by soft γ \gamma -rays.
This combination could destroy some Be 7 {}^{7}\mathrm{Be} at ∼ 10 2 ​ s \sim 10^{2}\,\mathrm{s} , avoiding overproduction of Li 7 {}^{7}\mathrm{Li} , and subsequently destroy excess D \mathrm{D} at ∼ 10 3 ​ – ​ 10 4 ​ s \sim 10^{3}\text{--}10^{4}\,\mathrm{s} . 
 II.2 Cosmic microwave background 
 II.2.1 Generation of entropy 
 The effects of PBH evaporations on the CMB were first analysed by Zel’dovich et al. Zel’dovich et al. 1977 .
They pointed out that photons from PBHs smaller than 10 9 ​ g 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} are emitted sufficiently early to be completely thermalised and merely contribute to the photon-to-baryon ratio.
The requirement that this does not exceed the observed ratio of around 10 9 10^{9} leads to a limit 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < 10 9 ​ ( M M Pl ) − 1 ≈ 10 − 5 ​ ( M 10 9 ​ g ) − 1 ( M < 10 9 ​ g ) , \beta^{\prime}(M)<10^{9}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\right)^{-1}\approx 10^{-5}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{9}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-1}\quad(M<10^{9}\,\mathrm{g})\,, 
 (24) 
 where M Pl M_{\mathrm{Pl}} is the Planck mass, so only PBHs below 10 4 ​ g 10^{4}\,\mathrm{g} could generate all of the CMB.
This limit is not shown in Fig.  4 because it is very weak. 
 II.2.2 CMB spectral distortions 
 Zel’dovich et al.   Zel’dovich et al. 1977 also noted that photons from PBHs in the range 10 11 ​ g < M < 10 13 ​ g 10^{11}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{13}\,\mathrm{g} , although partially thermalised, will produce noticeable distortions in the CMB spectrum unless 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < ( M M Pl ) − 1 ≈ 10 − 16 ​ ( M 10 11 ​ g ) − 1 ( 10 11 ​ g < M < 10 13 ​ g ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)<\left(\frac{M}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\right)^{-1}\approx 10^{-16}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{11}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-1}\quad(10^{11}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{13}\,\mathrm{g})\,. 
 (25) 
 This corresponds to the fraction of the density in PBHs being less than unity at evaporation.
In the intermediate mass range, 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 11 ​ g 10^{9}\text{--}10^{11}\,\mathrm{g} , there is a transition from limit ( 24 ) to the much stronger limit ( 25 ).
Subsequently the form of these distortions has been analysed in more detail.
If an appreciable number of photons are emitted after the freeze-out of double-Compton scattering ( t > 7 × 10 6 ​ s t>7\times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{s} ), corresponding to M > 10 11 ​ g M>10^{11}\,\mathrm{g} , the distribution of the CMB photons develops a non-zero chemical potential, leading to a μ \mu -distortion.
If the photons are emitted after the freeze-out of the single-Compton scattering ( t > 3 × 10 9 ​ s t>3\times 10^{9}\,\mathrm{s} ), corresponding to M > 10 12 ​ g M>10^{12}\,\mathrm{g} , the distribution is modified by a y y -distortion. 
 These constraints were first calculated in the context of decaying particle models Sarkar and Cooper-Sarkar 1984 ; Ellis et al. 1992 ; Hu and Silk 1993 .
In the PBH context, calculations of Tashiro and Sugiyama Tashiro and Sugiyama 2008 showed that the CMB distortion constraints are of order β ′ ​ ( M ) < 10 − 21 \beta^{\prime}(M)<10^{-21} for some range of M M , which is weaker than the BBN constraint but stronger than the constraint given by Eq. ( 25 ).
Later Lucca et al.   Lucca et al. 2020 pointed out some limitations of the Tashiro–Sugiyama analysis and on the basis of some strong assumptions significantly improved the PBH constraints.
Acharya and Khatri  Acharya and Khatri 2020b and Chluba et al.   Chluba et al. 2020 also derived stronger spectral distortion constraints by including secondary emission of electromagnetic particles but they used more conservative assumptions than Lucca et al. , whom they criticised for assuming that all the emitted energy goes into non-relativistic y y and μ \mu distortions.
Figure  4 uses the y y and μ \mu bounds found in a later paper by Acharya and Khatri  Acharya and Khatri 2020a and the μ \mu bound found by Chluba et al.   Chluba et al. 2020 .
PBH evaporations could also be constrained by their effect on the form of the recombination lines in the CMB spectrum Sunyaev and Chluba 2009 , just as in the annihilating DM scenario Chluba 2010 ; Slatyer et al. 2009 . 
 II.2.3 CMB anisotropies 
 Another constraint on PBHs evaporating after the time of recombination is associated with the damping of small-scale CMB anisotropies.
This limit was obtained in Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a by modifying an equivalent calculation for decaying particles, first described by Adams et al.   Adams et al. 1998 .
According to Zhang et al. Zhang et al. 2007 , the latter constraint can be written in the form 
 log 10 ⁡ ζ < − 10.8 − 0.50 ​ x + 0.085 ​ x 2 + 0.0045 ​ x 3 , x ≡ log 10 ⁡ ( Γ 10 − 13 ​ s − 1 ) , \log_{10}\zeta<-10.8-0.50\,x+0.085\,x^{2}+0.0045\,x^{3}\,,\quad x\equiv\log_{10}\left(\frac{\Gamma}{10^{-13}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}}\right)\,, 
 (26) 
 where Γ \Gamma is the decay rate, which corresponds to τ ​ ( M ) − 1 \tau(M)^{-1} in the PBH case, and ζ \zeta is the fraction of the CDM in PBHs times the fraction of the emitted energy which goes into heating the matter.
The last factor, which includes the effects of the electrons and positrons as well as the photons, will be denoted by f H f_{\mathrm{H}} and depends on the redshift MacGibbon and Carr 1991 ; Chen and Kamionkowski 2004 .
Most of the heating will be associated with the electrons and positrons; they are initially degraded by inverse Compton scattering off the CMB photons but after scattering have an energy 
 γ 2 ​ E CMB ≈ 300 ​ ( γ 10 3 ) 2 ​ ( 1 + z ) ​ eV ≈ 5 × 10 5 ​ ( M 10 13 ​ g ) − 2 ​ ( 1 + z ) ​ eV ≈ 20 ​ ( 1 + z ) 2 ​ eV , \gamma^{2}\,E_{\mathrm{CMB}}\approx 300\,\left(\frac{\gamma}{10^{3}}\right)^{2}\,(1+z)\,\mathrm{eV}\approx 5\times 10^{5}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-2}\,(1+z)\,\mathrm{eV}\approx 20\,(1+z)^{2}\,\mathrm{eV}\,, 
 (27) 
 where γ \gamma is the Lorentz factor and in the last expression we assume that the mass of a PBH evaporating at redshift z z in the matter-dominated era is M ≈ M ∗ ( 1 + z ) − 1 / 2 M\approx M_{*}\,(1+z)^{-1/2} .
Since this energy is always above the ionisation threshold for hydrogen ( 13.6 ​ eV 13.6\,\mathrm{eV} ), we can assume that the heating of the electrons and positrons is efficient before reionisation.
Using Eq. ( 5 ) for β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) and Eq. ( 14 ) for τ ⁡ ( M ) \tau(M) , one can now express Eq. ( 26 ) as a limit on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) .
In the mass range of interest, the rather complicated cubic expression in M M can be fitted by the approximation 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < 3 × 10 − 30 ​ ( f H 0.1 ) − 1 ​ ( M 10 13 ​ g ) 3.1 ( 2.5 × 10 13 ​ g ≲ M ≲ 2.4 × 10 14 ​ g ) , \beta^{\prime}(M)<3\times 10^{-30}\,\left(\frac{f_{\mathrm{H}}}{0.1}\right)^{-1}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{3.1}\quad(2.5\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}\lesssim M\lesssim 2.4\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g})\,, 
 (28) 
 where f H ≈ 0.1 f_{\mathrm{H}}\approx 0.1 is the fraction of emission in electrons and positrons.
Here the lower mass limit corresponds to PBHs evaporating at recombination and the upper one to those evaporating at a redshift 6 6   Fan et al. 2006 , after which the ionisation ensures the opacity is too low for the emitted electrons and positrons to heat the matter. 
 Equation ( 28 ) is stronger than all the other available limits in this mass range but had not been pointed out before CKSY.
Recently it has been studied more carefully by Poulin et al. Poulin et al. 2017a , who compute CMB anisotropy constraints on electromagnetic energy injection over a large range of timescales.
They apply their formalism for PBHs in the range 10 13.5 ​ g < M < 10 16.8 ​ g 10^{13.5}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{16.8}\,\mathrm{g} , showing that the anisotropy constraints are stronger than the EGB limit below around 10 14 ​ g 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} .
Stöcker et al. Stöcker et al. 2018 have followed up on this work, using the Boltzmann code CLASS.
Their bounds are several orders of magnitude stronger than the EGB limit in the range 10 13.5 ​ g < M < 10 14.5 ​ g 10^{13.5}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{14.5}\,\mathrm{g} and exclude PBHs with a monochromatic mass distribution in the range 10 13.5 ​ g < M < 10 16.3 ​ g 10^{13.5}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{16.3}\,\mathrm{g} from containing all of the DM.
Poulter et al. Poulter et al. have extended these constraints to the case in which the PBHs have a non-monochromatic mass function. 
 Lucca et al.   Lucca et al. 2020 find a stronger anisotropy constraint and their combined anisotropy/spectral distortion constraint appears to dominate other ones over a wide range of masses:
it is stronger than the BBN and EGB limit for 10 14.4 ​ g > M > 10 12.8 ​ g 10^{14.4}\,\mathrm{g}>M>10^{12.8}\,\mathrm{g} , the EGB and GGB limit for M > 10 15 ​ g M>10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} and the Voyager-1 electron and positron limit for M > 10 15.6 ​ g M>10^{15.6}\,\mathrm{g} .
However, Lucca et al. use the “on-the-spot” limit, which assumes that all of the injected energy goes into thermal bath immediately.
This is a good approximation for spectral distortions generated before recombination but not for CMB anisotropies generated after recombination because non-thermal relativistic particles such as electrons and positrons only gradually transfer their energy into the thermal bath.
This means that the on-the-spot limit gives overly strong bounds.
Figure  4 adopts the anisotropy constraint given in the later paper by Acharya and Khatri  Acharya and Khatri 2020a , in which they calculate the energy transferred into the thermal plasma without adopting the on-the-spot limit. 
 II.3 Extragalactic and Galactic γ \gamma -ray backgrounds 
 II.3.1 Extragalactic γ \gamma -ray background 
 One of the earliest works that applied the theory of black hole evaporation to astrophysics was carried out by Page and Hawking Page and Hawking 1976 .
They used the diffuse EGB observations to constrain the mean cosmological number density of PBHs which are completing their evaporation at the present epoch to be less than 10 4 ​ pc − 3 10^{4}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3} .
This corresponds to an upper limit on Ω PBH \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}} of around 10 − 8 10^{-8} .
The limit was subsequently refined by MacGibbon and Carr MacGibbon and Carr 1991 who considered how it is modified by including quark and gluon emission and inferred Ω PBH ≤ ( 7.6 ± 2.6 ) × 10 − 9 ​ h − 2 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}\leq(7.6\pm 2.6)\times 10^{-9}\,h^{-2} , corresponding to a 2 ​ σ 2\sigma upper limit of 1.3 × 10 − 8 ​ h − 2 1.3\times 10^{-8}\,h^{-2} .
Later they used EGRET observations to derive a slightly stronger limit Ω PBH ≤ ( 5.1 ± 1.3 ) × 10 − 9 ​ h − 2 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}\leq(5.1\pm 1.3)\times 10^{-9}\,h^{-2} Carr and MacGibbon 1998 or a 2 ​ σ 2\sigma upper limit of 7.7 × 10 − 9 ​ h − 2 7.7\times 10^{-9}\,h^{-2} .
Using the modern value of h h gives Ω PBH ≤ 1.5 × 10 − 8 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}\leq 1.5\times 10^{-8} and this corresponds to β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) < 6 × 10 − 26 \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})<6\times 10^{-26} from Eq. ( 6 ).
They also inferred from the form of the γ \gamma -ray spectrum that PBHs could not provide the dominant contribution to the background. 
 We now describe the more precise calculation of CKSY, evaluating the primary and secondary emission according to the prescription of Sec.  I.4.2 .
In order to determine the present background spectrum of photons generated by PBH evaporations, we must integrate over the lifetime of the black holes, allowing for the fact that particles generated at earlier cosmological epochs will be redshifted in energy by now.
If the PBHs all have the same initial mass M M , and if we approximate the number of emitted photons in the energy bin Δ ​ E γ ≃ E γ \Delta E_{\gamma}\simeq E_{\gamma} by N ˙ γ ​ ( E γ ) ≃ E γ ​ ( d ​ N ˙ γ / d ​ E γ CLOSE \dot{N}_{\gamma}(E_{\gamma})\simeq E_{\gamma}\,(\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\gamma}/\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma} ), then the emission rate per volume at cosmological time t t is 
 d ​ n γ d ​ t ​ ( E γ , t ) ≃ n PBH ​ ( t ) ​ E γ ​ d ​ N ˙ γ d ​ E γ ​ ( M ⁡ ( t ) , E γ ) , \frac{\mathrm{d}n_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}t}(E_{\gamma},t)\simeq n_{\mathrm{PBH}}(t)\,E_{\gamma}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(M(t),E_{\gamma})\,, 
 (29) 
 where the t t -dependence of M M just reflects the evaporation.
Since the photon energy and density are redshifted by factors ( 1 + z ) − 1 (1+z)^{-1} and ( 1 + z ) − 3 (1+z)^{-3} , respectively, the present number density of photons with energy E γ ​ 0 E_{\gamma 0} is 
 n γ ​ 0 ​ ( E γ ​ 0 ) = n PBH0 ​ E γ ​ 0 ​ ∫ t min min ⁡ ( t 0 , τ ) d ​ t ​ ( 1 + z ) ​ d ​ N ˙ γ d ​ E γ ​ ( M ⁡ ( t ) , ( 1 + z ) ​ E γ ​ 0 ) , n_{\gamma 0}(E_{\gamma 0})=n_{\mathrm{PBH}0}\,E_{\gamma 0}\,\int_{t_{\mathrm{min}}}^{\min(t_{0},\tau)}\!\mathrm{d}t\,(1+z)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E_{\gamma}}(M(t),(1+z)\,E_{\gamma 0})\,, 
 (30) 
 where t min t_{\mathrm{min}} corresponds to the earliest time at which the photons freely propagate and n PBH0 n_{\mathrm{PBH}0} is the current PBH number density for M > M ∗ M>M_{*} or the number density they would have now had they not evaporated for M < M ∗ M<M_{*}  .
The photon flux is 
 I ≡ c 4 ​ π ​ n γ ​ 0 . I\equiv\frac{c}{4\pi}\,n_{\gamma 0}\,. 
 (31) 
 The calculated present-day fluxes of primary and secondary photons are shown in Fig.  6 , where the number density n PBH0 n_{\mathrm{PBH}0} for each M M has the maximum value consistent with the observations. 
 Figure 6: Fluxes corresponding to the upper limit on the PBH abundance for various values of M M , updated from those in Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a with new observational data from Fermi-LAT  Ackermann et al. 2015 .
All PBHs produce primary photons but those with M ≲ M ∗ M\lesssim M_{*} also produce secondary photons (dotted lines), giving a stronger constraint on β \beta . 
 Note that the highest energy photons are associated with PBHs of mass M ∗ M_{*}  .
Photons from PBHs with M > M ∗ M>M_{*} are at lower energies because they are cooler, while photons from PBHs with M < M ∗ M<M_{*} are at lower energies because (although initially hotter) they are redshifted.
The spectral shape depends on the mass M M and can be easily understood.
PBHs with M > M ∗ M>M_{*} have a rather sharp peak, well approximated by the instantaneous black-body emission of the primary photons, while PBHs with M ≤ M ∗ M\leq M_{*} have an E γ ​ 0 − 2 E_{\gamma 0}^{-2} fall-off for E γ ​ 0 ≫ T BH / [ 1 + z ⁡ ( τ ) ] E_{\gamma 0}\gg T_{\mathrm{BH}}/[1+z(\tau)] due to their final phase of evaporation MacGibbon 1991 . 
 The relevant observations come from HEAO 1 and other balloon observations in the 3 ​ – ​ 500 ​ keV 3\text{--}500\,\mathrm{keV} range, COMPTEL in the 0.8 ​ – ​ 30 ​ MeV 0.8\text{--}30\,\mathrm{MeV} range, EGRET in the 30 ​ – ​ 100 ​ MeV 30\text{--}100\,\mathrm{MeV} range and Fermi-LAT in the 100 ​ MeV ​ – ​ 820 ​ GeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV}\text{--}820\,\mathrm{GeV} range.
All the observations are shown in Fig.  6 .
The origin of the diffuse X-ray and γ \gamma -ray backgrounds is thought to be primarily distant astrophysical sources, such as blazars, and in principle one should remove these contributions before calculating the PBH constraints.
This is the strategy adopted by Barrau et al. Barrau et al. 2003a , who thereby obtain a limit Ω PBH ≤ 3.3 × 10 − 9 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}\leq 3.3\times 10^{-9} .
CKSY did not attempt such a subtraction, so their constraints on β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) were more conservative. 
 Figure 7: Upper bounds on β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) from the extragalactic photon background, from Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a but updated, with no other contributors to the background having been subtracted. 
 In order to analyse the spectra of photons emitted from PBHs, different treatments are needed for PBHs with initial masses below and above M ∗ M_{*}  .
We saw in Sec.  I.4.2 that PBHs with M > M ∗ M>M_{*} can never emit secondary photons at the present epoch, whereas those with M ≤ M ∗ M\leq M_{*} will do so once M M falls below M q ≈ 2 × 10 14 ​ g M_{\mathrm{q}}\approx 2\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} .
One can use simple analytical arguments to derive the form of the primary and secondary peak fluxes.
The observed X-ray and γ \gamma -ray spectra correspond to I obs ∝ E γ ​ 0 − ( 1 + ϵ ) I^{\mathrm{obs}}\propto E_{\gamma 0}^{-(1+\epsilon)} where ϵ \epsilon lies between 0.1 0.1 and 0.4 0.4 .
For M < M ∗ M<M_{*}  , the limit is determined by the secondary flux and one can write the upper bound on β ′ \beta^{\prime} as 
 β ′ ( M ) ≲ 5 × 10 − 28 ( M M ∗ ) − 5 / 2 − 2 ϵ ( M < M ∗ ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)\lesssim 5\times 10^{-28}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{-5/2-2\,\epsilon}\quad(M<M_{*})\,. 
 (32) 
 For M > M ∗ M>M_{*}  , secondary photons are not emitted and one obtains a limit 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) ≲ 5 × 10 − 26 ​ ( M M ∗ ) 7 / 2 + ϵ ( M > M ∗ ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)\lesssim 5\times 10^{-26}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{7/2+\epsilon}\quad(M>M_{*})\,. 
 (33) 
 These M M -dependences explain qualitatively the slopes in Fig.  7 , for which we assume ϵ = 0.4 \epsilon=0.4 .
The limit drops by a factor of 7 7 below the mass M ∗ M_{*} because of secondary emission and bottoms out at 4 × 10 − 28 4\times 10^{-28} .
There is also a narrow band M ∗ < M < 1.005 ​ M ∗ M_{*}<M<1.005\,M_{*} in which PBHs have not yet completed their evaporation even though their current mass is below the mass M q ≈ 0.4 ​ M ∗ M_{\mathrm{q}}\approx 0.4\,M_{*} at which quark and gluon jets are emitted.
From Eq. ( 6 ), the associated limit on the density parameter is Ω PBH ​ ( M ∗ ) ≤ 7 × 10 − 11 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M_{*})\leq 7\times 10^{-11} .
This is factor 7 7 stronger than the limit in CKSY. 
 We stress that the discontinuity in the EGB constraint at the mass M ∗ M_{*} is entirely a consequence of the (presumably unrealistic) assumption that the PBHs have an exactly monochromatic mass function and even a tiny mass width Δ ​ M \Delta M would suffice to smear this out.
Indeed, many derivations of the EGB constraint, including the original Page–Hawking calculation, assume that the PBHs have a power-law mass function.
In this case, the discontinuity is removed and the stronger ( M < M ∗ M<M_{*} ) limit pertains.
Nevertheless, the discontinuity is still interesting in principle and the issue of non-monochromaticity will be even more important when we consider the Galactic background constraint. 
 Finally, we determine the mass range over which the γ \gamma -ray background constraint applies.
Since photons emitted at sufficiently early times cannot propagate freely, there is a minimum mass M min M_{\mathrm{min}} below which the above constraint is inapplicable.
The dominant interactions between γ \gamma -rays and the background Universe in the relevant energy range are pair-production off hydrogen and helium nuclei.
For the opacity appropriate for a 75 % hydrogen and 25 % helium mix, the redshift below which there is free propagation is given by MacGibbon and Carr 1991 
 1 + z max ≈ 1155 ( h 0.67 ) − 2 / 3 ( Ω b 0.05 ) − 2 / 3 , 1+z_{\mathrm{max}}\approx 1155\,\left(\frac{h}{0.67}\right)^{-2/3}\,\left(\frac{\Omega_{\mathrm{b}}}{0.05}\right)^{-2/3}\,, 
 (34) 
 with the baryon density parameter Ω b \Omega_{\mathrm{b}} normalised to the modern value.
The condition τ ⁡ ( M min ) = t ⁡ ( z max ) \tau(M_{\mathrm{min}})=t(z_{\mathrm{max}}) then gives
