 M min = ( t ⁡ ( z max ) t 0 ) 1 / 3 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M min ) f ∗ ) 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ≈ 3 × 10 13 ​ g . M_{\mathrm{min}}=\left(\frac{t(z_{\mathrm{max}})}{t_{0}}\right)^{1/3}\,\left(\frac{f(M_{\mathrm{min}})}{f_{*}}\right)^{1/3}\,M_{*}\approx 3\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (35) 
 The limit is therefore extended down to this mass in Fig.  7 .
It goes above the density constraint Ω PBH ​ ( M ) < 0.25 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M)<0.25 for M > 7 × 10 16 ​ g M>7\times 10^{16}\,\mathrm{g} . 
 Ballesteros et al. Ballesteros et al. 2020a have recently improved the EGB bound and extended its mass range by better modeling of the combined AGN and blazar emission in the MeV range.
They also estimate the constraints from any future X-ray experiment which can identify a significantly larger number of astrophysical sources contributing to the diffuse background in this energy range.
However, the conservative upper bound on the PBH abundance given in Fig. 2 of their paper is consistent with that given above.
Coogan et al.   Coogan et al. 2021 have recently studied the generation of soft γ \gamma -rays by asteroid-mass PBHs in nearby astrophysical structures and then used COMPTEL data to limit their DM contribution.
This already provides the strongest constraint up to 10 17 ​ g 10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} and is shown in Fig.  4 ; the next generation of MeV telescopes will further constrain the PBH parameter space. 
 In another refinement of the γ \gamma -ray background limit, Arbey et al. Arbey et al. 2020 have extended the EGB constraint on PBHs with masses 10 13 ​ – ​ 10 18 ​ g 10^{13}\text{--}10^{18}\,\mathrm{g} from a monochromatic distribution of Schwarzschild black holes to a non-monochromatic distribution of rotating Kerr black holes, showing that the lower mass window can be closed for near-extremal ones.
Constraints on spinning PBHs associated with superradiance have also been obtained in Ref.  Fukuda and Nakayama 2020 .
This effect has been studied further by Ferraz et al.   Ferraz et al. , who show that highly spinning PBHs of mass ∼ 10 12 ​ kg \sim 10^{12}\,\mathrm{kg} , potentially born in a matter-dominated era after inflation, can produce clouds of pions via the superradiant instability with up to nuclear density.
Neutral pion decay and charged pion annihilation then produce an isotropic γ \gamma -ray background comparable to that from Hawking evaporation. 
 II.3.2 Galactic γ \gamma -ray background 
 If PBHs of mass M ∗ M_{*} are clustered inside our own Galactic halo, as expected, then there should also be a Galactic γ \gamma -ray background and, since this would be anisotropic, it should be separable from the extragalactic background.
The ratio of the anisotropic to isotropic intensity depends on the Galactic longitude and latitude, the Galactic core radius and the halo flattening.
Some time ago Wright Wright 1996 claimed that such a halo background had been detected in EGRET observations between 30 ​ MeV 30\,\mathrm{MeV} and 120 ​ MeV 120\,\mathrm{MeV} Sreekumar et al. 1998 and attributed this to PBHs.
His detailed fit to the data, subtracting various other known components, required the PBH clustering factor to be ( 2 ​ – ​ 12 ) × 10 5 ​ h − 1 (2\text{--}12)\times 10^{5}\,h^{-1} , comparable to that expected, and the local PBH explosion rate to be ℛ = 0.07 ​ – ​ 0.42 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 \mathcal{R}=0.07\text{--}0.42\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} .
A later analysis of EGRET data between 70 ​ MeV 70\,\mathrm{MeV} and 150 ​ MeV 150\,\mathrm{MeV} , assuming a variety of distributions for the PBHs, was given by Lehoucq et al. Lehoucq et al. 2009 .
In the isothermal model, which gives the most conservative limit, the Galactic γ \gamma -ray background requires ℛ ≤ 0.06 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 \mathcal{R}\leq 0.06\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} .
They claimed that this corresponds to Ω PBH ​ ( M ∗ ) ≤ 2.6 × 10 − 9 \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M_{*})\leq 2.6\times 10^{-9} , which from Eq. ( 6 ) implies β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) < 1.4 × 10 − 26 \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})<1.4\times 10^{-26} , a factor of 30 30 above the extragalactic background constraint ( 32 ).
Lehoucq et al. themselves claimed that it corresponds to β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) < 1.9 × 10 − 27 \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})<1.9\times 10^{-27} because they used an inaccurate formula relating Ω PBH \Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}} and β \beta . 
 It should be stressed that the Lehoucq et al. analysis does not constrain PBHs of initial mass M ∗ M_{*} because these no longer exist.
Rather it constrains PBHs of current mass M ∗ M_{*} and, from Eq. ( 20 ) with m = M ∗ m=M_{*}  , this corresponds to an initial mass of 1.26 ​ M ∗ 1.26\,M_{*}  .
This contrasts to the situation with the extragalactic background, where the strongest constraint on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) comes from the time-integrated contribution of the M ∗ M_{*} black holes.
So the black holes generating the Galactic background correspond to the partially-evaporated “tail” population and the Galactic limit is sensitive to the non-monochromaticity in the PBH mass function.
In the present context, we assume this is narrow, so that β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) can be defined as the integral over the entire mass width. 
 To examine which value of M M corresponds to the strongest limit on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) , CKSY noted that Eq. ( 20 ) implies that the emission from PBHs with initial mass ( 1 + μ ) ​ M ∗ (1+\mu)\,M_{*} currently peaks at an energy E ≈ 100 ( 3 μ ) − 1 / 3 MeV E\approx 100\,(3\,\mu)^{-1/3}\,\mathrm{MeV} for μ < 1 \mu<1 , which is in the range 70 ​ – ​ 150 ​ MeV 70\text{--}150\,\mathrm{MeV} for 0.7 > μ > 0.08 0.7>\mu>0.08 .
The peak energy is above 150 ​ MeV 150\,\mathrm{MeV} for μ < 0.08 \mu<0.08 , so the γ \gamma -ray band is in the Rayleigh–Jeans region, where the flux of an individual hole scales as m 3 ∝ μ m^{3}\propto\mu , so the limit on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) scales as μ − 1 \mu^{-1} .
For 0.7 > μ > 0.08 0.7>\mu>0.08 , the current number flux of photons from each PBH scales as m − 1 ∝ μ − 1 / 3 m^{-1}\propto\mu^{-1/3} , so the effective limit on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) scales as μ 1 / 3 \mu^{1/3} .
The observed γ \gamma -ray band enters the Wien part of the spectrum for μ > 0.7 \mu>0.7 , so the limit on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) weakens exponentially for M > 1.7 ​ M ∗ M>1.7\,M_{*}  .
Hence the largest contribution to the Galactic background and the strongest constraint on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) comes from PBHs with M ≈ 1.08 ​ M ∗ M\approx 1.08\,M_{*} and has the form indicated in Fig.  4 . 
 Subsequently, the problem was studied in much greater detail by CKSY2 Carr et al. 2016b .
To go beyond the Lehoucq et al. analysis, we included several important effects.
First, we distinguished between the initial mass M = ( 1 + μ ) ​ M ∗ M=(1+\mu)\,M_{*} and the current mass m m , this being given by 
 m = { [ ( μ + 1 ) 3 − 1 + ( 1 − α − 1 ) ​ q 3 ] 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ( μ ≥ μ c ) ( 3 ​ α ​ μ ) 1 / 3 ​ ( 1 + μ + μ 2 / 3 ) 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ( 0 ≤ μ ≤ μ c ) , m=\begin{cases}\left[(\mu+1)^{3}-1+(1-\alpha^{-1})\,q^{3}\right]^{1/3}\,M_{*}&(\mu\geq\mu_{\mathrm{c}})\\
(3\,\alpha\,\mu)^{1/3}\,(1+\mu+\mu^{2}/3)^{1/3}\,M_{*}&(0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{\mathrm{c}})\,,\end{cases} 
 (36) 
 where 
 μ c ≈ q 3 / ( 3 ​ α ) = 0.005 ​ ( α / 4 ) − 1 ​ ( q / 0.4 ) 3 \mu_{\mathrm{c}}\approx q^{3}/(3\,\alpha)=0.005\,(\alpha/4)^{-1}\,(q/0.4)^{3} 
 (37) 
 is the value of μ \mu corresponding to the mass M c M_{\mathrm{c}} at which secondary emission eventually becomes important.
Second, we distinguished between primary and secondary emission (see Fig.  3 ).
Third, we distinguished between initial mass function d ​ n / d ​ M \mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}M and the current one, 
 d ​ n d ​ m = [ 1 α ​ ( m M ∗ ) 2 ​ ( d ​ n d ​ M ) ∗ , ( m M ∗ ) 2 ​ ( d ​ n d ​ M ) ∗ , ( d ​ n d ​ M ) ] for [ m < M q , M q < m ⁡ < M ∗ , m > ​ M ∗ ] , \frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}m}=\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\,\left(\frac{m}{M_{*}}\right)^{2}\,\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\right)_{*}\,,\left(\frac{m}{M_{*}}\right)^{2}\,\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\right)_{*}\,,\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\right)\right]\quad\textnormal{for}\quad[m<M_{\mathrm{q}}\,,M_{\mathrm{q}}<m<M_{*}\,,m>M_{*}]\,, 
 (38) 
 where M q M_{\mathrm{q}} is given by Eq. ( 17 ), so the main GGB contribution comes from the d ​ n / d ​ m ∝ m 2 \mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}m\propto m^{2} low mass tail. 
 Following Lehoucq et al. , we took the halo density profile to have the form 
 ρ PBH ​ ( R ) = f ​ ρ s ( R / R s ) γ ​ [ 1 + ( R / R s ) α ] ( β − α ) / α , \rho_{\mathrm{PBH}}(R)=\frac{f\,\rho_{\mathrm{s}}}{(R/R_{\mathrm{s}})^{\gamma}\,[1+(R/R_{\mathrm{s}})^{\alpha}]^{(\beta-\alpha)/\alpha}}\,, 
 (39) 
 with a set of best-fit parameters γ = 1.24 , α = 1 , β = 4 − γ = 2.86 , R s = 28.1 ​ kpc , ρ s = 3.50 × 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ ​ pc − 3 \gamma=1.24,\alpha=1,\beta=4-\gamma=2.86,R_{\mathrm{s}}=28.1\,\mathrm{kpc}\,,\rho_{\mathrm{s}}=3.50\times 10^{-3}\,M_{\odot}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3} .
We described the directional dependence with the function 
 g ⁡ ( 𝒏 ) = 1 r gal ​ ∫ 0 r gal d ​ r ​ ρ PBH ​ ( R ​ ( 𝒏 , r ) ) ρ ¯ PBH , g(\boldsymbol{n})=\frac{1}{r_{\mathrm{gal}}}\,\int_{0}^{r_{\mathrm{gal}}}\!\mathrm{d}r\,\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{PBH}}(R(\boldsymbol{n},r))}{\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{PBH}}}\,, 
 (40) 
 with r gal = 100 ​ kpc r_{\mathrm{gal}}=100\,\mathrm{kpc} being our distance from the edge of the halo.
We then compared the predicted intensity with Fermi-LAT observations and inferred constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) and n PBH ​ ( M ) n_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M) .
In Fig.  8 (a), we show the constraint for power-law extended mass function: 
 d ​ n d ​ M = d ​ n d ​ M ∗ ​ ( M M ∗ ) ν ( M < M f ) , \frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}=\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}_{*}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{\nu}\quad(M<M_{\mathrm{f}})\,, 
 (41) 
 this including the critical-collapse case with ν = 1.85 \nu=1.85 .
In Fig.  8 (b), we show the constraint for a nearly monochromatic “top hat” initial mass function: 
 d ​ n d ​ M = { n Δ ​ M f ( ( 1 − Δ ) ​ M f < M < M f ) 0 ( M < ( 1 − Δ ) ​ M f , M f < M ) \frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{\Delta M_{\mathrm{f}}}&((1-\Delta)M_{\mathrm{f}}<M<M_{\mathrm{f}})\\
0&(M<(1-\Delta)\,M_{\mathrm{f}}\,,M_{\mathrm{f}}<M)\end{cases} 
 (42) 
 with fractional width Δ < 1 \Delta<1 and total comoving number density n n .
Here one requires M f > M ∗ M_{\mathrm{f}}>M_{*} and each limit has a kink at the threshold of secondary emission.
The depth of the limit in Fig.  8 is sensitive to Δ \Delta and becomes arbitrarily small as Δ → 0 \Delta\rightarrow 0 .
However, a very small value of Δ \Delta is clearly unphysical and the extragalactic and Galactic limits are comparable for Δ ∼ 1 \Delta\sim 1 . 
 Figure 8: Constraint imposed by Galactic γ \gamma -ray background for an extended mass function (a), as defined by Eq. ( 41 ), or a nearly monochromatic mass function (b), as defined by Eq. ( 42 ), from Ref.  Carr et al. 2016b .
The extragalactic γ \gamma -ray background (EGRB) limit comes from CKSY. 
 II.4 Extragalactic and Galactic cosmic rays 
 II.4.1 Extragalactic antiprotons 
 Galactic antiprotons can constrain PBHs only in a very narrow range around M ≈ M ∗ M\approx M_{*} since they diffusively propagate to the Earth on a time much shorter than the cosmic age.
However, there will also be a spectrum of cosmic ray antiprotons from PBHs sufficiently light that they evaporated well before the epoch of galaxy formation ( ∼ 1 ​ Gyr \sim 1\,\mathrm{Gyr} ).
Although such pregalactic PBHs would not be clustered, the antiprotons they emitted could still be around today and may occasionally enter the Galaxy and be detectable at Earth, so we require that this does not happen frequently enough to exceed the observational limits.
We assume that the maximum mass relevant for this constraint corresponds to the PBHs evaporating at galaxy formation: 
 M ≲ ( 1 ​ Gyr t 0 ) 1 / 3 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) f ∗ ) 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ≈ 2 × 10 14 ​ g . M\lesssim\left(\frac{1\,\mathrm{Gyr}}{t_{0}}\right)^{1/3}\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{f_{*}}\right)^{1/3}\,M_{*}\approx 2\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (43) 
 This mass-scale is not necessarily relevant because even the antiprotons generated inside galaxies may escape on a cosmological timescale and become part of the extragalactic background.
However, the leakage time depends on the size of the cosmic ray region, which is rather uncertain, and could well be comparable to t 0 t_{0}  .
We therefore adopt the above upper mass limit.
The minimum mass relevant for this constraint is determined as follows.
The mean rate of p ¯ ​ p \bar{p}p annihilations in the cosmological background, where the target p p ’s are in hydrogen and helium nuclei, is 
 Γ p ¯ ​ p ​ ( t ) = ⟨ σ p ¯ ​ p ​ v p ¯ ⟩ ​ n p ​ ( t ) ≈ 2 × 10 − 22 ​ ( t t 0 ) − 2 ​ s − 1 , \Gamma_{\bar{p}p}(t)=\langle\sigma_{\bar{p}p}\,v_{\bar{p}}\rangle\,n_{p}(t)\approx 2\times 10^{-22}\,\left(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,, 
 (44) 
 where we have used ⟨ σ p ¯ ​ p ​ v p ¯ ⟩ / c ≈ 4 × 10 − 26 ​ cm 2 \langle\sigma_{\bar{p}p}\,v_{\bar{p}}\rangle/c\approx 4\times 10^{-26}\,\mathrm{cm}^{2} and n p ​ ( t 0 ) ≈ 2 × 10 − 7 ​ cm − 3 n_{p}(t_{0})\approx 2\times 10^{-7}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3} .
The condition that an antiproton emitted by a PBH with lifetime τ \tau survives until now is then 
 ∫ τ t 0 Γ p ¯ ​ p ​ ( t ′ ) ​ d ​ t ′ ≈ ( t 0 τ ) ​ ( t 0 5 × 10 21 ​ s ) ≈ 1.3 ​ Myr τ < 1 . \int_{\tau}^{t_{0}}\!\Gamma_{\bar{p}p}(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\approx\left(\frac{t_{0}}{\tau}\right)\,\left(\frac{t_{0}}{5\times 10^{21}\,\mathrm{s}}\right)\approx\frac{1.3\,\mathrm{Myr}}{\tau}<1\,. 
 (45) 
 Using Eq. ( 15 ), we infer that the extragalactic p ¯ \bar{p} limit applies for 
 M ≳ ( 1.3 ​ Myr t 0 ) 1 / 3 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) f ∗ ) 1 / 3 ​ M ∗ ≈ 4 × 10 13 ​ g . M\gtrsim\left(\frac{1.3\,\mathrm{Myr}}{t_{0}}\right)^{1/3}\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{f_{*}}\right)^{1/3}\,M_{*}\approx 4\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (46) 
 We have seen there is no primary emission of antiprotons from PBHs and the analog of Eq. ( 19 ) implies that the current number density from secondary emission of PBHs with mass M M is 
 n p ¯ ​ ( M , t 0 ) = n ⁡ ( τ ) ​ τ ( 1 + z ⁡ ( τ ) ) 3 ​ d ​ N ˙ p ¯ d ​ ln ⁡ E p ¯ ​ ( E p ¯ = 100 ​ MeV ) ≈ 6 × 10 11 ​ β ′ ​ ( M ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) f ∗ ) − 1 ​ ( M M ∗ ) 1 / 2 ​ cm − 3 . n_{\bar{p}}(M,t_{0})=\frac{n(\tau)\,\tau}{(1+z(\tau))^{3}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\dot{N}_{\bar{p}}}{\mathrm{d}\ln E_{\bar{p}}}(E_{\bar{p}}=100\,\mathrm{MeV})\approx 6\times 10^{11}\,\beta^{\prime}(M)\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{f_{*}}\right)^{-1}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3}\,. 
 (47) 
 Since the antiproton-to-proton ratio r r is required to be less than 10 − 5 10^{-5} , one finds the upper bound 
 β ′ ( M ) ≲ 3 × 10 − 24 ( r 10 − 5 ) ( f ⁡ ( M ) f ∗ ) ( M M ∗ ) − 1 / 2 ( 4 × 10 13 g ≲ M ≲ 2 × 10 14 g ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)\lesssim 3\times 10^{-24}\,\left(\frac{r}{10^{-5}}\right)\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{f_{*}}\right)\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{-1/2}\quad(4\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}\lesssim M\lesssim 2\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g})\,. 
 (48) 
 This constraint is shown by the green line in Fig.  4 and turns out to be much weaker than the Galactic antiproton constraint (discussed below). 
 II.4.2 Emission of other particles 
 Neutrinos may either be emitted directly as black-body radiation (primaries) or they may result from the decay of emitted pions, leptons, neutrons and anti-neutrons (secondaries).
As a result, their emission spectra are similar to those for photons up to a normalisation factor.
The neutrino background can in principle constrain PBHs whose lifetime exceeds the time of neutrino decoupling ( τ ≳ 1 ​ s \tau\gtrsim 1\,\mathrm{s} ).
This corresponds to a minimum mass of M min , ν ≈ 10 9 ​ g M_{\mathrm{min},\nu}\approx 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} .
However, the low-energy neutrinos which we have to use are poorly limited by observations.
In Super-Kamiokande (SK), the null detection of relic ν ¯ e \bar{\nu}_{e} ’s implies Φ ν ¯ e ≤ 1.2 ​ cm − 2 ​ s − 1 \Phi_{\bar{\nu}_{e}}\leq 1.2\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} above the threshold E ν ¯ e ​ 0 = 19.3 ​ MeV E_{\bar{\nu}_{e}0}=19.3\,\mathrm{MeV} Malek et al. 2003 .
As seen in Fig.  9 , this energy corresponds to the high-energy tail for light PBHs.
The constraint associated with the relic neutrinos is shown by the green line in Fig.  4 and is much weaker than the BBN and photon background limits.
Similar calculations have been made by Bugaev et al. Bugaev and Konishchev 2002 ; Bugaev and Klimai 2009 and Dasgupta et al. Dasgupta et al. 2020 .
However, they assume that the PBHs have a continuous mass function and they link their model with a particular inflationary scenario. 
 Lunardini and Perez-Gonzalez Lunardini and Perez-Gonzalez 2020 show that the neutrino emission depends on whether they are Dirac or Majorana.
In the Dirac case, PBHs radiate right-handed and left-handed neutrinos in equal amounts, thus increasing the effective number of neutrino species N eff N_{\mathrm{eff}}  .
They derive a new bound on β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) for M M in the range 4 × 10 7 ​ – ​ 10 9 ​ g 4\times 10^{7}\text{--}10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} .
Future measurements of N eff N_{\mathrm{eff}} may constrain the initial fraction for M M as low as 1 ​ g 1\,\mathrm{g} .
If an excess in N eff N_{\mathrm{eff}} is found, PBHs with Dirac neutrinos could provide an explanation of it. 
 Figure 9: An illustration of the maximum ν ¯ e \bar{\nu}_{e} flux allowed by the SK limit for three PBH masses, the shaded region being excluded, from Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a .
For M ≲ M ∗ M\lesssim M_{*}  , the lower (upper) curves represent primary (secondary) components and the thick curves denote their sum.
 M ≳ M ∗ M\gtrsim M_{*} holes emit primary neutrinos only. 
 Evaporating PBHs should produce any other particles predicted in theories beyond the Standard Model.
The number of PBHs is therefore limited by both the abundance of stable massive particles Green 1999 or the decay of long-lived ones Khlopov et al. 2006 .
In supersymmetry or supergravity, the lightest supersymmetric particle (LSP) is stable and becomes a candidate for the DM.
If LSPs are produced by the evaporation of PBHs, in order not to exceed the observed CDM density at present, one obtains the upper bound Lemoine 2000 : 
 β ′ ( M ) ≲ 10 − 18 ( M 10 11 ​ g ) − 1 / 2 ( m LSP 100 ​ GeV ) − 1 ( M < 10 11 ( m LSP / 100 GeV ) − 1 g ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)\lesssim 10^{-18}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{11}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-1/2}\,\left(\frac{m_{\mathrm{LSP}}}{100\,\mathrm{GeV}}\right)^{-1}\quad(M<10^{11}\,(m_{\mathrm{LSP}}/100\,\mathrm{GeV})^{-1}\,\mathrm{g})\,. 
 (49) 
 This constraint is shown in Fig.  4 but depends on the mass of the LSP and is therefore subject to considerable uncertainty Kohri et al. 2006 ; Kawasaki et al. 2008 .
In addition, unstable particles such as the gravitino or neutralino might be produced by evaporating PBHs.
The decay of these particles into lighter ones also affects BBN Kawasaki et al. 2005b ; Kawasaki et al. 2005a and this gives another constraint Khlopov et al. 2006 : 
 β ′ ( M ) ≲ 5 × 10 − 19 ( M 10 9 ​ g ) − 1 / 2 ( Y PBH 10 − 14 ) ( x ϕ 0.006 ) − 1 ( M < 10 9 g ) , \beta^{\prime}(M)\lesssim 5\times 10^{-19}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{9}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-1/2}\,\left(\frac{Y_{\mathrm{PBH}}}{10^{-14}}\right)\,\left(\frac{x_{\phi}}{0.006}\right)^{-1}\quad(M<10^{9}\,\mathrm{g})\,, 
 (50) 
 where Y PBH Y_{\mathrm{PBH}} is the PBH number density to entropy density ratio and x ϕ x_{\phi} is the fraction of the luminosity going into quasi-stable massive particles, both being normalised to reasonable values.
This limit is not shown explicitly in Fig.  4 but it has a similar form to Eq. ( 49 ). 
 PBHs could dominate the density of the Universe before they evaporate and particles emitted by them might then contribute to the DM or baryon asymmetry  Fujita et al. 2014 ; Lennon et al. 2018 ; Morrison et al. 2019 .
Recently Auffinger et al.   Auffinger et al. 2021 have considered the emission of warm DM candidates by PBHs in the range 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 9 ​ g 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} .
If such particles provide the DM, this could inhibit structure formation, which gives an upper limit on β \beta in this mass range.
The limit is weakened for increasing particle spin but PBH domination is ruled out for spin values up to 2.
Hooper and Krnjaic  Hooper and Krnjaic 2021 have suggested that the cosmic baryon asymmetry may have been generated by PBH emission of GUT Higgs or gauge bosons.
The PBHs evaporate after the EW phase transition and thereby evade the sphaleron wash-out encountered in the usual GUT baryogenesis model.
They identify a range of scenarios in which this could work for M < 10 8 ​ g M<10^{8}\,\mathrm{g} . 
 II.4.3 Reionisation and 21 cm signature 
 The Planck 2018 results give the optical depth as τ ∼ 0.05 \tau\sim 0.05 Aghanim et al. 2020a for CMB photons emitted from the last scattering surface and observations of Gunn–Peterson absorption troughs imply that reionisation of the Universe occurred at z ∼ 6 z\sim 6   Fan et al. 2006 .
Thus PBHs cannot be so numerous that they reionise the Universe earlier than z ∼ 6 z\sim 6 .
In principle, this leads to a constraint on PBHs with 
 M ≥ M ∗ ​ ( t dec t 0 ) 1 / 3 ​ ( f ⁡ ( M ) f ∗ ) 1 / 3 ≈ 2 × 10 13 ​ g . M\geq M_{*}\,\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{dec}}}{t_{0}}\right)^{1/3}\,\left(\frac{f(M)}{f_{*}}\right)^{1/3}\approx 2\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}\,. 
 (51) 
 On the other hand, Belotsky et al. have attributed the reionisation of the pregalactic medium to evaporating PBHs.
They show that PBHs with a monochromatic mass distribution around 5 × 10 16 ​ g 5\times 10^{16}\,\mathrm{g} could ensure this Belotsky and Kirillov 2015 .
They also argue that their reionisation effect and DM contribution can be simultaneously enhanced with an extended mass distribution around this mass Belotsky et al. 2017 .
More recently, Cang et al.   Cang et al. have studied how Hawking radiation from PBHs in the 10 15 ​ – ​ 10 17 ​ g 10^{15}\text{--}10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} range with various mass spectra can change the cosmic recombination history by ionizing and heating the intergalactic medium, this modifying the CMB anisotropies.
They find that future experiments can improve current Planck bounds by about two orders of magnitudes.
All monochromatic distributions above 4 × 10 16 ​ g 4\times 10^{16}\,\mathrm{g} that are allowed by current CMB data could be excluded. 
 An increase in the ionisation of the intergalactic medium would also produce a 21 cm signature.
Mack and Wesley Mack and Wesley have argued that future observations of 21 cm radiation from high redshift neutral hydrogen could place important constraints on PBHs in the mass range 5 × 10 13 ​ g < M < 10 17 ​ g 5\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} , essentially because photons emitted from PBHs during 30 < z < 300 30<z<300 peak in the energy range in which the intergalactic medium has low optical depth.
Any process which heats the intergalactic medium in this period will produce a signal but the ionising flux of photons, electrons and positrons from PBHs would generate a distinctive feature in the 21 cm brightness temperature.
PBHs with 5 × 10 13 ​ g < M < 10 14 ​ g 5\times 10^{13}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} evaporate in 30 < z < 90 30<z<90 and would raise the 21 cm brightness temperature, thereby reducing the absorption seen against the CMB.
PBHs with M ∼ 10 14 ​ g M\sim 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} would raise the spin temperature above the CMB, so that the 21 cm line appears in emission rather than absorption.
PBHs with 10 14 ​ g < M < 10 17 ​ g 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} would have a less pronounced effect.
The Mack–Wesley limit therefore bottoms out at around 10 14 ​ g 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} and has the form 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < 3 × 10 − 29 ​ ( M 10 14 ​ g ) 7 / 2 ( M > 10 14 ​ g ) , \beta^{\prime}(M)<3\times 10^{-29}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{14}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{7/2}\quad(M>10^{14}\,\mathrm{g})\,, 
 (52) 
 which is well below the γ \gamma -ray background limit.
Recently EDGES (Experiment to Detect the Global Epoch of Reionization Signature) reported the detection of a 21 cm absorption signal stronger than astrophysical expectations.
Clark et al. Clark et al. 2018 have inferred a constraint on any energy injection into the intergalactic medium by evaporating PBHs because the heating of the neutral hydrogen gas would weaken the 21 cm absorption signal.
For e ​ γ ​ γ e\gamma\gamma final states they find strong 21 cm bounds that can be more stringent than the current extragalactic diffuse photon bounds.
However, none of these reionisation or 21 cm limits is shown in Fig.  4 because of various astrophysical uncertainties. 
 II.4.4 Galactic electrons and positrons 
 The evaporation of PBHs with M > 10 17 ​ g M>10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} is expected to inject sub-GeV electrons and positrons into the Galaxy.
These particles are shielded by the solar magnetic field for Earth-bound detectors, but not for Voyager 1, which is now beyond the heliopause.
Boudaud and Cirelli Boudaud and Cirelli 2019 use its data to constrain the number of PBHs in the Galaxy and, as indicated in Fig.  4 , find β ′ < 10 − 20 \beta^{\prime}<10^{-20} for M < 10 16 ​ g M<10^{16}\,\mathrm{g} for one particular model.
Their limits are based on local Galactic measurements and thus complement those derived from cosmological observations.
Dutta et al.   Dutta et al. 2021 point out that mildly relativistic e ± from PBHs in the mass range 10 15 ​ – ​ 10 17 ​ g 10^{15}\text{--}10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} generate radio emission through inverse-scattering on CMB photons and suggest that SKA will be able to detect such emission from the nearby ultra-faint dwarf galaxies, Segue I and Ursa Major II, as well as the globular cluster ω \omega -Cen and the Coma cluster. 
 Kim  Kim obtains a constraint by noting that fast electrons from evaporating PBHs heat up the interstellar medium through Coulomb interactions.
Using observations of the dwarf galaxy Leo T, he obtains an upper bound for M < 10 17 ​ g M<10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} which is stronger than either the extragalactic or Galactic γ \gamma -ray background limit.
This limit is shown by the “Leo T” line in Fig.  4 .
Laha et al.   Laha et al. have re-analysed this effect and find that the limit from Leo T is weaker than claimed by Kim but that it is strengthened if the PBHs are spinning.
High-energy particles from PBH evaporations would emit radio synchrotron radiation in the presence of a strong magnetic field, so Chan and Lee  Chan and Lee 2020 use radio data from the Galactic centre to constrain the number of PBHs there.
Using three different mass distributions (monochromatic, lognormal and power-law), they show that PBHs can provide only a very small fraction of DM in the mass range below 10 17 ​ g 10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} .
This limit is shown by the “GC radio” line in Fig.  4 . 
 II.4.5 Galactic antiprotons 
 Since the ratio of antiprotons to protons in cosmic rays is less than 10 − 4 10^{-4} over the energy range 100 ​ MeV ​ – ​ 10 ​ GeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV}\text{--}10\,\mathrm{GeV} , whereas PBHs should produce them in equal numbers, PBHs could only contribute appreciably to the antiprotons Turner 1982 ; Kiraly et al. 1981 .
It is usually assumed that the observed antiprotons are secondary particles, produced by spallation of the interstellar medium by primary cosmic rays.
However, the spectrum of secondary antiprotons should show a steep cut-off at kinetic energies below 2 ​ GeV 2\,\mathrm{GeV} , whereas the spectrum of PBH antiprotons should continue down to 0.2 ​ GeV 0.2\,\mathrm{GeV} .
Also any primary antiproton fraction should tend to 0.5 0.5 at low energies.
Both these features provide a distinctive signature of any PBH contribution. 
 The black hole temperature must be much larger than 1 ​ GeV 1\,\mathrm{GeV} to generate antiprotons, so the local cosmic ray flux from PBHs should be dominated by the ones just entering their explosive phase at the present epoch.
Such PBHs should be clustered inside our halo, so any charged particles emitted will have their flux enhanced relative to the extragalactic spectra by a factor ζ \zeta which depends upon the halo concentration factor and the time for which particles are trapped inside the halo by the Galactic magnetic field.
This time is rather uncertain and also energy-dependent.
At 100 ​ MeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV} one expects roughly ζ ∼ 10 3 \zeta\sim 10^{3} for electrons or positrons and ζ ∼ 10 4 \zeta\sim 10^{4} for protons or antiprotons Carr and MacGibbon 1998 . 
 MacGibbon and Carr MacGibbon and Carr 1991 originally calculated the PBH density required to explain the interstellar antiproton flux at 1 ​ GeV 1\,\mathrm{GeV} and found a value somewhat larger than the density associated with the γ \gamma -ray background limit.
After the BESS balloon experiment measured the antiproton flux below 0.5 ​ GeV 0.5\,\mathrm{GeV} Yoshimura et al. 1995 , Maki et al. Maki et al. 1996 tried to fit this in the PBH scenario by using Monte Carlo simulations of cosmic ray propagation.
They found that the local antiproton flux would be mainly due to PBHs exploding within a few kpc and used the observational data to infer a limit on the local PBH explosion rate of ℛ < 0.017 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 \mathcal{R}<0.017\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} .
A later attempt to fit the antiproton data came from Barrau et al. Barrau et al. 2003b , who concluded that PBHs with β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) ≈ 5 × 10 − 28 \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})\approx 5\times 10^{-28} would be numerous enough to explain the observations by BESS95 Yoshimura et al. 1995 , BESS98 Orito et al. 2000 , CAPRICE Boezio et al. 2001 and AMS Jacholkowska 2007 .
However, this was based on the assumption that the PBHs have a spherically symmetric isothermal profile with a core radius of 3.5 ​ kpc 3.5\,\mathrm{kpc} .
A different clustering assumption would lead to a different constraint on β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) \beta^{\prime}(M_{*}) . 
 PBHs might also be detected by their antideuteron flux.
Barrau et al. Barrau et al. 2003c argue that AMS (Alpha Magnetic Spectrometer) and GAPS (General AntiParticle Spectrometer) would be able to detect the antideuterons from PBH explosions if their local density were sufficiently large and inferred potential limits β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) < 1.5 × 10 − 26 ​ ( ζ / 10 4 ) − 1 \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})<1.5\times 10^{-26}\,(\zeta/10^{4})^{-1} and 8.2 × 10 − 28 ​ ( ζ / 10 4 ) − 1 8.2\times 10^{-28}\,(\zeta/10^{4})^{-1} .
More recent data come from the BESS Polar-I experiment, which flew over Antarctica in December 2004 Abe et al. 2008 .
The reported antiproton flux lies between that of BESS(95+97) and BESS(2000) and the p ¯ / p \bar{p}/p ratio ( r ≈ 10 − 5 r\approx 10^{-5} ) is similar to that reported by BESS(95+97).
Although the latter indicated an excess flux below 400 ​ MeV 400\,\mathrm{MeV} , this was not found in the BESS Polar-I data.
However, given the magnitude of the error bars, we might still expect a constraint on the local PBH number density similar to that discussed above, in which case the constraint on β ⁡ ( M ∗ ) \beta(M_{*}) becomes 
 β ′ ​ ( M ∗ ) < 3.9 × 10 − 25 ​ ( ζ 10 4 ) − 1 , \beta^{\prime}(M_{*})<3.9\times 10^{-25}\,\left(\frac{\zeta}{10^{4}}\right)^{-1}\,, 
 (53) 
 where we have used Eq. ( 6 ).
For reasonable values of ζ \zeta , this is much weaker than the γ \gamma -ray background limit, but the value of ζ \zeta is anyway very uncertain and so this limit is not shown explicitly in Fig.  4 . 
 II.4.6 Galactic centre radiation 
 Several authors have suggested that the 511 ​ keV 511\,\mathrm{keV} annihilation line radiation from the Galactic centre could result from the positrons emitted by the PBHs there Bugaev and Konishchev 2002 ; Bugaev and Klimai 2009 ; Bambi et al. 2008 , as first suggested by Okele and Rees Okele and Rees 1980 .
Most recently, DeRocco and Graham DeRocco and Graham 2019 and Laha Laha 2019 have constrained 10 16 ​ – ​ 10 17 ​ g 10^{16}\text{--}10^{17}\,\mathrm{g} PBHs from measurements of this line by SPI/INTEGRAL, excluding models in which they constitute all the DM.
Laha et al.   Laha et al. 2020 have also used this data to constrain PBHs radiating primary photons and shown that – depending on their mass function and other astrophysical uncertainties – PBHs smaller than 10 18 ​ g 10^{18}\,\mathrm{g} could contribute less than 1 % of the DM.
In Fig.  4 , the positron annihilation-line and continuum γ \gamma -ray constraints are indicated by the “GC e + e^{+} ” and “GC γ \gamma ” lines, respectively.
The former is particularly interesting if the PBHs are spinning Dasgupta et al. 2020 .
In this case, the limit is stronger in the higher mass range and stronger than the diffuse γ \gamma -ray limit for M M exceeding a few times 10 16 ​ g 10^{16}\,\mathrm{g} .
However, it should be stressed that Galactic constraints depend on the assumed radial profile, as well as the local DM density and propagation uncertainties, whereas the extragalactic bounds are free from these uncertainties. 
 II.5 PBH explosions 
 The extragalactic γ \gamma -ray background limit implies that the PBH explosion rate ℛ \mathcal{R} could be at most 10 − 6 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 10^{-6}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} if the PBHs are uniformly distributed or 10 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 10\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} if they are clustered inside galactic halos Page and Hawking 1976 .
The latter figure might be compared to the Lehoucq et al. Galactic γ \gamma -ray limit of 0.06 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 0.06\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} Lehoucq et al. 2009 and the Maki et al. antiproton limit of 0.02 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 0.02\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} Maki et al. 1996 .
We now compare these limits to the direct observational constraints on the explosion rate.
These come both from γ \gamma -ray bursts and high-energy cosmic ray showers. 
 II.5.1 Gamma-ray bursts and photosphere effects 
 In the Standard Model of particle physics, where the number of elementary particle species never exceeds around 100 100 , it has been appreciated for a long time that the likelihood of detecting the final explosive phase of PBH evaporations is very low Semikoz 1994 .
However, the physics of the QCD phase transition is still uncertain and the prospects of detecting explosions would be improved in less conventional particle physics models  Porter and Weekes 1979 .
For example, in a Hagedorn-type picture Carter et al. 1976 , where the number of particle species exponentiates at the quark-hadron temperature, the upper limit on ℛ \mathcal{R} is reduced to 0.05 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 0.05\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} Fichtel et al. 1994 , which is comparable to the antiproton limit. 
 Even without the Hagedorn effect, something dramatic may occur at the QCD temperature since the number of species emitted increases substantially  Halzen et al. 1991 .
For this reason, Cline and colleagues have long argued that the formation of a fireball at the QCD temperature could explain some of the short period γ \gamma -ray bursts (i.e. those with duration less than 100 ​ ms 100\,\mathrm{ms} ) Cline and Hong 1992 ; Cline and Hong 1996 ; Cline et al. 1997 .
In Ref.  Cline et al. 2003 they claim to find 42 42 BATSE candidates of this kind and the fact that their distribution matches the spiral arms suggests that they are Galactic.
In Ref.  Cline et al. 2005 they report a class of short-period KONUS bursts which has a much harder spectrum than usual and identify these with exploding PBHs.
In Ref.  Cline et al. 2006 they find a further 8 8 candidates in the Swift data.
Overall they claim that the BATSE, KONUS and Swift data correspond to a 4.5 ​ σ 4.5\sigma effect and that several events exhibit the time structure expected of PBH evaporations Cline and Otwinowski .
One distinctive feature of γ \gamma -ray bursts generated by PBH explosions would be a temporal delay between the high and low energy pulses, an effect which might be detectable by Fermi LAT Ukwatta et al. 2009 .
Multimessenger techniques of searching for signals from evaporating PBHs are discussed in Ref.  Petkov et al. 2018 . 
 It is clearly important to understand how likely PBHs are to resemble γ \gamma -ray bursts from a theoretical perspective.
The usual assumption that there is no interaction between emitted particles Oliensis and Hill 1984 was refuted by Heckler Heckler 1997a , who claimed that QED (Quantum Electrodynamics) interactions could produce an optically thick photosphere once the black hole temperature exceeds T BH = 45 ​ GeV T_{\mathrm{BH}}=45\,\mathrm{GeV} .
He proposed that a similar effect may operate at the lower temperature T BH ≈ 200 ​ MeV T_{\mathrm{BH}}\approx 200\,\mathrm{MeV} due to QCD effects Heckler 1997b .
Variants of these models and their astrophysical implications have been studied by various authors Cline et al. 1999 ; Kapusta 2001 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2002 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2003 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2006 . 
 MacGibbon et al. MacGibbon et al. 2008 have identified a number of physical and geometrical effects which may invalidate these claims.
First, the particles must be causally connected in order to interact and this means that the standard cross-sections are reduced (viz. the particles are created at a finite time and do not go back to the infinite past).
Second, because of the Landau–Pomeranchuk–Migdal effect, a scattered particle requires a minimum distance to complete each bremsstrahlung interaction, with the consequence that there is unlikely to be more than one complete interaction per particle near the black hole.
They conclude that the emitted particles do not interact sufficiently to form a QED photosphere and that the conditions for QCD photosphere formation could only be temporarily satisfied (if at all) when the black hole temperature is of order Λ QCD \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}  .
Even in this case, the strong damping of the Hawking production of QCD particles around this threshold may suffice to suppress it.
In any case, they claim that no QCD photosphere persists once the black hole temperature climbs above Λ QCD \Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}  . 
 A rather different way of producing a γ \gamma -ray burst is to assume that the outgoing charged particles form a plasma due to turbulent magnetic field effects at sufficiently high temperatures Belyanin et al. 1996 .
However, MacGibbon et al. argue that this too is implausible.
They conclude that the observational signatures of a cosmic or Galactic halo background of PBHs or an individual high-temperature black hole remain essentially those of the standard Hawking model, with little change to the detection probability.
Nevertheless, perhaps the best strategy is to accept that our understanding of such effects is incomplete and focus on the empirical aspects of the γ \gamma -ray burst observations. 
 II.5.2 High energy cosmic rays showers 
 At much higher energies, several groups have looked for 1 ​ – ​ 100 ​ TeV 1\text{--}100\,\mathrm{TeV} photons from PBH explosions using cosmic ray detectors.
However, in this case, the constraints are also strongly dependent on the theoretical model Bugaev et al. 2007 .
In the Standard Model the upper limits on the explosion rate are 5 × 10 8 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 5\times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} from the CYGNUS array Alexandreas et al. 1993 , 8 × 10 6 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 8\times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} from the Tibet array Amenomori et al. 1996 , 1 × 10 6 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 1\times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} from the Whipple Cherenkov telescope Linton et al. 2006 , and 8 × 10 8 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 8\times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} from the Andyrchy array Petkov et al. 2008a .
These limits correct for the effects of burst duration and array “dead time.”
Such limits are far weaker than the ones associated with observations at 100 ​ MeV 100\,\mathrm{MeV} .
They would be even weaker in the QED photosphere model advocated by Heckler, since there are then far fewer TeV particles Petkov et al. 2008b .
For example, Bugaev et al. Bugaev et al. have used Andyrchy data to obtain an upper limit of 1 × 10 9 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 1\times 10^{9}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} in the Daghigh–Kapusta model Kapusta 2001 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2002 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2003 ; Daghigh and Kapusta 2006 and 5 × 10 9 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 5\times 10^{9}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} in the Heckler model Heckler 1997a .
Because of the uncertainties, we do not show any of these limits in Fig.  4 . 
 The most recent constraints on PBH explosions come from observations of the High Altitude Water Cherenkov Observatory, which is sensitive to γ \gamma -rays with energies of 300 ​ GeV 300\,\mathrm{GeV} to 100 ​ TeV 100\,\mathrm{TeV} .
With its large instantaneous field-of-view of 2 2 steradians and a duty cycle over 95 %, the observatory is well suited to perform an all-sky search for PBH bursts and Albert et al. Albert et al. 2020 have obtained an upper limit on the local PBH burst rate density of 3400 ​ pc − 3 ​ yr − 1 3400\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} at the 99 % confidence level, far stronger than any existing electromagnetic limit. 
 II.6 More speculative effects 
 II.6.1 Higgs instability 
 Evaporating PBHs can act as seeds for a cosmological phase transition.
The analysis of this problem was pioneered by Hiscock Hiscock 1987 , who assumed that the black hole mass remains constant throughout the transition.
The more general case, without this assumption, was studied by Gregory et al.   Gregory et al. 2014 , who concluded that small PBHs can catalyze a phase transition.
This argument was applied to the stability of the Higgs vacuum in Refs.  Burda et al. 2015a ; Burda et al. 2015b ; Burda et al. 2016 , where it was shown that a PBH with mass up to ∼ 10 9 ​ M Pl \sim 10^{9}\,M_{\mathrm{Pl}} may destabilize the vacuum before evaporating.
The thermal effects of the Hawking radiation of such PBHs has been studied by a number of authors Mukaida and Yamada 2017 ; Kohri and Matsui 2018 ; Hayashi et al. 2020 but with conflicting conclusions due to different interpretations and different choices of the quantum state around the black hole.
In principle, one can constrain the abundance of such small PBHs by percolation analysis of vacuum decay  Dai et al. 2020 .
For example, Kohri and Matsui Kohri and Matsui 2018 have obtained an upper bound, 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < 10 − 21 ​ ( M 10 9 ​ g ) 3 / 2 ( M < M ∗ ) . \beta^{\prime}(M)<10^{-21}\,\left(\frac{M}{10^{9}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{3/2}\quad(M<M_{*})\,. 
 (54) 
 However, we cannot draw a definite conclusion without a proper formulation and understanding of quantum tunneling in the presence of gravity, so this limit is not included in Fig.  4 . 
 II.6.2 Planck mass relic constraints 
 If PBH evaporations leave stable Planck-mass relics, these might also contribute to the DM.
This was first pointed out by MacGibbon MacGibbon 1987 and has subsequently been explored in the context of inflationary scenarios by numerous authors Barrow et al. 1992 ; Carr et al. 1994 ; Green and Liddle 1997 ; Alexeyev et al. 2002 ; Chen and Adler 2003 ; Barrau et al. 2004 ; Chen 2005 ; Nozari and Mehdipour 2005 .
If the relics have a mass κ ​ M Pl \kappa\,M_{\mathrm{Pl}} and reheating occurs at a temperature T R T_{\mathrm{R}}  , this also being the maximum temperature after inflation if reheating is rapid, then the requirement that they have less than the critical density today implies Carr et al. 1994 
 β ′ ​ ( M ) < 8 × 10 − 28 ​ κ − 1 ​ ( M M Pl ) 3 / 2 \beta^{\prime}(M)<8\times 10^{-28}\,\kappa^{-1}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\right)^{3/2} 
 (55) 
 for the mass range 
 ( T Pl T R ) 2 < M M Pl < 10 11 ​ κ 2 / 5 . \left(\frac{T_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{T_{\mathrm{R}}}\right)^{2}<\frac{M}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}<10^{11}\,\kappa^{2/5}\,. 
 (56) 
 We would now require the density to be less than Ω CDM ≈ 0.25 \Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}\approx 0.25 , which strengthens the original limit by a factor of 4 4 .
The lower mass limit arises because PBHs generated before reheating are diluted exponentially.
The upper mass limit arises because PBHs larger than this dominate the total density before they evaporate, in which case the final cosmological baryon-to-photon ratio is determined by the baryon-asymmetry associated with their emission.
This might be compared to lower limit imposed by the trans-Planckian censorship conjecture Cai and Wang 2020 .
Alexander and Mészáros Alexander and Mészáros have advocated an extended inflationary scenario in which evaporating PBHs naturally generate the DM, the entropy and the baryon asymmetry of the Universe.
This triple coincidence applies providing inflation ends at t ∼ 10 − 23 ​ s t\sim 10^{-23}\,\mathrm{s} , so the PBHs would need to have an initial mass M ∼ 10 6 ​ g M\sim 10^{6}\,\mathrm{g} . 
 It is usually assumed that Planck-mass relics would be undetectable apart from their gravitational effects.
However, Lehmann et al. Lehmann et al. 2019 point out that they may carry electric charge, making them visible to terrestrial detectors.
They evaluate constraints and detection prospects in detail and show that this scenario, if not already ruled out by monopole searches, can be explored within the next decade using planned experimental equipment. 
 Although Planck-mass relics might be a natural consequence of quantum gravity effects, stable PBH relics could in principle be much larger than this.
For example, given a suitable dark sector, Bai and Orlofsky Bai and Orlofsky 2020 argue that PBHs lighter than 10 9 ​ g 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} but above the Planck mass could become stable against evaporation by being charged under a new dark gauged U(1) and becoming near-extremal.
If such relics exist, their mergers would have interesting observational signature in the form of high-energy neutrinos and γ \gamma -rays.
PBH evaporation might also cease as a result of near-extremal rotation, and de Freitas Pacheco and Silk  de Freitas Pacheco and Silk 2020 argue that this might apply for PBHs formed in an early post-inflation matter-dominated epoch during preheating.
If one invokes an early accretion phase, this could naturally lead to asteroid-scale DM. 
 III Constraints on non-evaporated PBHs 
 We next review the various constraints associated with PBHs which are too large to have evaporated completely by now, updating the equivalent discussion in Refs.  Carr et al. 2010a ; Carr et al. 2016a .
All the limits assume that PBHs cluster in galactic halos in the same way as other forms of CDM unless they are too large to reside in halos.
In this section we also assume the PBHs have a monochromatic mass function, in the sense that they span a mass range of at most Δ ​ M ∼ M \Delta M\sim M .
In this case, the fraction f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) of the halo in PBHs is related to β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) by 
 f ( M ) ≡ Ω PBH ​ ( M ) Ω CDM ≈ 3.79 Ω PBH ( M ) = 3.81 × 10 8 β ′ ( M ) ( M M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 , f(M)\equiv\frac{\Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M)}{\Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}}\approx 3.79\,\Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M)=3.81\times 10^{8}\,\beta^{\prime}(M)\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1/2}\,, 
 (57) 
 where we have taken Ω CDM = 0.264 \Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}=0.264 from Ref.  Aghanim et al. 2020b .
For an extended or multi-modal mass function, which we discuss later, one can define the total PBH contribution to the DM, f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} , by integrating over M M and there is then a constraint f PBH < 1 f_{\mathrm{PBH}}<1 .
Our limits on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) are summarised in Fig.  10 , which is an updated version of Fig. 8 of Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a .
The main constraints derive from (partial) PBH evaporations, gravitational lensing, numerous dynamical effects, PBH accretion and gravitational wave observations.
Since Fig.  10 is complicated, the constraints for each of these categories are shown separately in subsequent figures.
We note that there are several limits in most mass ranges. 
 It must be stressed that the constraints have varying degrees of certainty and most come with caveats.
For some, the observations are well understood (e.g. the CMB and gravitational lensing data) but there are uncertainties in the black hole physics.
For others, the observations themselves are not fully understood or depend upon additional astrophysical assumptions;
we will give explicit examples of this below.
Many of the constraints depend on physical parameters which are not indicated explicitly, and some of them depend on various cosmological and astrophysical assumptions.
Sometimes the limit can be assigned a confidence level (CL) but this is often hard to assess and not indicated in the original paper.
Note that some of the limits are extended into the f > 1 f>1 domain, although this is obviously unphysical.
This is because some limits will shift downward as the observational data improve, so it is useful to know their form. 
 Similar figures can be found in numerous other papers;
see, for example, Table 1 of Josan et al. Josan et al. 2009 , Fig. 4 of Mack et al. Mack et al. 2007 , Fig. 9 of Ricotti et al. Ricotti et al. 2008 , Fig. 1 of Capela et al. Capela et al. 2013a , Fig. 1 of Clesse & García-Bellido Clesse and García-Bellido 2017 and Fig. 10 of Sasaki et al. Sasaki et al. 2018 .
However, we believe this is the most comprehensive treatment available to date (i.e. prior to 2021).
We group the limits by type and discuss those within each type in order of increasing mass.
Sometimes we express the limit in terms of an analytic function f max ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{max}}(M) over some mass range but this is not always possible. 
 Figure 10: Constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) from evaporation (red), lensing (magenta), dynamical effects (green), gravitational waves (black), accretion (light blue), CMB distortions (orange), large-scale structure (dark blue) and background effects (grey).
Evaporation limits come from the extragalactic γ \gamma -ray background (EGB), CMB anisotropies (CMB), the Galactic γ \gamma -ray background (GGB) and Voyager-1 e ± e^{\pm} limits (V).
Lensing effects come from microlensing of stars in M31 by Subaru (HSC), in the Magellanic Clouds by MACHO (M) and EROS (E), in the local neighbourhood by Kepler (K), in the Galactic bulge by OGLE (O) and the Icarus event in a cluster of galaxies (I), microlensing of supernovae (SN) and quasars (Q), and millilensing of compact radio sources (RS).
Dynamical limits come from disruption of wide binaries (WB) and globular clusters (GC), heating of stars in the Galactic disk (DH), survival of star clusters in Eridanus II (Eri) and Segue 1 (S1), infalling of halo objects due to dynamical friction (DF), tidal disruption of galaxies (G), and the CMB dipole (CMB).
Accretion limits come from X-ray binaries (XB), CMB anisotropies measured by Planck (PA) and gravitational waves from binary coalescences (GW).
Large-scale structure constraints come from the Lyman- α \alpha forest (Ly α \alpha ) and various other cosmic structures (LSS).
Background constraints come from CMB spectral distortion ( μ \mu ), 2nd order gravitational waves (GW2) and the neutron-to-proton ratio ( n / p n/p ).
The incredulity limit (IL) corresponds to one hole per Hubble volume.
These constraints are broken down into different categories in subsequent figures, these including some less certain limits which are omitted here. 
 III.1 Evaporation constraints 
 For PBHs somewhat larger than M ∗ M_{*}  , one can use the γ \gamma -ray background to constrain the value of f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) .
This can be derived from the constraint on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) derived in Sec.  II.3.1 by using Eq. ( 57 ).
For M > 2 ​ M ∗ M>2M_{*}  , one can neglect the change of mass and the time-integrated spectrum d ​ N γ / d ​ E \mathrm{d}N^{\gamma}/\mathrm{d}E of photons from each PBH is just obtained by multiplying the instantaneous spectrum by the age of the Universe t 0 t_{0}  .
For PBHs of mass M M , the discussion in the appendix of Ref.  Carr et al. 2010a gives 
 d ​ N γ d ​ E ∝ { E 3 ​ M 3 ( E < M − 1 ) E 2 ​ M 2 ​ e − E ​ M ( E > M − 1 ) , \frac{\mathrm{d}N^{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E}\propto\begin{cases}E^{3}\,M^{3}&(E<M^{-1})\\
E^{2}\,M^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-EM}&(E>M^{-1})\,,\end{cases} 
 (58) 
 where we put ℏ = c = G = 1 \hbar=c=G=1 .
This peaks at E ∼ M − 1 E\sim M^{-1} with a value independent of M M .
The number of background photons per unit energy per unit volume from all PBHs is then obtained by integrating over the mass function: 
 ℰ ⁡ ( E ) = ∫ M min M max d ​ M ​ d ​ n d ​ M ​ d ​ N γ d ​ E ​ ( M , E ) , \mathcal{E}(E)=\int_{M_{\mathrm{min}}}^{M_{\mathrm{max}}}\!\mathrm{d}M\,\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}N^{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E}(M,E)\,, 
 (59) 
 where M min M_{\mathrm{min}} and M max M_{\mathrm{max}} specify the mass limits.
For a monochromatic mass function, this gives

E^{2}\,M\,\mathrm{e}^{-EM}&(E>M^{-1})\end{cases} 
 (60) 
 and the associated intensity is 
 I ⁡ ( E ) ≡ E ​ ℰ ​ ( E ) 4 ​ π ∝ f ⁡ ( M ) × { E 4 ​ M 2 ( E < M − 1 ) E 3 ​ M ​ e − E ​ M ( E > M − 1 ) I(E)\equiv\frac{E\,\mathcal{E}(E)}{4\pi}\propto f(M)\times\begin{cases}E^{4}\,M^{2}&(E<M^{-1})\\

 (61) 
 with units s − 1 ​ sr − 1 ​ cm − 2 \mathrm{s}^{-1}\,\mathrm{sr}^{-1}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2} .
This peaks at E ∼ M − 1 E\sim M^{-1} with a value I max ​ ( M ) ∝ f ⁡ ( M ) ​ M − 2 I^{\mathrm{max}}(M)\propto f(M)\,M^{-2} .
The observed extragalactic intensity is I obs ∝ E − ( 1 + ϵ ) ∝ M 1 + ϵ I^{\mathrm{obs}}\propto E^{-(1+\epsilon)}\propto M^{1+\epsilon} where ϵ \epsilon lies between 0.1 0.1 (the value favoured in Ref.  Sreekumar et al. 1998 ) and 0.4 0.4 (the value favoured in Ref.  Strong et al. 2004 ).
Hence requiring I max ​ ( M ) ≤ I obs ​ ( M ) I^{\mathrm{max}}(M)\leq I^{\mathrm{obs}}(M) gives Carr et al. 2010a 
 f ⁡ ( M ) ≲ 2 × 10 − 8 ​ ( M M ∗ ) 3 + ϵ ( M > M ∗ = 5 × 10 14 ​ g ) . f(M)\lesssim 2\times 10^{-8}\,\left(\frac{M}{M_{*}}\right)^{3+\epsilon}\quad(M>M_{*}=5\times 10^{14}\mathrm{g})\,. 
 (62) 
 As expected, this is equivalent to condition ( 33 ), which is represented in Fig.  7 .
We have seen that the Galactic γ \gamma -ray background may give a stronger limit but this depends sensitively on the form of the mass function. 
 PBHs smaller than 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} have evaporated completely and therefore cannot contribute to the DM.
The function f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) is not defined in this range, so the abundance is usually described in terms of the collapse fraction β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) .
Nevertheless, one can still formally relate β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) to f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) using Eq. ( 57 ).
The dominant constraints in Fig.  4 are therefore also represented in Fig.  11 , including the Voyager-1 limits Boudaud and Cirelli 2019 .
However, we do not show constraints which depend on the DM profile in the Galactic centre or dwarf galaxies because these are uncertain. 
 Figure 11: Evaporation constraints from BBN  Carr et al. 2010a , CMB spectral distortions and anisotropies  Acharya and Khatri 2020a ; Chluba et al. 2020 , extragalactic γ \gamma -rays  Carr et al. 2010a , Galactic γ \gamma -rays  Carr et al. 2016b and Voyager-1 e ±   Boudaud and Cirelli 2019 , based on Fig.  4 and Eq. ( 57 ). 
 III.2 Lensing constraints 
 The lensing constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) are summarised in Fig.  12 .
Where possible, we use 95 % CL constraints but one must distinguish between limits based on positive detections and null detections.
Claimed positive detections come from OGLE in the low mass range Niikura et al. 2019a , from MACHO and quasar microlensing Hawkins 1993 in the solar mass range and from millilensing of AGN jets Vedantham et al. 2017 in the high mass range. 
 Figure 12: Lensing constraints from HSC  Smyth et al. 2020 , Kepler  Griest et al. 2013 ; Griest et al. 2014 , OGLE  Niikura et al. 2019a , EROS  Tisserand et al. 2007 , MACHO  Alcock et al. 2001a , Icarus  Oguri et al. 2018 , SNe  Zumalacárregui and Seljak 2018 and radio sources  Wilkinson et al. 2001 , with the disputed GRB constraint  Barnacka et al. 2012 and the original HSC constraint Niikura et al. 2019b shown by broken lines. 
 III.2.1 Femtolensing and picolensing 
 It has been argued that constraints on PBHs with very low mass come from the femtolensing of γ \gamma -ray bursts (GRBs).
Assuming the bursts are at a redshift z ∼ 1 z\sim 1 , early studies Marani et al. 1999 ; Nemiroff et al. 2001 claimed to exclude f = 1 f=1 in the mass range 10 − 16 ​ – ​ 10 − 13 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-16}\text{--}10^{-13}\,M_{\odot} and later work Barnacka et al. 2012 gave a limit which can be approximated as 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < 0.1 ( 5 × 10 16 ​ g < M < 10 19 ​ g ) . f(M)<0.1\quad(5\times 10^{16}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{19}\,\mathrm{g})\,. 
 (63) 
 The femtolensers could either be PBHs or ultra-compact DM minihalos (e.g. made up of QCD axions).
However, Katz et al. Katz et al. 2018 have reviewed this argument, taking into account the extended nature of the source as well as wave-optic effects, and argued that most GRBs are inappropriate for femtolensing searches due to their large sizes.
A small fraction, characterised by fast variability, might be small enough to be useful but many such bursts would be needed to achieve meaningful constraints.
The femtolensing limit ( 63 ) is therefore not shown in Fig.  10 and indicated with a broken line in Fig.  12 .
However, Jung and Kim Jung and Kim 2020 have recently discussed another technique which probes the asteroid mass range, using the lensing parallax of GRBs observed simultaneously by spatially separated detectors. 
 III.2.2 Microlensing of stars 
 Microlensing (ML) observations of stars in the Magellanic Clouds (LMC and SMC) probe the fraction of the Galactic halo in compact objects in the mass range 10 − 7 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-7}\,M_{\odot}<M<10\,M_{\odot} Paczynski 1986 .
The optical depth of the halo towards the LMC and SMC, defined as the probability that any given star is amplified by at least 1.34 1.34 at a given time, is 
 τ L ( SMC ) = 1.4 ​ τ L ( LMC ) = 6.6 × 10 − 7 ​ f ​ ( M ) \tau^{(\mathrm{SMC})}_{\mathrm{L}}=1.4\,\tau^{(\mathrm{LMC})}_{\mathrm{L}}=6.6\times 10^{-7}\,f(M) 
 (64) 
 for the S halo model Alcock et al. 2001b .
Although the initial motivation for ML surveys was to search for brown dwarfs with M < 0.08 ​ M ⊙ M<0.08\,M_{\odot}  , the possibility that the halo is dominated by these objects was soon ruled out by the MACHO experiment.
Indeed, this excluded all the DM being in PBHs over the mass range 0.1 ​ – ​ 0.9 ​ M ⊙ 0.1\text{--}0.9\,M_{\odot} and the upper limit was later extended to 30 ​ M ⊙ 30M_{\odot} Alcock et al. 2001a .
The associated constraint is shown in Fig.  12 .
However, MACHO did observe ML and claimed their 13–17 events were consistent with compact objects of M ∼ 0.5 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 0.5\,M_{\odot} contributing 20 % of the halo mass Alcock et al. 2000 .
This raised the possibility that some of the DM could be PBHs formed at the QCD phase transition Jedamzik 1997 ; Widerin and Schmid ; Jedamzik and Niemeyer 1999 .
Similar claims were later made by the POINT-AGAPE collaboration, who detected six ML events in a survey of M31 Calchi-Novati et al. 2005 .
They argued that this was more than could be expected from self-lensing alone and concluded that 20 % of the halo mass in the direction of M31 could be PBHs in the range 0.5 ​ – ​ 1 ​ M ⊙ 0.5\text{--}1\,M_{\odot}  . 
 The EROS collaboration monitored brighter stars in a wider solid angle and thus obtained more stringent constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) , arguing that some of the MACHO events were due to self-lensing.
Specifically, they excluded 6 × 10 − 8 ​ M ⊙ < M < 15 ​ M ⊙ 6\times 10^{-8}\,M_{\odot}<M<15\,M_{\odot} objects from having f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 Tisserand et al. 2007 .
This gives the 95 % CL limit shown in Fig.  12 , which may be approximated as 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { 1 ( 6 × 10 − 8 ​ M ⊙ < M < 15 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.1 ( 10 − 6 ​ M ⊙ < M < 1 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.04 ( 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ < M < 0.1 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}1&(6\times 10^{-8}\,M_{\odot}<M<15\,M_{\odot})\\
0.1&(10^{-6}\,M_{\odot}<M<1\,M_{\odot})\\
0.04&(10^{-3}\,M_{\odot}<M<0.1\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (65) 
 There are also constraints from the OGLE experiment, which monitored both the Galactic Bulge and Magellanic Clouds.
In particular, OGLE-III data towards the LMC Wyrzykowski et al. 2011a and SMC Wyrzykowski et al. 2011b gave stronger results than indicated above in the high mass range.
Combining SMC constraints from EROS and OGLE-III  Calchi Novati et al. 2013 excludes f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 over the range 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{2}M_{\odot}  , with the limit bottoming out with f ∼ 0.1 f\sim 0.1 at 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-2}\,M_{\odot}  .
We do not include this limit in Fig.  12 because it depends on some unidentified detections being attributed to self-lensing. 
 Recently Niikura et al. Niikura et al. 2019a have used five years of observations of stars in the Galactic bulge by OGLE to constrain the PBH abundance.
The ML light-curves span a distribution of timescales over the range t E ≈ [ 1,300 ] ​ days t_{E}\approx[1,300]\,\mathrm{days} and can be well modeled as being due to ordinary stars or stellar remnants.
However, there are also six ultra-short events with t E ≈ [ 0.1 , 0.3 ] ​ day t_{E}\approx[0.1,0.3]\,\mathrm{day} .
These might be attributed to free-floating planets but they could also be generated by planetary-mass PBHs with f ∼ 0.01 f\sim 0.01 .
On the null hypothesis that the OGLE data contain no PBH events, the 95 % CL upper bound may be approximated as 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { 1 ( 10 − 7 ​ M ⊙ < M < 1 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.1 ( 10 − 6 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.01 ( M ∼ 10 − 4 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}1&(10^{-7}\,M_{\odot}<M<1\,M_{\odot})\\
0.1&(10^{-6}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{-2}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.01&(M\sim 10^{-4}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (66) 
 and this limit is shown in Fig.  12 .
Wyrzykowski and Mandel Wyrzykowski and Mandel 2020 have used Gaia data on the distances and proper motions of non-blended sources to improve the mass estimates in the parallax ML events found in 8 years of OGLE-III observations towards the Galactic Bulge.
They identify 18 18 new events and the derived distribution of masses for the lenses is consistent with a continuous distribution of stellar remnant masses.
However, they also find 8 8 candidates in the theoretically expected mass gap of 2 ​ – ​ 5 ​ M ⊙ 2\text{--}5\,M_{\odot} between neutron stars and black holes.
These might conceivably be PBHs Carr et al. 2021a , although they could also be stellar black holes if they were born with large recoil velocities. 
 The interpretation of the MACHO, EROS and OGLE results is very sensitive to the properties of the Milky Way halo  Green 2017 .
All the above constraints assume a semi-isothermal density sphere but Calcino et al. Calcino et al. 2018 argue that this is inconsistent with the Milky Way rotation curve and does not incorporate uncertainties in the shape of the halo.
With their new model, which is non-spherical and accounts for these features, the ML constraints for LMC weaken for masses around 10 ​ M ⊙ 10\,M_{\odot} but tighten at lower masses.
They claim that a wide PBH mass distribution could also shift the constraint towards smaller masses and that spatial clustering of the PBHs may remove the constraint altogether at M ∼ 1 ​ – ​ 10 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 1\text{--}10\,M_{\odot} since there would be some probability that a given source is not lensed at all.
Hawkins has come to a similar conclusion, arguing that the recent low-mass Galactic halo models would relax the constraints and allow the halo to consist entirely of solar-mass PBHs Hawkins 2015 . 
 Kepler data have considerably improved the limits in the low mass range Griest et al. 2013 ; Griest et al. 2014 , implying 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < 0.3 ( 2 × 10 − 9 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 − 7 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<0.3\qquad(2\times 10^{-9}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{-7}\,M_{\odot})\,. 
 (67) 
 This limit is indicated on Fig.  12 .
Niikura et al. Niikura et al. 2019b have carried out a dense-cadence 7-hour observation of M31 with the Subaru Hyper Suprime-Cam (HSC) to search for ML by PBHs of similar mass in the halos of the Milky Way or M31.
If such PBHs make up a significant fraction of DM, they expect to find many ML events.
However, they identify only a single candidate event, which translates into the most stringent current upper bound on the PBH abundance in the mass range 10 − 11 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 − 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-11}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{-6}\,M_{\odot}  .
The black hole size becomes less than photon wavelength and the Einstein radius becomes smaller than the source size at the lower bound.
However, Smyth et al. Smyth et al. 2020 point out that Niikura et al. underestimate this effect because they assume a source size of a solar radius, whereas the sources are likely to be larger than this.
They find that the HSC constraints are weaker by up to almost three orders of magnitude in some cases, increasing the mass below which PBHs can provide the DM from 10 − 11 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-11}\,M_{\odot} to 10 − 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-10}\,M_{\odot}  .
The Niikura et al. limit is therefore shown by a broken line in Fig.  12 .
Sugiyama et al. Sugiyama et al. 2020 have also studied this effect and point out that, if a denser-cadence monitoring of a sample of white dwarfs over a year were available, then one could probe the wave-optics effect on a ML light-curve and push the PBH constraint down to 10 − 11 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-11}\,M_{\odot}  . 
 Going beyond the local neighbourhood, recent observations of fast transient events in massive galaxy clusters, interpreted as individual stars in giant arcs highly magnified due to caustic crossing, can also be used to constrain PBHs.
For example, Oguri et al. Oguri et al. 2018 find that the lens mass associated with the first event for MACS J1149 must be in the range 0.1 ​ – ​ 4 × 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 0.1\text{--}4\times 10^{3}\,M_{\odot}  .
The derived lens properties are consistent with a ML event produced by such a star contributing to the intra-cluster light but DM models with f > 0.1 f>0.1 in compact objects in the mass range 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{2}\,M_{\odot} would predict too low magnification.
This corresponds to the “Icarus” line in Fig.  12 . 
 III.2.3 Microlensing of supernovae 
 PBHs cause most lines of sight to be demagnified relative to the mean, with a long tail of higher magnifications.
This effect was first applied to type Ia supernovae (SNe) by Metcalf and Silk  Metcalf and Silk 2007 , who inferred constraints on compact objects in the mass range 10 − 2 ​ – ​ 10 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-2}\text{--}10^{10}\,M_{\odot}  .
More recently, Zumalacárregui and Seljak Zumalacárregui and Seljak 2018 have used the lack of lensing of SNe to constrain PBHs, modelling the effects of large-scale structure, allowing for a non-Gaussian model for the intrinsic SNe luminosity distribution and addressing potential systematic errors.
Using current JLA SNe data, they derive a 95 % CL bound f < 0.35 f<0.35 , which rules out PBHs comprising all the DM with 5 ​ σ 5\sigma significance.
The finite size of SNe limits the validity of the results to M > 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ M>10^{-2}\,M_{\odot} but this closes a previously open PBH mass range.
This limit is shown in Fig.  12 . 
 García-Bellido et al. García-Bellido et al. 2018 argue that this limit can be weakened if the PBHs have an extended mass function or are clustered, concluding that the bound on the fraction of DM in PBHs is f < 1.09 ​ ( 1.38 ) f<1.09\,(1.38) for the JLA (Union 2.1) catalogues.
This would be compatible with PBHs providing all the DM in the LIGO band.
However, Zumalacárregui and Seljak dispute this claim, pointing out that part of the discrepancy arises through a (later corrected) error in the radius of the SNe photosphere and arguing that the dependence on mass only enters if the mass function has considerable support in the M < 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ M<10^{-2}M_{\odot} region where the constraints become weaker. 
 III.2.4 Quasar microlensing and millilensing 
 Early studies of the ML of quasars Dalcanton et al. 1994 seemed to exclude all the DM being in objects with 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ < M < 60 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-3}\,M_{\odot}<M<60\,M_{\odot}  .
However, this limit does not apply in the Λ \Lambda CDM picture and so is not shown in Fig.  10 .
At around the same time, Hawkins Hawkins 1993 claimed evidence for a critical density of Jupiter-mass objects from observations of quasar ML and associated these with PBHs formed at the quark-hadron transition.
However, his later analysis yielded a lower density (cf. Ω DM ≈ 0.3 \Omega_{\mathrm{DM}}\approx 0.3 ) and a larger mass of around 1 ​ M ⊙ 1\,M_{\odot} Hawkins 2007 .
The status of this claim remains unclear Zackrisson et al. 2003 .
Studies of quasar ML by Mediavilla et al. initially gave a limit Mediavilla et al. 2009 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < 1 ( 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ < M < 60 ​ M ⊙ ) f(M)<1\quad(10^{-3}\,M_{\odot}<M<60\,M_{\odot}) 
 (68) 
 but they later found positive evidence for ML, this indicating that 20 % of the total mass is in compact objects in the mass range 0.05 ​ – ​ 0.45 ​ M ⊙ 0.05\text{--}0.45\,M_{\odot} Mediavilla et al. 2017 .
Although they attributed this to a normal stellar component, Hawkins has argued that one requires PBHs to explain the observations Hawkins 2020 .
Since it is difficult to express the result of Ref.  Mediavilla et al. 2017 as a quantitative upper bound on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) , we do not include it in Figs.  12 . 
 In a higher mass range, Vedantham et al. Vedantham et al. 2017 claim to have detected long-term radio variability – what they term Symmetric Achromatic Variability – in the light-curves of active galactic nuclei (AGN).
They propose that this arises from gravitational millilensing of features in AGN jets moving relativistically through gravitational lensing caustics created by 10 3 ​ – ​ 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\text{--}10^{6}\,M_{\odot} condensates or black holes within intervening galaxies.
The lower end of this mass range has been inaccessible with previous gravitational lensing observations.
This offers a powerful probe of the cosmological matter distribution on these mass scales, as well as micro-arcsecond resolution of AGN, a factor of 30–100 greater than is possible with Very Long Baseline Interferometry (VLBI).
Banik et al.   Banik et al. 2019 consider the kink-like distortions of razor-thin lensing arcs caused by free-floating black holes along the line-of-sight to AGN observed in the radio.
The null detection of such distortions by VLBI and the Atacama Large Millimeter/submillimeter Array (ALMA) potentially constrains the density of PBHs with M > 10 3 ​ M ⊙ M>10^{3}\,M_{\odot} to be f < 10 − 5 f<10^{-5} . 
 Millilensing of compact radio sources Wilkinson et al. 2001 gives a limit which can be approximated as 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 2 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) − 2 ( M < 10 5 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.06 ( 10 5 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / 4 × 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) 2 ( M > 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/2\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})^{-2}&(M<10^{5}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.06&(10^{5}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{8}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/4\times 10^{8}\,M_{\odot})^{2}&(M>10^{8}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (69) 
 Though weaker than other constraints in this mass range, we include this limit in Fig.  12 .
Hezaveh et al. Hezaveh et al. 2016 have studied substructure in the matter density near galaxies, using ALMA observations of the strong lensing system SDP.81.
They find evidence for a 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 10^{9}\,M_{\odot} subhalo near one of the images and derive constraints on the abundance of DM subhalos down to around 10 7 ​ M ⊙ 10^{7}\,M_{\odot} (the mass of the smallest detected satellites in the Local Group).
There are hints of additional substructure but the results are consistent with the Λ \Lambda CDM prediction.
Although they do not explicitly constrain PBHs, so we do not include their results in Fig.  12 , this suggests f < 0.04 f<0.04 for 10 7 < M / M ⊙ < 10 9 10^{7}<M/M_{\odot}<10^{9} . 
 III.2.5 Microlensing of Mira variables, pulsars and fast radio bursts 
 Finally, we mention a number of other lensing effects which have been discussed in the literature and may eventually constrain the DM fraction in PBHs.
The first two effects have been primarily considered in the context of searching for the black hole remnants of ordinary stars but the mass range could also be relevant for PBHs.
The other effects are specifically relevant to PBHs.
The associated constraints are not shown in Fig.  12 since they are only potential rather than actual limits. 
 Abrams and Takada  Abrams and Takada 2020 suggest monitoring 10 9 10^{9} long-duration ( > 100 ​ days >100\,\mathrm{days} ) ML events in the Galactic bulge using the Large Synoptic Survey Telescope.
This would correspond to black holes larger than 30 ​ M ⊙ 30\,M_{\odot}  , the dominant source of ML for a Salpeter mass function.
Karami et al. Karami et al. 2016 discuss the detection of ML of Mira variables with VLBI.
These stars are sufficiently bright and compact to permit direct imaging using the Very Long Baseline Array (VLBA).
ML by Galactic black holes would be relatively common and features in the associated images would be sufficiently well resolved to fully reconstruct the lens properties, enabling the measurement of mass, distance, and tangential velocity of the lensing object to a precision of better than 15 %.
Future radio ML surveys conducted with upcoming radio telescopes combined with modest improvements in the VLBA could increase the rate of Galactic black hole events to roughly 10 ​ yr − 1 10\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} , sufficient to double the number of known stellar mass black holes in a couple years and determining the distribution functions of stellar mass black holes. 
 Other interesting constraints come from pulsar timing.
Schutz and Liu Schutz and Liu 2017 claim the non-detection of the 3rd order Shapiro time delay as PBHs move around the Galactic halo will soon constrain those with mass in the range 1 ​ – ​ 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 1\text{--}10^{3}\,M_{\odot}  .
Indeed, observations with the Square Kilometre Array (SKA) potentially preclude f f exceeding 0.01 ​ – ​ 0.1 0.01\text{--}0.1 in this mass range.
Munoz et al. Muñoz et al. 2016 have pointed out that similar limits could come form the lensing of fast radio bursts (FRBs).
The potential constraint in this case bottoms out at a mass of around 10 ​ M ⊙ 10\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 0.01 f\sim 0.01 .
Other advances in this context could include studies of interference pattern in the frequency spectrum Katz et al. 2020 and searches for the lensing of mini-FRBs Laha 2020 .
Pulsars are sensitive to acceleration and gravitational redshifts induced by any transiting PBHs and Dror et al. Dror et al. 2019 have studied the sensitivity of pulsar timing arrays (PTAs) to this effect.
They find that the SKA could exclude f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 over the mass range 10 − 12 ​ – ​ 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-12}\text{--}10^{2}\,M_{\odot} and the constraint would be f < 0.01 f<0.01 in the range 10 − 4 ​ – ​ 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-4}\text{--}10^{-2}\,M_{\odot}  .
PTAs can potentially probe a substantially lower density of such objects because of the large effective radius over which they can be observed. 
 Jow et al. Jow et al. 2020 have studied the importance of wave effects for the ML of point-like radio bursts.
The frequency dependence of wave effects breaks degeneracies in the usual geometric optics limit and constructive interference leads to a generic increase in the ML cross-section in the wave-optics regime compared to the geometric-optics regime.
For example, fast radio bursts (FRBs) and pulsars will be lensed in the full wave-optics regime by isolated masses in the range 10 − 7 ​ – ​ 10 − 4 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-7}\text{--}10^{-4}\,M_{\odot}  , with the interference pattern itself determining the lens mass. 
 Bai and Orlofsky Bai and Orlofsky 2019 point out that X-ray pulsars with higher photon energies and smaller sizes are good sources for ML in the 10 − 16 ​ – ​ 10 − 11 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-16}\text{--}10^{-11}\,M_{\odot} window.
Among existing X-ray pulsars, the most promising is SMC X-1 because of its apparent brightness and large distance.
Their analysis of existing data from the RXTE telescope suggests that f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 in PBHs is close to being excluded in the asteroid mass range.
Future observations of this source by X-ray telescopes with larger effective areas could close this mass window entirely. 
 III.3 Dynamical constraints 
 A variety of dynamical constraints come into play at higher mass scales and many of them involve the destruction of various astronomical objects by the passage of nearby PBHs.
As shown by Carr and Sakellariadou Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 , if the PBHs have density ρ \rho and velocity dispersion V V , while the objects have mass M c M_{c}  , radius R c R_{c}  , velocity dispersion V c V_{c} and survival time t L t_{L}  , then the constraint has the general form: 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { M c ​ V / ( G ​ M ​ ρ ​ t L ​ R c ) ( M < M c ​ ( V / V c ) ) M c / ( ρ ​ V c ​ t L ​ R c 2 ) ( M c ​ ( V / V c ) < M < M c ​ ( V / V c ) 3 ) M ​ V c 2 / ( ρ ​ R c 2 ​ V 3 ​ t L ) ​ exp ⁡ [ ( M / M c ) ​ ( V c / V ) 3 ] ( M > M c ​ ( V / V c ) 3 ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}M_{c}\,V/(G\,M\,\rho\,t_{L}\,R_{c})&(M<M_{c}\,(V/V_{c}))\\
M_{c}/(\rho\,V_{c}\,t_{L}\,R_{c}^{2})&(M_{c}\,(V/V_{c})<M<M_{c}\,(V/V_{c})^{3})\\
M\,V_{c}^{2}/(\rho\,R_{c}^{2}\,V^{3}\,t_{L})\,\exp[(M/M_{c})\,(V_{c}/V)^{3}]&(M>M_{c}\,(V/V_{c})^{3})\,.\end{cases} 
 (70) 
 The three limits correspond to disruption by multiple encounters, one-off encounters and non-impulsive encounters, respectively, and we assume V > V c V>V_{c}  .
The fraction is thus constrained over the mass range 
 M c ​ V G ​ ρ DM ​ t L ​ R c < M < M c ​ ( V V c ) 3 , \frac{M_{c}\,V}{G\,\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}\,t_{L}\,R_{c}}<M<M_{c}\,\left(\frac{V}{V_{c}}\right)^{3}\,, 
 (71) 
 where ρ DM \rho_{\mathrm{DM}} is the DM
density.
The mass limits correspond to the values of M M for which one would have f = 1 f=1 , so PBHs could provide all the DM only if M M is below the lower limit or above the higher limit.
Note that various numerical factors are omitted in these expressions. 
 Constraint ( 70 ) applies providing there is at least one PBH within the relevant environment, be that a galactic halo, a cluster of galaxies or the Universe itself.
This is termed the “incredulity limit” and for an environment of mass M E M_{\mathrm{E}}   it corresponds to the condition 
 f ⁡ ( M ) > f IL ≡ ( M / M E ) , f(M)>f_{\mathrm{IL}}\equiv(M/M_{\mathrm{E}})\,, 
 (72) 
 where M E M_{\mathrm{E}} is around 3 × 10 12 ​ M ⊙ 3\times 10^{12}\,M_{\odot} for halos, 10 14 ​ M ⊙ 10^{14}\,M_{\odot} for clusters and 10 22 ​ M ⊙ 10^{22}\,M_{\odot} for the Universe.
However, this is only relevant if the exponential upper cut-off in Eq. ( 70 ) lies to the right of the incredulity limit and this applies for M E < ρ DM ​ t L ​ t c 2 ​ V 3 M_{\mathrm{E}}<\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}\,t_{L}\,t_{c}^{2}\,V^{3} .
Otherwise Eq. ( 70 ) is unaffected. 
 The dynamical limits are summarised in Fig.  13 and most of them apply to objects in galactic halos.
In this case, the upper limit on the mass of the objects which provide the DM lies in the range 10 ​ – ​ 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10\text{--}10^{6}\,M_{\odot}  , so this is particularly relevant for intermediate mass objects.
The limits would also apply to forms of DM other than PBHs.
For example, Raidal et al. have suggested that large primordial curvature fluctuations could collapse into horizonless exotic compact objects Raidal et al. 2018 .
The DM might also be clusters of PBHs Carr and Lacey 1987 ; Belotsky et al. 2015 and this is suggested by the claim by Dokuchaev et al. Dokuchaev et al. 2005 and Chisholm Chisholm 2006 that PBHs could form in tight clusters, giving a local overdensity well in excess of that provided by the halo concentration alone.
Overdense regions might also form ultra-compact minihalos (UCMHs), with the UCMH constraints on the density perturbations being stronger than the PBH constraints in some mass ranges Bringmann et al. 2012 .
