 Figure 13: Dynamical constraints from Segue I  Koushiappas and Loeb 2017 , Eridanus II  Zoutendijk et al. 2020 , wide binaries  Monroy-Rodríguez and Allen 2014 and, based on Ref.  Carr and Silk 2018 , from globular clusters, disk heating, dynamical friction, galaxy disruption in clusters, the CMB dipole and the incredulity limit.
The disputed white dwarf Capela et al. 2013b and neutron star   Capela et al. 2013a and supernovae  Graham et al. 2015 limits are shown by broken lines. 
 III.3.1 Terrestrial and solar system encounters 
 The effects of PBH collisions on the Earth have been a subject of long-standing interest, ever since Ryan suggested this could explain the Tunguska event  Jackson and Ryan 1973 .
For example, Khriplovich et al. Khriplovich et al. have examined whether terrestrial collisions could be detected acoustically.
The seismic energy deposited by a 10 15 ​ g 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} PBH is comparable to a 4th magnitude earthquake but the time between collisions is very long ( 10 7 ​ yr 10^{7}\,\mathrm{yr} ) even if such PBHs provide all the DM.
Encounters with larger (Earth-threatening) PBHs are even rarer but the rate at which non-collisional close encounters could be detected by seismic activity is two orders of magnitude larger.
Luo et al. Luo et al. 2012 have proposed searching for PBH transits through or near the Earth by studying the seismic waves generated in Earth’s interior.
These would have two signatures: the almost simultaneous arrival of the waves everywhere on the Earth’s surface and the excitation of unusual spheroidal modes with a characteristic frequency-spacing.
We do not show any of these constraints in Fig.  13 , because they are only important for masses so low that the PBHs would have evaporated.
However, the equivalent limits for other forms of DM (e.g. snowballs) are shown in Ref.  Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 . 
 Scholtz and Unwin Scholtz and Unwin 2020 propose that a planetary-mass PBH could be captured by the Solar System and thereby explain Planet 9.
This possibility is discussed further by Siraj and Loeb  Siraj and Loeb 2020 , while Arbey and Auffinger  Arbey and Auffinger suggest that a fly-by spacecraft might even measure its Hawking radiation in the radio band.
Looking further to the future, gravitational-wave observatories in space might detect the dynamical effects of PBHs.
For example, LISA could detect PBHs in the mass range 10 14 ​ – ​ 10 20 ​ g 10^{14}\text{--}10^{20}\,\mathrm{g} by measuring the gravitational impulse induced by any nearby ones  Adams and Bloom ; Seto and Cooray 2004 . 
 III.3.2 Stars, neutron stars and white dwarfs 
 Zhilyaev Zhilyaev has suggested that PBHs could be captured by stars and eventually collide with them.
For those in the mass range 10 − 3 ​ – ​ 10 − 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-3}\text{--}10^{-2}\,M_{\odot}  , such collisions could produce the small class of γ \gamma -ray bursts emitting thermal bremsstrahlung in the range 20 ​ – ​ 300 ​ keV 20\text{--}300\,\mathrm{keV} .
Roncadelli et al.   Roncadelli et al. have considered halo PBHs being captured and swallowed by stars in the Galactic disc.
The stars would eventually be accreted by the holes, producing radiation and a population of subsolar black holes which could only be of primordial origin.
Every disc star would contain such a black hole if the DM were in PBHs smaller than 3 × 10 26 ​ g 3\times 10^{26}\,\mathrm{g} .
Since the time-scale on which a star captures a PBH scales as τ cap ∝ n PBH − 1 ∝ M ​ f ​ ( M ) − 1 \tau_{\mathrm{cap}}\propto n_{\mathrm{PBH}}^{-1}\propto M\,f(M)^{-1} , requiring this to exceed the age of the Galactic disc implies  Carr et al. 2010a 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < M / 3 × 10 26 ​ g , f(M)<M/3\times 10^{26}\,\mathrm{g}\,, 
 (73) 
 where we have used the normalisation of Roncadelli et al. This corresponds to a lower limit on the mass of objects providing the DM.
A similar analysis of the collisions of PBHs with main-sequence stars, red-giant cores, white dwarfs and neutron stars by Abramowicz et al. Abramowicz et al. 2009 gives a lower limit of 10 20 ​ g 10^{20}\,\mathrm{g} .
Capela et al. have also considered the capture of PBHs by white dwarfs Capela et al. 2013b and neutron stars Capela et al. 2013a and find that the survival of these objects implies a limit which Ref.  Carr et al. 2016a approximates as 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < M 4.7 × 10 24 ​ g ​ ( 1 − exp ⁡ [ − M 2.9 × 10 23 ​ g ] ) − 1 ( 2.5 × 10 18 ​ g < M < 10 25 ​ g ) . f(M)<\frac{M}{4.7\times 10^{24}\,\mathrm{g}}\,\left(1-\exp\left[-\frac{M}{2.9\times 10^{23}\,\mathrm{g}}\right]\right)^{-1}\quad\left(2.5\times 10^{18}\mathrm{g}<M<10^{25}\,\mathrm{g}\right)\,. 
 (74) 
 This is similar to Eq. ( 73 ) at the high-mass end but flattens off at intermediate masses.
The lower cut-off at 2 × 10 18 ​ g 2\times 10^{18}\,\mathrm{g} arises because PBHs lighter than this will not have time to consume the neutron stars during the age of the Universe.
This explains the form of the ‘neutron star’ limit in Fig.  13 .
Pani and Loeb Pani and Loeb 2014 have argued that this excludes PBHs from providing the dark matter throughout the sublunar window.
However, this argument is sensitive to the dark matter density at the centers of globular clusters.
This was taken to be 10 4 ​ GeV ​ cm − 3 10^{4}\,\mathrm{GeV}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3} but it is now known to be much lower than this for particular globular clusters Ibata et al. 2013 ; Bradford et al. 2011 .
Since these limits are no longer believed Capela et al. ; Defillon et al. 2014 , they are not included in Fig.  10 but they are shown by broken lines in Fig.  13 . 
 Graham et al. Graham et al. 2015 argue that PBHs can trigger white dwarf (WD) explosions.
Their transit through a WD causes localised heating through dynamical friction and this can initiate runaway thermonuclear fusion, causing the WD to explode as a supernova.
The shape of the observed WD distribution rules out PBHs with masses 10 19 ​ – ​ 10 20 ​ g 10^{19}\text{--}10^{20}\,\mathrm{g} providing the local DM, while those in the range 10 20 ​ – ​ 10 22 ​ g 10^{20}\text{--}10^{22}\,\mathrm{g} are constrained by the observed supernova rate.
This raises the intriguing possibility that a class of supernova may be triggered through rare events induced by DM rather than the conventional mechanism of accreting white dwarfs exploding upon reaching the Chandrasekhar mass.
However, Montero-Camacho et al. argue that this argument is inapplicable Montero-Camacho et al. 2019 , so the supernovae limit is shown by a broken line in Fig.  13 . 
 Several authors have suggested that PBH interactions with a neutron star (NS) could explain various astronomical phenomena.
There are too many astrophysical uncertainties in these models to infer clear constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) but they all depend on the fact that a PBH captured by a NS sinks to the center and consumes it from the inside.
For example, Takhistov et al. Takhistov 2019 ; Takhistov 2018 argue that collisions between NSs and PBHs could generate a population of solar-mass PBHs, leading to a new class of γ \gamma -ray bursts and micro-quasars.
Abramowicz and Bejger Abramowicz et al. 2018 argue that collisions with PBHs of 10 23 ​ g 10^{23}\,\mathrm{g} may explain the phenomenology of FRBs, in particular their millisecond durations and large luminosities.
Contrary to the usual explanation for FRBs, no large magnetic fields are needed in their model. 
 Fuller et al. Fuller et al. 2017 show that some or all of the inventory of r-process nucleosynthesis can be produced in such interactions if PBHs with masses 10 − 14 ​ – ​ 10 − 8 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-14}\text{--}10^{-8}\,M_{\odot} make up just a few percent of the DM.
When such a PBH is swallowed by a rotating millisecond NS, they argue that the resulting spin-up ejects ∼ 0.1 ​ – ​ 0.5 ​ M ⊙ \sim 0.1\text{--}0.5\,M_{\odot} of relatively cold neutron-rich material.
This can also produce a kilonova-type afterglow and an FRB but not significant gravitational radiation or neutrinos, allowing such events to be differentiated from the compact-object mergers detectable by gravitational-wave observatories.
Ejected matter is also heated by beta decay, which leads to positron emission consistent with the observed 511 ​ keV 511\,\mathrm{keV} line from the Galactic Center.
They claim that this scenario is compatible with pulsar and NS statistics, as well as with the r-process evolution history within the Galaxy. 
 III.3.3 Wide binaries 
 Binary stars with wide separation are vulnerable to disruption from encounters with PBHs or any other type of compact object Bahcall et al. 1985 ; Weinberg et al. 1987 .
Observations of wide binaries in the Galaxy therefore constrain the abundance of halo PBHs.
By comparing the result of simulations with observations, Yoo et al. Yoo et al. 2004 originally ruled out compact objects with M > 43 ​ M ⊙ M>43\,M_{\odot} from providing most of the halo mass.
However, Quinn et al. Quinn et al. 2009 later performed a more careful analysis of the radial velocities of these binaries and found that one of the widest-separation ones was spurious.
The resulting 95 % CL constraint became 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 500 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 500 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 10 3 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.5 ( 10 3 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/500\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(500\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 10^{3}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.5&(10^{3}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{8}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (75) 
 It flattens off above 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\,M_{\odot} because one-off disruption then dominates, as implied by Eq. ( 70 ).
More recent studies by Monroy-Rodríguez and Allen  Monroy-Rodríguez and Allen 2014 have reduced the lower mass limit from 500 ​ M ⊙ 500\,M_{\odot} to 21 ​ – ​ 78 ​ M ⊙ 21\text{--}78\,M_{\odot}  , so the limit is taken to be 100 ​ M ⊙ 100\,M_{\odot} in Fig.  13 and f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) then flattens off at around 0.1 0.1 .
The narrow window between the ML lower bound and the wide-binary upper bound is therefore shrinking and may in principle be eliminated altogether.
More positively, Tian et al. Tian et al. 2020 have recently studied more than 4,000 halo wide binaries in the Gaia survey and detected a break in their separation distribution that may be indicative of PBHs with M > 10 ​ M ⊙ M>10M_{\odot}  . 
 III.3.4 Disruption of globular clusters and dwarf galaxies 
 An argument similar to the binary disruption one shows that the survival of globular clusters against tidal disruption by passing PBHs gives a limit 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.03 ( 10 6 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 11 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{6}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.03&(10^{6}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{11}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (76) 
 although this depends sensitively on the mass and the radius of the cluster.
The limit flattens off above 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{6}\,M_{\odot} because one-off encounters then dominate.
The upper limit of 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ 3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot} on the mass of objects dominating the halo is consistent with the numerical calculations of Moore Moore 1993 . 
 In a related limit, Brandt Brandt 2016 claims that a mass above 5 ​ M ⊙ 5\,M_{\odot} is excluded by the fact that a star cluster near the centre of the dwarf galaxy Eridanus II has not been disrupted by halo objects.
His constraint can be written as Carr et al. 2016a 
 f ⁡ ( M ) ≲ { ( M / 3.7 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 / [ 1.1 − 0.1 ​ ln ⁡ ( M / M ⊙ ) ] ( M < 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M > 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)\lesssim\begin{cases}(M/3.7\,M_{\odot})^{-1}/[1.1-0.1\ln(M/M_{\odot})]&(M<10^{4}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/10^{6}\,M_{\odot})&(M>10^{4}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (77) 
 where the density and velocity dispersion of the DM at the centre of the galaxy are taken to be 0.1 ​ M ⊙ ​ pc − 3 0.1\,M_{\odot}\,\mathrm{pc}^{-3} and 5 ​ km ​ s − 1 5\,\mathrm{km}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} , respectively, and the age of the star cluster is taken to be 3 ​ Gyr 3\,\mathrm{Gyr} .
This limit is shown in Fig.  13 .
The second expression in Eq. ( 77 ) was not included in Ref.  Brandt 2016 but corresponds to having at least one black hole in the dwarf galaxy (the incredulity limit).
Zoutendijk et al. Zoutendijk et al. 2020 update Brandt’s constraint with new data on Eridanus II and present more secure evidence that the stellar overdensity seen towards it is a star cluster belonging to that galaxy. 
 Koushiappas and Loeb Koushiappas and Loeb 2017 have studied another type of effect of PBHs on the dynamical evolution of stars in dwarf galaxies.
They find that mass segregation leads to a depletion of stars in the center of such galaxies and the appearance of a ring in the projected stellar surface density profile.
Using Segue 1 as an example, they show that current observations of the projected surface stellar density imply f < 0.04 f<0.04 at 99.9 % CL for masses above around 10 ​ M ⊙ 10\,M_{\odot}  .
Their limit is shown in Fig.  13 and has the expected M − 1 M^{-1} form. 
 Several groups have obtained PBH constraints from observations of ultra-faint dwarf galaxies (UFDGs), although they do not express their results as limits on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) .
Zhu et al. Zhu et al. 2018 follow the joint evolution of stars and PBHs in UFDGs with a Fokker–Planck code for different DM parameters and then use a Markov chain approach to constrain the PBH properties.
They find that two-body relaxation between the stars and PBHs drives up the stellar core size and increases the central stellar velocity dispersion.
Using the observed half-light radius and stellar velocity dispersion as joint constraints, they infer that the PBHs must have a mass range of 2 ​ – ​ 14 ​ M ⊙ 2\text{--}14\,M_{\odot} if they are to provide a substantial fraction of the DM.
Boldrini et al. Boldrini et al. 2020 use an N-body code to show that the PBHs can induce a cusp-to-core transition in UFDGs via the dynamical friction of the DM particles on PBHs in the mass range 25 ​ – ​ 100 ​ M ⊙ 25\text{--}100\,M_{\odot} and this requires an upper limit f < 0.01 f<0.01 .
Stegmann et al. Stegmann et al. 2020 have improved these limits by considering a much larger sample of UFDGs and allowing the PBHs to have a lognormal mass distribution.
The analysis of the half-light radii and velocity dispersions resulting from their simulations appears to exclude PBHs with 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ​ – ​ 100 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(1\text{--}100)\,M_{\odot} from providing all the DM. 
 III.3.5 Disc heating and tidal streams 
 Other dynamical limits come into play at higher mass scales.
Halo objects will overheat the stars in the Galactic disc unless one has Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 3 × 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( M < 3 × 10 9 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / M halo ) ( M > 3 × 10 9 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/3\times 10^{6}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(M<3\times 10^{9}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/M_{\mathrm{halo}})&(M>3\times 10^{9}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (78) 
 where the lower expression is the incredulity limit.
The upper limit of 3 × 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 3\times 10^{6}\,M_{\odot} agrees with the more precise calculations by Lacey and Ostriker Lacey and Ostriker 1985 , although they argued that black holes with 2 × 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 2\times 10^{6}\,M_{\odot} could explain some features of disc heating.
Constraint ( 78 ) bottoms out at M ∼ 3 × 10 9 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 3\times 10^{9}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 10 − 3 f\sim 10^{-3} and is shown in Fig.  13 .
Evidence for a similar effect may come from the claim of Totani Totani 2010 that elliptical galaxies are puffed up by dark halo objects of 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}\,M_{\odot}  , although this limit is not shown in Fig.  13 . 
 Tidal streams in the Milky Way are sensitive probes of the population of low-mass DM subhalos predicted by CDM simulations.
This was originally considered for subhalos made of thermal relics Banik et al. 2018 but the argument can also be applied to constrain PBHs.
Bovy et al. Bovy et al. 2017 have developed a method for calculating the perturbed distribution function of a stream segment, computing the perturbed density for subhalo distributions down to 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}\,M_{\odot}  .
They apply this formalism to the Pal 5 stream and 10 10 DM subhalos within 20 ​ kpc 20\,\mathrm{kpc} of the Galactic center and with masses in the range 10 6.5 ​ – ​ 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 10^{6.5}\text{--}10^{9}\,M_{\odot}  .
If one applies this argument to PBHs and assumes that the Pal 5 stream is 5 ​ Gyr 5\,\mathrm{Gyr} old, their limit corresponds to f ⁡ ( M ) < 0.002 f(M)<0.002 in this mass range.
However, Bovy et al. do not infer this constraint themselves, so it is not shown in our figures. 
 III.3.6 Dynamical friction effect on halo objects 
 Another limit in this mass range arises because halo objects will be dragged into the nucleus of our own Galaxy by the dynamical friction of the spheroid stars and halo objects themselves (if they have an extended mass function), this leading to excessive nuclear mass unless Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 2 × 10 4 M ⊙ ) − 10 / 7 ( r c / 2 kpc ) 2 ( M < 5 × 10 5 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / 4 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) − 2 ​ ( r c / 2 ​ kpc ) 2 ( 5 × 10 5 ​ M ⊙ < M < 2 × 10 6 ​ ( r c / 2 ​ kpc ) ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / 0.1 M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 ( 2 × 10 6 ​ ( r c / 2 ​ kpc ) ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / M halo ) ( M > 10 8 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/2\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})^{-10/7}\,(r_{\mathrm{c}}/2\,\mathrm{kpc})^{2}&(M<5\times 10^{5}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/4\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})^{-2}\,(r_{\mathrm{c}}/2\,\mathrm{kpc})^{2}&(5\times 10^{5}\,M_{\odot}<M<2\times 10^{6}\,(r_{\mathrm{c}}/2\,\mathrm{kpc})\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/0.1\,M_{\odot})^{-1/2}&(2\times 10^{6}\,(r_{\mathrm{c}}/2\,\mathrm{kpc})\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{8}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/M_{\mathrm{halo}})&(M>10^{8}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (79) 
 The last expression is the incredulity limit and first three correspond to the drag being dominated by spheroid stars (low M M ), halo objects (high M M ) and some combination of the two (intermediate M M ).
The limit is sensitive to the halo core radius r c r_{\mathrm{c}} but with the normalisation of 2 ​ kpc 2\,\mathrm{kpc} one requires f < 1 f<1 for M < 2 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ M<2\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot}  .
The limit bottoms out at M ∼ 10 8 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 10^{8}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 3 × 10 − 5 f\sim 3\times 10^{-5} and is shown in Figs.  13 .
However, there is a caveat here in that holes drifting into the nucleus might be ejected by the slingshot mechanism if there is already a binary black hole there Hut and Rees 1992 .
This possibility was explored by Xu and Ostriker Xu and Ostriker 1994 , who obtained a larger upper limit of 3 × 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 3\times 10^{6}\,M_{\odot}  . 
 III.3.7 Disruption and tidal distortion of galaxies in clusters 
 A similar argument to that used for globular clusters shows that the survival of galaxies in clusters against tidal disruption by giant cluster PBHs gives a limit  Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 10 10 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 10 10 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 11 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.1 ( 10 11 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 13 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M / 2 × 10 14 ​ M ⊙ ) ( M > 10 13 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/10^{10}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(10^{10}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{11}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.1&(10^{11}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{13}\,M_{\odot})\\
(M/2\times 10^{14}\,M_{\odot})&(M>10^{13}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (80) 
 where 2 × 10 14 ​ M ⊙ 2\times 10^{14}\,M_{\odot} in the last term corresponds to the mass of a typical cluster.
This limit is shown in Fig.  13 .
There is also a constraint from the lack of unexplained tidal distortions of galaxies in clusters.
If the fraction of distorted galaxies at any time is Δ g \Delta_{\mathrm{g}} , this limit takes the form  Carr and Sakellariadou 1999 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < λ − 3 ​ Δ g ​ ( ρ g ρ c ) ≈ 20 ​ ( λ 3 ) − 3 ​ Δ g , f(M)<\lambda^{-3}\,\Delta_{\mathrm{g}}\,\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{g}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}\right)\approx 20\,\left(\frac{\lambda}{3}\right)^{-3}\,\Delta_{\mathrm{g}}\,, 
 (81) 
 where ρ g \rho_{\mathrm{g}} and ρ c \rho_{\mathrm{c}} are the densities of the galaxy and cluster, respectively, and λ ≈ 3 \lambda\approx 3 represents the difference between distortion and disruption.
So one has an interesting limit providing the fraction of unexplained distorted galaxies is less than 5 %.
This limit only applies for M > λ − 3 ​ M g ≈ 3 × 10 9 ​ M ⊙ M>\lambda^{-3}M_{\mathrm{g}}\approx 3\times 10^{9}\,M_{\odot} since otherwise the tidal distortion radius is smaller than R g R_{\mathrm{g}}  .
In particular, van den Bergh  van den Bergh 1969 concluded from observations of the Virgo cluster, which has a mass of 10 15 ​ M ⊙ 10^{15}\,M_{\odot} and only 4 of 73 cluster members showing unexplained tidal distortions, that the DM could not be in the form of compact object in the mass range 10 9 ​ – ​ 10 13 ​ M ⊙ 10^{9}\text{--}10^{13}\,M_{\odot}  .
This limit is not shown in Fig.  13 since it is sensitive to poorly determined astrophysical parameters. 
 III.3.8 Intergalactic PBHs 
 If there were a population of huge intergalactic PBHs with density parameter Ω D ​ ( M ) \Omega_{D}(M)  , each galaxy would have a peculiar velocity due to its gravitational interaction with the nearest one Carr 1978 .
If the objects were smoothly distributed, the typical distance between them would be 
 d ≈ 40 Ω D ( M ) − 1 / 3 ( M / 10 16 M ⊙ ) 1 / 3 ( h / 0.7 ) − 2 / 3 Mpc . d\approx 40\,\Omega_{D}(M)^{-1/3}\,(M/10^{16}\,M_{\odot})^{1/3}\,(h/0.7)^{-2/3}\,\mathrm{Mpc}\,. 
 (82) 
 This would also be the expected distance of the nearest black hole to the Milky Way.
Over the age of the Universe, this should induce a peculiar velocity 
 V pec ≈ G ​ M ​ f ​ ( Ω 0 ) ​ t 0 / d 2 , V_{\mathrm{pec}}\approx G\,M\,f(\Omega_{0})\,t_{0}/d^{2}\,, 
 (83) 
 where Ω 0 ≈ 0.3 \Omega_{0}\approx 0.3 is the total density parameter and f ⁡ ( Ω 0 ) ≈ Ω 0 0.6 f(\Omega_{0})\approx\Omega_{0}^{0.6} .
Since the CMB dipole anisotropy shows that the peculiar velocity of our Galaxy is only 400 ​ km ​ s − 1 400\,\mathrm{km}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} , one infers 
 Ω D < ( M / 2 × 10 17 M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 ( t 0 / 14 Gyr ) − 3 / 2 ( Ω 0 / 0.3 ) − 0.9 ( h / 0.7 ) − 2 . \Omega_{D}<(M/2\times 10^{17}\,M_{\odot})^{-1/2}\,(t_{0}/14\,\mathrm{Gyr})^{-3/2}\,(\Omega_{0}/0.3)^{-0.9}\,(h/0.7)^{-2}\,. 
 (84) 
 This scenario is interesting only if there is at least one such object within the observable universe and this corresponds to the cosmological incredulity limit 
 Ω D ​ ( M ) > 3 × 10 − 8 ​ ( M / 10 16 ​ M ⊙ ) ​ ( t 0 / 14 ​ Gyr ) − 3 ​ ( h / 0.7 ) − 2 , \Omega_{D}(M)>3\times 10^{-8}\,(M/10^{16}\,M_{\odot})\,(t_{0}/14\,\mathrm{Gyr})^{-3}\,(h/0.7)^{-2}\,, 
 (85) 
 where we have taken the horizon scale to be d ≈ 3 ​ c ​ t 0 ≈ 10 ​ h − 1 ​ Gpc d\approx 3\,c\,t_{0}\approx 10\,h^{-1}\,\mathrm{Gpc} .
This intersects Eq. ( 84 ) at M ≈ 10 21 ​ M ⊙ M\approx 10^{21}\,M_{\odot}  , so this corresponds to the largest possible intergalactic dark object within the visible Universe.
Constraints ( 84 ) and ( 85 ) are shown in Fig.  13 and close off Fig.  10 at the right. 
 III.4 Cosmic structure constraints 
 The dynamical constraints show that PBHs larger than 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{2}\,M_{\odot} cannot provide the DM but Carr and Silk Carr and Silk 2018 point out that such PBHs could generate cosmic structures through the ‘seed’ or ‘Poisson’ effect even if f f is small.
The seed effect was first pointed out by Hoyle and Narlikar Hoyle and Narlikar 1966 and subsequently studied in Refs.  Ryan 1972 ; Carr and Rees 1984 .
The Poisson effect was first pointed out by Mészáros Mészáros 1975 and subsequently studied in Refs.  Carr 1977 ; Freese et al. 1983 ; Carr and Silk 1983 .
This raises the issue of the maximum mass of a PBH and whether the 10 6 ​ – ​ 10 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{6}\text{--}10^{10}\,M_{\odot} black holes in galactic nuclei could be of primordial origin.
It is sometimes argued that the success of the BBN scenario implies that PBHs can only form before 1 ​ s 1\,\mathrm{s} , corresponding to M < 10 5 ​ M ⊙ M<10^{5}\,M_{\odot}  .
However, the fraction of Universe collapsing into PBHs at that time is only 10 − 6 ​ ( t / 1 ​ s ) 1 / 2 10^{-6}\,(t/1\,\mathrm{s})^{1/2} , so the effect on BBN should be tiny.
As discussed later, the most important constraint in this mass range comes from the limit on the CMB μ \mu distortion. 
 If the Universe contains a population of PBHs of mass M M , then a region of mass ℳ \mathcal{M} may contain just one of them (giving a seed effect) or many of them (giving a Poisson effect).
The associated initial fluctuation is 
 δ i ≈ { M / ℳ ( seed ) ( f ​ M / ℳ ) 1 / 2 ( Poisson ) . \delta_{\mathrm{i}}\approx\begin{cases}M/\mathcal{M}&(\text{seed})\\
(f\,M/\mathcal{M})^{1/2}&(\text{Poisson})\,.\end{cases} 
 (86) 
 If f = 1 f=1 , the Poisson effect dominates for all ℳ \mathcal{M} .
If f ≪ 1 f\ll 1 , the seed effect dominates for ℳ < M / f \mathcal{M}<M/f , the limit corresponding to one PBH per region of mass ℳ \mathcal{M} (i.e. the incredulity limit).
In either case, the fluctuation grows as z − 1 z^{-1} from the epoch of matter domination ( z eq ≈ 4000 z_{\mathrm{eq}}\approx 4000 ), so the mass binding at redshift z B z_{\mathrm{B}} is 
 ℳ ≈ { 4000 ​ M ​ z B − 1 ( seed ) 10 7 ​ f ​ M ​ z B − 2 ( Poisson ) . \mathcal{M}\approx\begin{cases}4000\,M\,z_{\mathrm{B}}^{-1}&(\text{seed})\\
10^{7}\,f\,M\,z_{\mathrm{B}}^{-2}&(\text{Poisson})\,.\end{cases} 
 (87) 
 If PBHs provide the DM ( f = 1 f=1 ), the dynamical limits discussed in the previous section require M < 10 3 ​ M ⊙ M<10^{3}\,M_{\odot} and so the Poisson effect could only bind a scale ℳ < 10 10 ​ z B − 2 ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{M}<10^{10}\,z_{\mathrm{B}}^{-2}\,M_{\odot}  , which is necessarily subgalactic.
This assumes the PBHs have a monochromatic mass function.
If it is extended, the situation is more complicated Carr and Silk 2018 because the mass of the effective seed for a region depends on the mass of that region ( ℳ \mathcal{M} ). 
 Even if PBHs play no role in generating cosmic structures, one can still place interesting upper limits on the fraction of DM in them by requiring that various types of structure do not form too early.
Carr and Silk find the constraints associated with dwarf galaxies, Milky-Way-type galaxies and clusters of galaxies, these being shown in Fig.  14 and discussed below.
There is also a Poisson constraint associated with observations of the Lyman- α \alpha forest  Afshordi et al. 2003 ; Murgia et al. 2019 , which was somewhat misrepresented in our earlier work  Carr et al. 2010a . 
 The effect of PBH Poisson fluctuations - also termed PBH shot noise - on cosmic structure is also of interest because it enhances the matter power spectrum on small scales.
In particular, several authors have suggested that this would allow the first bound halos to form earlier and more abundantly than in the standard scenario  Carr and Silk 2018 ; Kashlinsky et al. 2005 .
Most recently, Oguri and Takahashi  Oguri and Takahashi 2020 have pointed out that PBHs larger than 0.1 ​ M ⊙ 0.1\,M_{\odot} would significantly enhance the power spectrum above 10 5 ​ h ​ Mpc − 1 10^{5}\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1} , thereby increasing the gravitational lensing dispersions at 10 ​ – ​ 100 ​ Hz 10\text{--}100\,\mathrm{Hz} by more than an order of magnitude. 
 Figure 14: Large-scale structure constraints from the requirement that various types of cosmic structures do not form too early  Carr and Silk 2018 and Lyman- α \alpha forest observations  Murgia et al. 2019 . 
 III.4.1 Lyman- α \alpha systems 
 Afshordi et al.   Afshordi et al. 2003 used observations of the Lyman- α \alpha forest to obtain an upper limit of about 10 4 ​ M ⊙ 10^{4}\,M_{\odot} on the mass of PBHs which provide the DM.
CKSY extended this result to the case in which the PBHs provide a fraction f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) of the DM on the assumption that the Lyman- α \alpha objects are associated with a single mass M Ly ​ α ∼ 10 10 ​ M ⊙ M_{\mathrm{Ly}\alpha}\sim 10^{10}\,M_{\odot}  .
Since the Poisson fluctuation in the number of PBHs on this mass scale grows between z eq ≈ 4000 z_{\mathrm{eq}}\approx 4000 and the redshift up to which the Lyman- α \alpha forest is observed ( z Ly ​ α ≈ 4 z_{\mathrm{Ly}\alpha}\approx 4 ), corresponding to a growth factor of z eq / z Ly ​ α ≈ 10 3 z_{\mathrm{eq}}/z_{\mathrm{Ly}\alpha}\approx 10^{3} , CKSY claimed that the forest will form earlier than observed unless f ⁡ ( M ) < ( M / 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 f(M)<(M/10^{4}\,M_{\odot})^{-1} .
However, there was some confusion here because the Afshordi et al. limit is independent of the nature of the Lyman- α \alpha objects themselves;
it just uses their power spectrum as a tracer of the density fluctuations.
So the CKSY limit just corresponds to the requirement that 10 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{10}\,M_{\odot} objects (i.e. dwarf galaxies) must not bind too early, as discussed below. 
 Murgia et al. Murgia et al. 2019 have recently improved the Lyman- α \alpha limit, using a grid of hydrodynamic simulations to explore different values of astrophysical parameters.
They obtain a ( 2 ​ σ 2\sigma ) upper limit of the form f ⁡ ( M ) < ( M / 60 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 f(M)<(M/60\,M_{\odot})^{-1} when a Gaussian prior on the reionisation redshift is imposed, weakening to f ⁡ ( M ) < ( M / 170 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 f(M)<(M/170\,M_{\odot})^{-1} when a conservative flat prior is assumed.
Both limits are significantly stronger than the Afshordi et al. one and the first is included in Fig.  14 .
The limit flattens off at 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\,M_{\odot} with f ∼ 0.05 f\sim 0.05 but they do not discuss how to extend the limit above 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}M_{\odot}  .
This is problematic because the seed effect then dominates but only influences a small fraction of regions, so the interpretation of the observations is unclear. 
 III.4.2 Galaxies and clusters of galaxies 
 Galaxies clearly span a range of masses and form over a range of redshifts.
However, if we apply the above argument to Milky-Way-type galaxies, assuming these have a typical mass of 10 12 ​ M ⊙ 10^{12}\,M_{\odot} and must not bind before a redshift z B ∼ 3 z_{\mathrm{B}}\sim 3 , we obtain 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 10 6 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 10 9 ​ M ⊙ ) M / 10 12 ​ M ⊙ ( 10 9 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 12 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/10^{6}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(10^{6}\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 10^{9}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/10^{12}\,M_{\odot}&(10^{9}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{12}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (88) 
 This limit is shown in Fig.  14 and bottoms out at M ∼ 10 9 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 10^{9}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 0.001 f\sim 0.001 .
The first expression can be obtained by putting ℳ ∼ 10 12 ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{M}\sim 10^{12}\,M_{\odot} and z B ∼ 3 z_{\mathrm{B}}\sim 3 in Eq. ( 87 ).
The second expression corresponds to having just one PBH per galaxy and is also the line above which the seed effect dominates the Poisson effect.
Indeed, since the initial seed fluctuation is M / ℳ M/\mathcal{M} , the seed mass required for a Milky-Way-type galaxy to bind at z ≈ 3 z\approx 3 is immediately seen to be 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 10^{9}\,M_{\odot}  .
There is no constraint on PBHs below this line because the fraction of the Universe going into galaxies would be small, with most of the baryons presumably going into the intergalactic medium. 
 If we apply the same argument to dwarf galaxies, assuming these have a mass of 10 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{10}\,M_{\odot} and must not bind before z B ∼ 10 z_{\mathrm{B}}\sim 10 , we obtain 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 10 5 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 10 5 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 3 × 10 7 ​ M ⊙ ) M / 10 10 ​ M ⊙ ( 3 × 10 7 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 10 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/10^{5}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(10^{5}\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 3\times 10^{7}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/10^{10}\,M_{\odot}&(3\times 10^{7}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{10}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (89) 
 this bottoming out at M ∼ 3 × 10 7 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 3\times 10^{7}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 0.003 f\sim 0.003 .
On the other hand, if we apply the argument to clusters of galaxies, assuming these have a mass of 10 14 ​ M ⊙ 10^{14}\,M_{\odot} and must not bind before z B ∼ 1 z_{\mathrm{B}}\sim 1 , we obtain 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 10 7 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 10 7 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 3 × 10 10 ​ M ⊙ ) M / 10 14 ​ M ⊙ ( 3 × 10 10 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 14 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/10^{7}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(10^{7}\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 3\times 10^{10}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/10^{14}\,M_{\odot}&(3\times 10^{10}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{14}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (90) 
 this bottoming out at M ∼ 3 × 10 10 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 3\times 10^{10}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 0.0003 f\sim 0.0003 .
Clearly all these limits are very approximate and the division into different types of bound structure is just made for simplification.
In practice, one should combine all the limits shown in Fig.  14 to obtain the boundary indicated by the solid line. 
 III.4.3 First baryonic clouds 
 The first bound baryonic clouds would be expected to have a mass of 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{6}M_{\odot} and be surrounded by minihalos of CDM in the usual picture.
We cannot apply the above argument to constrain PBHs directly because there is no observational evidence for the formation redshift of such clouds.
However, they would form at z B ∼ 100 z_{\mathrm{B}}\sim 100 in the CDM picture, so we can still derive a limit corresponding to the requirement that the standard picture is not perturbed.
In this case, Eq. ( 88 ) is replaced by 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 10 3 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 ( 10 3 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) M / 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ( 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 6 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/10^{3}\,M_{\odot})^{-1}&(10^{3}\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/10^{6}\,M_{\odot}&(3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{6}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (91) 
 this bottoming out at M ∼ 3 × 10 4 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 3\times 10^{4}\,M_{\odot} with a value f ∼ 0.03 f\sim 0.03 .
This is not shown in Fig.  14 since it is not an actual constraint.
This scenario has been studied in more detail by Kadota and Silk  Kadota and Silk 2021 , who argue that a large fraction of the DM can reside in minihalos which formed at z > 100 z>100 .
Since they would have a higher density than in the conventional scenario (without PBHs), they would be less prone to tidal disruption. 
 Kashlinsky has been prompted by the LIGO/Virgo observations to consider the formation of bound clouds by the Poisson fluctuations associated with a population of 30 ​ M ⊙ 30\,M_{\odot} PBHs Kashlinsky 2016 .
However, he adds an interesting new feature to the scenario by suggesting that this could explain the spatial coherence of the fluctuations in the source-subtracted near-IR cosmic infrared background (CIB) detected by the Spitzer/Akari satellites and the X-ray background  Kashlinsky et al. 2007 ; Cappelluti et al. 2013 ; Kashlinsky et al. 2012 ; Kashlinsky et al. 2018 .
The idea is that the infrared radiation is generated by the stars which form in the first halos and the X-rays by the PBHs.
It has long been appreciated that the CIB and its fluctuations would be a crucial test of scenarios in which the DM comprises black holes Bond et al. 1986 but in this case the PBHs are merely triggering high-redshift star formation and not generating the CIB directly. 
 In a related scenario, the formation of bound clusters of PBHs through the Poisson effect is invoked to generate the supermassive black holes (SMBHs) thought to reside in galactic nuclei Düchting 2004 ; Khlopov et al. 2005 ;
if one replaces ℳ \mathcal{M} with 10 8 ​ M ⊙ 10^{8}\,M_{\odot} and z B z_{\mathrm{B}} with 10 10 , the limiting mass implied by Eq. ( 87 ) is reduced to 10 3 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\,M_{\odot}  .
The process whereby a cluster of PBHs can evolve into the single SMBH associated with a quasar has been studied by Dokuchaev et al.   Dokuchaev et al. 2005 .
Recently, Kashlinksky  Kashlinsky 2021 has linked these scenarios by arguing that advection will lead to a common motion of DM and baryons on the scales relevant for the formation of the first luminous sources.
The advection rate reaches a minimum at around 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 10^{9}M_{\odot}  , associated with the formation of quasars, and then rises to a maximum near 10 12 ​ M ⊙ 10^{12}M_{\odot}  , associated with galaxy formation.
This scenario was also discussed by Serpico et al.   Serpico et al. 2020 , who pointed out that it is constrained by the CMB polarisation measurements because the advection-dominated accretion inevitably emit extra photons at high redshifts. 
 III.4.4 Clusters of PBHs 
 Clesse and García-Bellido stress that PBHs could be spatially clustered into subhaloes if they are part of a larger-scale overdense region Clesse and García-Bellido 2017 .
Indeed, this idea goes back to the early work of Chisholm Chisholm 2006 ; Chisholm 2011 .
They infer that the lensing constraints in the mass range required to explain the LIGO/Virgo detections can be relaxed and this also has important implications for the efficacy of PBH merging in the early Universe.
However, this argument depends on details of small-scale structure formation which are not fully understood.
Indeed, Bringmann et al. Bringmann et al. 2019 argue that the PBH constraints become even stronger if there is large initial clustering.
Ballesteros et al.   Ballesteros et al. 2018 argue that this only applies if the PBHs have a very wide mass function but they agree that PBHs with a narrow mass function cannot be numerous enough to explain the LIGO/Virgo detections. 
 Ali-Haïmoud Ali-Haïmoud 2018 has studied the initial clustering of PBHs formed from the gravitational collapse of large density fluctuations and does not find small-scale clustering beyond that expected from Poisson effects.
He claims that Chisholm’s analysis is flawed due to an improper series expansion.
He also derives an analytic expression for the two-point correlation function of large-threshold fluctuations, generalizing previous results to arbitrary separation.
This conclusion is supported by Moradinezhad Dizgah et al.   Moradinezhad Dizgah et al. 2019 .
Desjacques and Riotto Desjacques and Riotto 2018 have discussed the impact of PBH spatial clustering on the large-scale power spectrum.
While a Poissonian term is always present in the zero-lag correlation, this need not imply that PBHs are Poisson distributed because their initial clustering depends on the shape of the small-scale power spectrum.
However, this is not relevant for a narrow spectral feature and for the PBH masses still allowed by observations. 
 Young and Byrnes Young and Byrnes 2020 consider the impact of local non-Gaussianity on the initial PBH clustering and mass function due to mode coupling between long and short wavelength modes.
They show that even a small amount of non-Gaussianity results in a significant enhancement of clustering and merging, shifting the mass function to higher masses.
However, as the clustering becomes strong, the increased local PBH number density leads to a large theoretical uncertainty in the merger rate.
Using a functional integration approach, Suyama and Yokoyama Suyama and Yokoyama 2019 find that PBH clustering on super-Hubble scales can never be induced if the initial primordial fluctuations are Gaussian but that it can be enhanced by the local trispectrum of the primordial curvature perturbations.
Franciolini et al.   Franciolini et al. 2018 have used a path integral approach to study the effects of non-Gaussianity on both the formation and clustering of PBHs.
Matsubara et al. have considered the clustering of PBHs if they form in an early matter-dominated epoch from non-Gaussian fluctuations Matsubara et al. 2019 .
Clustering is less significant in this case but can still be much larger than Poisson shot noise.
The situation is complicated and a clear picture of the effect on non-Gaussianities on PBH abundance and distribution has not yet emerged. 
 III.5 Accretion and μ \mu -distortion constraints 
 There are a large number of constraints associated with the accretion of PBHs and the strongest of these are summarised in Fig.  15 .
However, it should be stressed that all these limits depend on various astrophysical parameters and qualitative features, such as whether one has disc or spherical accretion.
Also most studies assume Bondi accretion, even though there is no direct evidence that this applies.
Therefore these limits may be less secure than the dynamical ones.
Since accretion can modify both the mass and mass function of PBHs, this also changes one’s interpretation of any limits on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) , considerably weakening the constraints for PBHs larger than a few solar masses  De Luca et al. 2020a . 
 Figure 15: Accretion (cyan and yellow) and gravitational wave (grey) constraints.
The Planck limits Serpico et al. 2020 assume that dark halos form in the late matter-dominated epoch.
The EDGES limit  Hektor et al. 2018a from the 21 cm absorption signal is shown by a broken line because it depends on several ill-determined parameters.
The X-ray binaries limit Inoue and Kusenko 2017 and X-ray/radio limits Manshanden et al. 2019 come from source counts; the latter are shown by a broken line because of the criticism of Ref.  Hektor et al. 2018b .
The limit from the heating of Leo T  Lu et al. 2021 is shown by a broken line because it depends on several astrophysical assumptions.
The LIGO/Virgo limits come from the merger rates in O1 Ali-Haïmoud et al. 2017 and O2 Abbott et al. 2019a (solid line) and from the small number of burst detections (lower broken line) and non-detection of a stochastic GWB (upper broken line) in O2 Vaskonen and Veermäe 2020 . 
 III.5.1 Accretion during radiation-dominated era 
 There are good reasons for believing that PBHs cannot grow much during the radiation-dominated era.
Although a simple Bondi argument suggests that they could grow as fast as the horizon Zel’dovich and Novikov 1967 , this does not account for the background cosmological expansion and a fully relativistic calculation shows that such self-similar growth is impossible Carr and Hawking 1974 ; Bicknell and Henriksen 1978a ; Bicknell and Henriksen 1978b .
Consequently there is very little growth during the radiation era.
However, self-similar black hole solutions in an expanding background do exist for cosmological models dominated by a “dark energy” fluid with p < − ρ / 3 p<-\rho/3 , as in the quintessence scenario Harada et al. 2008 ; Maeda et al. 2008 ; Carr et al. 2010b .
This may support the suggestion of Bean and Magueijo Bean and Magueijo 2002 that intermediate-mass PBHs might accrete quintessence efficiently enough to evolve into the SMBHs in galactic nuclei. 
 III.5.2 Limit from ionisation and thermal history 
 Even if PBHs cannot accrete appreciably in the radiation-dominated era, they might still do so in the period after decoupling and the Bondi-type analysis should then apply.
The associated accretion and emission of radiation could have a profound effect on the thermal history of the Universe.
There are two types of constraints: one associated with the background radiation density generated (usually X-rays) and the other with the thermal history.
This problem was first analysed by Carr Carr 1981 on the assumption that the PBHs accrete at the Bondi rate with a luminosity bounded by the Eddington limit.
However, as discussed in a recent reassessment of this analysis Carr et al. 2021b , some of the assumptions made there were unrealistic and the Bondi formula itself fails above 10 4 ​ M ⊙ 10^{4}\,M_{\odot} because the accretion timescale exceeds the cosmic expansion timescale.
We therefore do not discuss the analysis of Ref.  Carr 1981 further here. 
 The problem was investigated more carefully by Ricotti et al. Ricotti et al. 2008 , who studied the effects of accreting PBHs on the ionisation and temperature evolution of the Universe.
The emitted X-rays would produce measurable anisotropies and spectral distortions in the CMB.
Using WMAP data to constrain the first, they obtained the limit 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 30 ​ M ⊙ ) − 2 ( 30 ​ M ⊙ < M ≲ 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ) 10 − 5 ( 10 4 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 11 ​ M ⊙ ) M / M ℓ = 100 ( M > 10 11 ​ M ⊙ ) , f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/30\,M_{\odot})^{-2}&(30\,M_{\odot}<M\lesssim 10^{4}\,M_{\odot})\\
10^{-5}&(10^{4}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{11}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/M_{\ell=100}&(M>10^{11}\,M_{\odot})\,,\end{cases} 
 (92) 
 where the last expression is not included in Ref.  Ricotti et al. 2008 but corresponds to having one PBH on the scale associated with the CMB anisotropies;
for ℓ = 100 \ell=100 , this is M ℓ = 100 ≈ 10 16 ​ M ⊙ M_{\ell=100}\approx 10^{16}\,M_{\odot}  .
Using FIRAS data to constrain the second, they obtained the limit 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < { ( M / 1 ​ M ⊙ ) − 2 ( 1 ​ M ⊙ < M ≪ 10 3 ​ M ⊙ ) 0.015 ( 10 3 ​ M ⊙ ≲ M < 10 14 ​ M ⊙ ) M / M ℓ = 100 ( M > 10 14 ​ M ⊙ ) . f(M)<\begin{cases}(M/1\,M_{\odot})^{-2}&(1\,M_{\odot}<M\ll 10^{3}\,M_{\odot})\\
0.015&(10^{3}\,M_{\odot}\lesssim M<10^{14}\,M_{\odot})\\
M/M_{\ell=100}&(M>10^{14}\,M_{\odot})\,.\end{cases} 
 (93) 
 Both limit flatten off above 10 3 ​ – ​ 10 4 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\text{--}10^{4}\,M_{\odot} because the luminosity tends to the Eddington limit but we note that the Bondi formula also fails in this regime.
These limits are stronger than the ones inferred from the X-ray background itself  Carr 1979 and appear to exclude f = 1 f=1 down to masses as low as 1 ​ M ⊙ 1\,M_{\odot}  .
However, they are very model-dependent and there was also a technical error (an incorrect power of redshift in one of the integrals), so they are not shown in Fig.  15 .
Nevertheless, the qualitative form of the above limits is correct. 
 This problem has subsequently been reconsidered by various groups.
Ali-Haïmoud and Kamionkowski Ali-Haïmoud and Kamionkowski 2017 include the suppression of accretion by Compton drag and Compton cooling from CMB photons, and allow for the PBH velocity relative to the background gas.
They find that the spectral distortions are too small to be detected, while the anisotropy constraints have a similar form to Eq. ( 92 ) but only exclude f = 1 f=1 above 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{2}\,M_{\odot}  .
Horowitz Horowitz performs a similar analysis and obtains an upper limit of 30 ​ M ⊙ 30\,M_{\odot}  .
However, neither of these analyses extends to sufficiently high mass to exhibit the flattening of the limit.
Aloni et al. Aloni et al. 2017 also calculate the effect of PBH accretion on the ionisation history but do not give an explicit constraint on the DM fraction.
Chen et al. Chen et al. 2016 use Planck data to constrain the DM contribution from PBHs for two different reionisation models and improve the upper limit ( 92 ) by two orders of magnitude.
However, their result depends on the Ricotti et al. analysis and is affected by the same error.
Although all these analyses exclude PBHs comprising the DM above some critical mass, they do not exclude the small fraction required to seed galaxies.
The fraction of the DM in SMBHs in galactic nuclei is Ω SMBH / Ω CDM ∼ 2 × 10 − 5 \Omega_{\mathrm{SMBH}}/\Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}\sim 2\times 10^{-5} , whereas the accretion limit generally flattens off about this value. 
 All the above arguments assume spherical accretion.
However, Poulin et al. Poulin et al. 2017b argue that this approximation breaks down, with an accretion disk forming instead, and this affects the statistical properties of the CMB anisotropies.
Even under conservative accretion assumptions, their constraints exclude a monochromatic distribution of PBHs with mass above 2 ​ M ⊙ 2\,M_{\odot} as the dominant form of DM.
However, the bound on the median PBH mass gets more stringent if a broad (lognormal) mass function is considered.
In follow-up work, Serpico et al. Serpico et al. 2020 study disk and spherical accretion onto a PBH plus halo system, this being strongly constrained by the latest observations of CMB polarisation Aghanim et al. 2020b ; Aghanim et al. 2020a , and these are the accretion limits shown in Fig.  15 . 
 Ewall-Wice et al. Ewall-Wice et al. 2018 estimate the 21 cm radio background from accretion onto intermediate-mass BHs between z = 30 z=30 and z = 16 z=16 .
Combining plausible scenarios for black hole formation and growth with empirical correlations between luminosity and radio emission observed in low-redshift AGN, they find that the black hole remnants of Population III stars are able to produce a 21 cm background.
In particular, this could explain the surprisingly large amplitude of the 21 cm absorption feature recently reported by the EDGES collaboration Bowman et al. 2018 .
The black holes could also contribute to the 0.5 ​ – ​ 2 ​ keV 0.5\text{--}2\,\mathrm{keV} soft X-ray background at a level consistent with existing constraints.
Although the Ewall-Wice et al. analysis may also apply for PBHs, they do not explicitly constrain the function f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) . 
 Hektor et al. Hektor et al. 2018a have used the EDGES results to place bounds on energy injection from the accretion of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ​ – ​ 100 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(1\text{--}100)\,M_{\odot} PBHs.
By requiring that the gas temperature at z ∼ 17 z\sim 17 does not exceed the EDGES value, they infer 
 f ⁡ ( M ) < C ⁡ ( β ) ​ ( 0.15 f E ) ​ ( λ 0.01 ​ M 10 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 − β , f(M)<C(\beta)\,\left(\frac{0.15}{f_{E}}\right)\,\left(\frac{\lambda}{0.01}\,\frac{M}{10\,M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1-\beta}\,, 
 (94) 
 where λ \lambda , β \beta and f E f_{E} are accretion parameters.
We include this as a broken line in Fig.  15 as an example of a constraint which can be expressed analytically but depends on various ill-determined parameters.
Mena et al. Mena et al. 2019 show that observations of the 21 cm transition in neutral hydrogen during the epoch of reionisation provide stringent constraints on solar-mass PBHs.
There are three distinct effects: (i) the modification to the primordial power spectrum induced by Poisson noise, (ii) a uniform heating and ionisation of the intergalactic medium via X-rays produced during accretion, and (iii) a local modification to the temperature and density of the ambient medium surrounding isolated PBHs.
Using a 4 4 -parameter astrophysical model, they show that experiments like SKA and HERA (Hydrogen Epoch of Reionization Array) could potentially improve upon existing constraints by more than an order of magnitude.
However, Hütsi et al.   Hütsi et al. 2019 show that PBH accretion could be suppressed due to enhanced substructure in the PBH distribution, softening the bounds from the 21 cm signature. 
 Abe et al. Abe et al. 2019 have investigated the Sunyaev–Zel’dovich (SZ) effect on the CMB caused by the emission of UV and X-ray photons by accreting PBHs, these photons ionizing and heating the intergalactic medium (IGM).
They compute the ionisation and temperature profiles around each PBH and infer the y y -parameter created by the IGM gas.
They find that the angular power spectrum of the SZ temperature anisotropy is a flat up to the scale of the ionised region ( l ≤ 2000 l\leq 2000 ) and could dominate the primordial temperature spectrum below the Silk scale. 
 Bosch-Ramon and Bellomo  Bosch-Ramon and Bellomo 2020 argue that outflows can reduce the accretion rate of PBHs with masses in the LIGO/Virgo range by at least one order of magnitude if aligned with the PBH motion.
If the outflow is perpendicular to the motion, the effect is less important.
Hasinger  Hasinger 2020 estimates the contribution of baryon accretion onto a population of 10 − 8 ​ – ​ 10 − 10 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-8}\text{--}10^{-10}\,M_{\odot} PBHs to the infrared and X-ray cosmic backgrounds, as indicated by the cross-correlation between the fluctuations in these backgrounds  Kashlinsky et al. 2018 .
Assuming Bondi capture and advection-dominated disk accretion, he claims the model fits the X-ray data, with the predicted flux peaking at redshifts z ≈ 17 ​ – ​ 30 z\approx 17\text{--}30 , although the PBH contribution to the infrared background fluctuations is only about 1 %. 
 III.5.3 Limit from accretion at present epoch 
 Limits associated with accretion at the present epoch avoid the uncertainties associated with early cosmological evolution but depend upon the environment and are not specific to black holes of primordial origin.
An early attempt to constrain f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) for black holes in galactic discs, galactic halos and clusters of galaxies by requiring that the background radiation generated not exceed the observed values in the appropriate waveband was made in Ref.  Carr 1979 .
Because the Bondi and Eddington accretion rates scale as M 2 M^{2} and M M , respectively, the limit on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) was found to always have the same form, scaling as M − 1 M^{-1} at low M M and flattening off when M M gets large enough for the Eddington limit to apply.
This constraint depends on the luminosity efficiency (taken to be 0.1 0.1 ) and the waveband in which radiation is emitted, with the upper limit on the mass of black holes providing the DM varying from 10 ​ M ⊙ 10\,M_{\odot} for X-ray emission to 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10^{2}\,M_{\odot} for UV-ray emission.
However, these limits are not included in Fig.  15 because they are very old and dependent on uncertain astrophysical parameters. 
 Another type of limit is derived from discrete source counts.
PBH accretion within galaxies should result in a significant X-ray flux, thereby contributing to the observed number of compact X-ray objects in galaxies.
For example, Mack et al. Mack et al. 2007 considered the growth of large PBHs through the capture of DM halos and suggested that the accretion of such enhanced holes when passing through molecular clouds could give rise to the ultra-luminous X-ray sources observed in nearby galactic discs.
They concluded that such PBHs could provide seeds for SMBHs in galactic nuclei but not all the DM.
Kawaguchi et al. Kawaguchi et al. 2008 considered the accretion of the interstellar medium by the intermediate mass PBHs formed in a double-inflation scenario.
More recently, Inoue and Kusenko Inoue and Kusenko 2017 have used existing X-ray data to constrain the PBH number density in the mass range from a few to 2 × 10 7 ​ M ⊙ 2\times 10^{7}\,M_{\odot}  .
Their limit is shown in Fig.  15 and marginally allows the black holes detected by LIGO/Virgo. 
 Lu et al.   Lu et al. 2021 use data from the Leo T dwarf galaxy to set a limit on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) in the mass range 1 ​ – ​ 10 7 ​ M ⊙ 1\text{--}10^{7}\,M_{\odot} by considering their heating of the interstellar medium by accretion, dynamical friction and disk outflows.
However, they make the strong assumption that dissipation by gas turbulence in Leo T induces a radiatively inefficient-accretion flow around a PBH, so their limit is only shown by a broken line in Fig.  15 . 
 Gaggero et al. Gaggero et al. 2017 model the accretion of gas onto a population of massive PBHs in the Milky Way and compare the predicted radio and X-ray emission with observational data.
With conservative assumptions about the accretion process, they use the VLA radio catalog at 1.4 GHz and the Chandra X-ray catalog to exclude 𝒪 ⁡ ( 10 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(10)\,M_{\odot} PBHs from providing all of the DM at the 5 ​ σ 5\sigma level.
More sensitive future radio and X-ray surveys could identify a PBH population.
Similar arguments have been made by Manshanden et al. Manshanden et al. 2019 , whose limit is also shown in Fig.  15 .
Hektor et al. Hektor et al. 2018b have considered the PBH constraints based on X-ray data from the Galactic centre and point out that all these accretion limits could be substantially weakened due to gas turbulence inside the interstellar medium and molecular clouds, this modifying the relative velocity between the PBHs and gas. 
 III.5.4 μ \mu distortion 
 If PBHs form from the high- σ \sigma tail of Gaussian density fluctuations, as in the simplest scenario Carr 1975 , then another interesting limit comes from the dissipation of those density fluctuations by Silk damping at a much later time.
This merely decreases the baryon-to-photon ratio for t < 7 × 10 6 ​ s t<7\times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{s} , with the success of the BBN scenario then giving a weak limit.
However, it leads to a μ \mu distortion in the CMB spectrum Chluba et al. 2012 for 7 × 10 6 ​ s < t < 3 × 10 9 ​ s 7\times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{s}<t<3\times 10^{9}\,\mathrm{s} , giving an upper limit δ ⁡ ( M ) < μ ∼ 10 − 2 \delta(M)<\sqrt{\mu}\sim 10^{-2} over the mass range 10 3 < M / M ⊙ < 10 12 10^{3}<M/M_{\odot}<10^{12} .
This limit was first given in Ref.  Carr and Lidsey 1993 , based on a result in Ref.  Barrow and Coles 1991 , but the limit on μ \mu is now stronger and will become still stronger in the future  Chluba et al. ; Delabrouille et al. .
There is also a y y distortion for 3 × 10 9 ​ s < t < 3 × 10 12 ​ s 3\times 10^{9}\,\mathrm{s}<t<3\times 10^{12}\,\mathrm{s} (the time of decoupling) but this only applies for much larger masses and is not discussed here. 
 In principle, limits on the μ \mu distortion can be translated into a very strong constraint on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) in the range 10 4 < M / M ⊙ < 10 12 10^{4}<M/M_{\odot}<10^{12} Kohri et al. 2014 .
However, the assumption that the fluctuations are Gaussian may be incorrect.
For example, Nakama et al. Nakama et al. 2016 have proposed a “patch” model, in which the relationship between the background inhomogeneities and the overdensity in the tiny fraction of volumes collapsing to PBHs is modified.
Kawasaki and Murai Kawasaki and Murai 2019 have another non-Gaussian model, in which PBH seeds for SMBHs are produced by inhomogeneous baryogenesis in a modified Affleck–Dine mechanism.
The μ \mu distortion constraint could thus be much weaker and one needs to consider its dependence on the possible non-Gaussianity of primordial fluctuations.
A phenomenological description of such non-Gaussianity was introduced in Ref.  Nakama et al. 2016 and involves a parameter p p , such that the dispersion of the primordial fluctuations becomes smaller as p p is reduced, thereby reducing the μ \mu distortion. 
 Recently, Nakama et al. Nakama et al. 2018 have calculated the μ \mu -distortion constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) for the p p model, using both the current FIRAS limit and the projected upper limit from PIXIE  Abitbol et al. 2017 .
However, one must be careful in the interpretation of M M here.
The diffusion mass is given by 
 M D ∼ M τ ​ M H ∼ { 10 10 ​ ( t / t eq ) 7 / 4 ​ M ⊙ ( t < t eq ) 10 13 ​ ( t / t dec ) 11 / 6 ​ M ⊙ ( t eq < t < t dec ) , M_{\mathrm{D}}\sim\sqrt{M_{\tau}\,M_{\mathrm{H}}}\sim\begin{cases}10^{10}\,(t/t_{\mathrm{eq}})^{7/4}\,M_{\odot}&(t<t_{\mathrm{eq}})\\
10^{13}\,(t/t_{\mathrm{dec}})^{11/6}\,M_{\odot}&(t_{\mathrm{eq}}<t<t_{\mathrm{dec}})\,,\end{cases} 
 (95) 
 where M H M_{\mathrm{H}} is the horizon mass and M τ M_{\tau} is the mass of unit optical depth to Thomson scattering.
A PBH has the horizon mass at formation and it subsequently remains constant, whereas the radiation mass is reduced by redshift effects.
One can show that the PBH and diffusion masses on a given scale are related by 
 M ∼ { 10 2 ​ ( M D / M ⊙ ) 6 / 7 ​ M ⊙ ( t < t eq ) 10 ​ ( M D / M ⊙ ) 10 / 11 ​ M ⊙ ( t eq < t < t dec ) . M\sim\begin{cases}10^{2}\,(M_{\mathrm{D}}/M_{\odot})^{6/7}\,M_{\odot}&(t<t_{\mathrm{eq}})\\
10\,(M_{\mathrm{D}}/M_{\odot})^{10/11}\,M_{\odot}&(t_{\mathrm{eq}}<t<t_{\mathrm{dec}})\,.\end{cases} 
 (96) 
 The probability distribution for the curvature perturbation ζ \zeta is taken to have the non-Gaussian form, 
 P ⁡ ( ζ ) = 1 2 ​ 2 ​ σ ~ ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + 1 / p ) ​ exp ⁡ [ − ( | ζ | 2 ​ σ ~ ) p ] , P(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\,\tilde{\sigma}\,\Gamma\left(1+1/p\right)}\,\exp\left[-\left(\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{2}\,\tilde{\sigma}}\right)^{p}\right]\,, 
 (97) 
 where σ ~ \tilde{\sigma} is the root-mean-square curvature fluctuation, this becoming Gaussian for p = 2 p=2 .
The collapse fraction is therefore 
 β = ∫ ζ c ∞ P ⁡ ( ζ ) ​ 𝑑 ζ = Γ ( 1 / p , 2 − p / 2 ( ζ c / σ ~ ) p ) 2 ​ p ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + 1 / p ) , \beta=\int_{\zeta_{c}}^{\infty}P(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta=\frac{\Gamma(1/p,2^{-p/2}(\zeta_{c}/\tilde{\sigma})^{p})}{2p\,\Gamma(1+1/p)}\,, 
 (98) 
 where ζ c \zeta_{c} is the threshold for PBH formation and Γ ⁡ ( a , z ) \Gamma(a,z) is the incomplete gamma function.
The μ \mu distortion is 
 μ ≃ 2.2 ​ σ 2 ​ [ exp ⁡ ( − k ^ ∗ 5400 ) − exp ⁡ ( − [ k ^ ∗ 31.6 ] 2 ) ] , \mu\simeq 2.2\,\sigma^{2}\,\biggl[\exp\biggl(-\frac{\hat{k}_{*}}{5400}\biggr)-\exp\biggl(-\biggl[\frac{\hat{k}_{*}}{31.6}\biggr]^{2}\biggr)\biggr]\,, 
 (99) 
 where k ^ ∗ \hat{k}_{*} is the wave-number in units of Mpc − 1 \mathrm{Mpc}^{-1} , this peaking at k = 80 ​ Mpc − 1 k=80\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1} or 3 × 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 3\times 10^{9}\,M_{\odot}  .
This is required to be less than 9 × 10 − 5 9\times 10^{-5} from FIRAS and 10 − 9 10^{-9} from HYPERPIXIE.
Equations ( 98 ) and ( 99 ) and the relation 
 k ≃ 7.5 × 10 5 γ 1 / 2 ( g ∗ 10.75 ) − 1 / 12 ( M 30 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 Mpc − 1 , k\simeq 7.5\times 10^{5}\,\gamma^{1/2}\,\left(\frac{g_{*}}{10.75}\right)^{-1/12}\,\left(\frac{M}{30\,M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}\,, 
 (100) 
 where the parameters γ \gamma and g ∗ g_{*} are defined in Sec.  I.3 , then lead to constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) and f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) . 
 The limit on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) is shown in Fig.  16 , and this indicates that the PBH density is severely constrained in the range 10 5 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 11 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{11}\,M_{\odot}  , with the mass range around 10 9 ​ M ⊙ 10^{9}\,M_{\odot} being most restricted.
We need a low value of p p (i.e. large non-Gaussianity) if such massive PBHs are to evade the μ \mu distortion constraints (cf. García-Bellido et al. 2017 ).
It would therefore be more plausible to invoke PBHs with initial masses below 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{5}\,M_{\odot} which undergo substantial accretion between the μ \mu -distortion era and the time of matter-radiation equality.
In particular, PBHs with 10 6 < M / M ⊙ < 10 10 10^{6}<M/M_{\odot}<10^{10} and f ∼ 10 − 4 f\sim 10^{-4} , such as are required to power quasars, require either p < 0.5 p<0.5 or an accretion factor of ( M / 10 5 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 (M/10^{5}\,M_{\odot})^{-1} . 
 Figure 16: Upper limits on f f from μ \mu constraints for different values of the non-Gaussianity parameter p p , from Ref.  Nakama et al. 2018 .
The solid curves are for FIRAS ( μ = 9 × 10 − 5 \mu=9\times 10^{-5} ) and correspond to p = 0.5 , 1 , 2 p=0.5,1,2 (from top to bottom).
The dashed and dotted curves are for PIXIE ( μ = 3.6 × 10 − 7 \mu=3.6\times 10^{-7} ) and HYPERPIXIE ( μ = 10 − 9 \mu=10^{-9} ), respectively, with the same values of p p from top to bottom.
The SINGLE line corresponds to the incredulity limit of one PBH per Hubble volume. 
 III.6 Gravitational wave constraints 
 In recent years interest in PBHs has intensified because of the detection of gravitational waves (GWs) from coalescing binary black holes by LIGO/Virgo Abbott et al. 2016a ; Abbott et al. 2016b ; Abbott et al. 2016c ; Abbott et al. 2019b and the possibility that these might be of primordial rather than stellar origin.
Even if this is not the case, the observations place important constraints on the number of PBHs.
Indeed, as illustrated in Fig.  15 , the combination of the accretion and GW limits already makes it unlikely that the LIGO/Virgo black holes (even if primordial) could explain all the DM.
On the hand, if the PBHs have an extended mass function, the mass at which the GW signal peaks is expected to be larger than the mass at which most of the density resides, so this need not preclude PBHs providing the dark matter at a somewhat lower mass.
In this section, we discuss three different ways in which GW searches can constrain the PBH proposal. 
 III.6.1 Gravitational wave background 
 A population of black holes would be expected to generate a stochastic gravitational wave background (GWB) Carr 1980a .
This would be especially interesting if there were a population of binary black holes, coalescing at the present epoch due to gravitational-radiation losses.
This was first discussed by Bond and Carr Bond and Carr 1984 in the context of Population III black holes and by Nakamura et al.   Nakamura et al. 1997 and Ioka et al.   Ioka et al. 1999 in the context of PBHs.
However, the precise formation epoch of the holes is not crucial since the coalescences occur much later.
In either case, the black holes would be expected to cluster inside galactic halos (along with other forms of DM) and so the detection of the associated GWB would provide a probe of the halo distribution Inoue and Tanaka 2003 .
Dalianis and Kouvaris  Dalianis and Kouvaris have pointed out that regions forming PBHs in an early matter-dominated era should collapse asymmetrically to form pancakes and the modified form of GWB generated would be probed by future interferometers. 
 The LIGO data had already placed weak constraints on the PBH scenario over a decade ago Abbott et al. 2007 but the constraints are now much stronger.
A few years ago Raidal et al. Raidal et al. 2017 showed that the expected GWB could be detected by the LIGO/Virgo runs, with the data at that time already giving the strongest available constraint on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) in the mass range 0.5 ​ – ​ 30 ​ M ⊙ 0.5\text{--}30\,M_{\odot}  .
Indeed, Wang et al. Wang et al. 2018 showed that Advanced LIGO’s first run gave the best PBH constraint in the range 1 ​ – ​ 100 ​ M ⊙ 1\text{--}100\,M_{\odot}  .
However, as we will discuss shortly, Vaskonen and Veermäe Vaskonen and Veermäe 2020 showed that the merger rate of binaries would be significantly reduced for f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 due to perturbations in small halos.
They found a more robust limit from a potential non-detection of stochastic GWB in the LIGO/Virgo O2 data and this is shown by the upper broken line in Fig.  15 .
More recently, Kapadia et al.   Kapadia et al. 2020 ; Kapadia et al. 2021 have searched for a stochastic GWB in the LIGO’s second observing run and inferred upper limits on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) in the mass range 10 − 20 ​ – ​ 10 − 19 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-20}\text{--}10^{-19}\,M_{\odot}  .
Such PBHs should have evaporated by now but this implies a tight limit even if Hawking evaporation is somehow avoided.
Bartolo et al. Bartolo et al. 2020 have calculated the anisotropies and non-Gaussianity in a GWB generated by PBHs on account of propagation effects and conclude that PBHs cannot provide all the DM if these effects are large.
The non-Gaussianity required to avoid the μ \mu limit (discussed above) would result in more mergers and therefore increase the GWB  Young and Byrnes 2020 . 
 Hooper et al.   Hooper et al. consider a more exotic scenario in which the early Universe was dominated by PBHs with M < 5 × 10 8 ​ g M<5\times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{g} which form binaries and undergo merging before evaporation and prior to BBN.
The merged PBHs will possess substantial angular momentum, causing their evaporation to produce a significant background of high energy gravitons and their mergers to produce a high-frequency GWB.
Inomata et al.   Inomata et al. 2020 consider a similar scenario, with reheating through PBH evaporations changing the equation of state of the Universe much more suddenly than conventional particle decays.
This enhances the GWB and future observations could give a constraint β < 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 − 8 \beta<10^{-5}\text{--}10^{-8} for 10 3 ​ – ​ 10 5 ​ g 10^{3}\text{--}10^{5}\,\mathrm{g} if the PBH mass function is very narrow. 
 If PBHs form in an early matter-dominated era and eventually reheat the Universe by Hawking evaporation, then Domènech et al.   Domènech et al. 2021 and Papanikolaou et al.   Papanikolaou et al. 2021 argue that the density fluctuations associated with their inhomogeneous distribution will generate a GWB at the time of reheating.
BBN constraints on the GWB imply that the initial collapse fraction is below 10 − 4 10^{-4} but this limit decreases as M M increases and Papanikolaou et al. find it has the form 10 − 4 ( M / 10 9 g ) − 1 / 4 10^{-4}\,(M/10^{9}\,\mathrm{g})^{-1/4} .
Although this constraint is outside the mass range shown in Fig.  4 , Domènech et al. point out that the GWB is in the LIGO and DECIGO windows for M ∼ 10 4 ​ – ​ 10 8 ​ g M\sim 10^{4}\text{--}10^{8}\,\mathrm{g} , so this scenario could soon be testable. 
 Looking further to the future, Wang et al. Wang et al. 2020 point out that some PBHs may accumulate at the center of a galaxy and follow a prograde or retrograde orbit due to the gravity of the central SMBH.
If the PBH mass is 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(1)\,M_{\odot} or smaller, the incoherent superposition of all the extreme-mass-ratio inspirals in the Universe could then generate a GWB detectable by LISA.
In an extension of this argument, Kühnel et al.   Kühnel et al. 2020 investigate GW production by PBHs in the mass range 10 − 13 ​ – ​ 1 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-13}\text{--}1\,M_{\odot} orbiting a SMBH.
While the individual objects would be undetectable, the extended stochastic emission from a large number of them might be. 
 III.6.2 PBHs and the LIGO/Virgo events 
 During the first two LIGO/Virgo runs, 10 10 events were observed with component masses in the range 8 ​ – ​ 51 ​ M ⊙ 8\text{--}51\,M_{\odot}  , which is larger than originally predicted for black holes of stellar origin.
After the first detection, Bird et al.   Bird et al. 2016 claimed that the expected merger rate for PBHs providing the DM was compatible with the range 9 ​ – ​ 240 ​ Gpc − 3 ​ yr − 1 9\text{--}240\,\mathrm{Gpc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} obtained by the LIGO analysis and this was supported by other studies  Clesse and García-Bellido 2017 ; Blinnikov et al. 2016 .
However, Sasaki et al.   Sasaki et al. 2016 argued that the merger rate would be in tension with the CMB distortion constraints unless the PBHs provided only a small fraction of the DM.
This conclusion could be avoided if the LIGO/Virgo back holes derived from the accretion and merger of smaller PBHs Clesse and García-Bellido 2017 .
However, Eroshenko  Eroshenko 2018 argued that the PBH merger rates would be highly suppressed by tidal forces and inferred that the LIGO/Virgo results would allow only 0.1 % of the DM to be in PBHs. 
 A cleaner PBH signature would be a mass below 1 ​ M ⊙ 1\,M_{\odot} since such black holes definitely cannot result from stellar evolution.
However, the LIGO/Virgo runs have found no compact binary systems with component masses in the range 0.2 ​ – ​ 1.0 ​ M ⊙ 0.2\text{--}1.0\,M_{\odot} Abbott et al. 2018 .
Indeed, for monochromatic non-spinning PBHs, the merger rate of 0.2 ​ M ⊙ 0.2\,M_{\odot} and 1.0 ​ M ⊙ 1.0\,M_{\odot} binaries is constrained to be less than 3.7 × 10 5 ​ Gpc − 3 ​ yr − 1 3.7\times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{Gpc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} and 5.2 × 10 3 ​ Gpc − 3 ​ yr − 1 5.2\times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{Gpc}^{-3}\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1} , respectively.
This corresponds to at most 16 % or 2 % of the DM, respectively  Abbott et al. 2019a , this limit being shown in Fig.  15 .
Future sensitivities for mergers below 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(1)\,M_{\odot} are studied in Ref.  Wang et al. 2019 . 
 A crucial issue for the PBH model is whether the binaries form in the early Universe or after galaxy formation.
Sasaki et al.   Sasaki et al. 2016 argued that the first is more important unless the formation of binaries in the radiation-dominated era is significantly reduced for some reason.
Bird et al.   Bird et al. 2016 invoked the second mechanism and this is the basis of their claim that PBHs could provide all the DM.
However, Ali-Haïmoud et al. Ali-Haïmoud et al. 2017 , a subset of the same authors, calculated the binary merger rate resulting from gravitational capture in halos and concluded that this should be subdominant if binaries formed in the early Universe survive until the present, as suggested by their analytic estimates.
They found that tidal torquing by other PBHs and the halo field, which might reduce the surviving fraction, is much weaker than previously calculated.
Their limit from the LIGO/Virgo O1 data is f ⁡ ( M ) < 0.01 f(M)<0.01 for 10 ​ – ​ 300 ​ M ⊙ 10\text{--}300\,M_{\odot} PBHs but only the part above 100 ​ M ⊙ 100\,M_{\odot} is shown in Fig.  15 because the lower part is just a potential constraint. 
 Raidal et al. Raidal et al. 2017 studied the production and merging of PBH binaries for an extended PBH mass function and showed that it is possible to satisfy all current PBH constraints if this has a lognormal form.
However, this ignored the suppression of mergers due to the binaries being perturbed and they later considered the possibility of a clustered spatial distribution.
In this case, the probability distribution of orbital parameters is modified and binaries are disrupted when they get too close to other PBHs  Raidal et al. 2019 .
The merger estimates are not reliable when PBHs provide more than 10 % of the DM, in which case the constraint from the observed LIGO merger rate is strongest in the mass range 2 ​ – ​ 160 ​ M ⊙ 2\text{--}160\,M_{\odot}  .
Their conclusion is that the LIGO/Virgo events can result from PBH mergers but that the PBHs can only provide the DM if they have an extended mass function. 
 The simulations of Vaskonen and Veermäe  Vaskonen and Veermäe 2020 show that the fraction of disrupted initial binaries can be much larger than estimated in Ref.  Ali-Haïmoud et al. 2017 if PBHs make up a large fraction of DM.
Nevertheless, even for scenarios in which early substructure survives and PBH binary disruption is certain, the merger rate is still large enough to rule out PBH DM in some mass range.
This puts the LIGO/Virgo merger constraints shown by the lower broken line in Fig.  15 on a more solid footing.
However, this is only a potential limit since it assumes a small number of burst detections in the O2 survey.
Jedamzik  Jedamzik 2021 uses full numerical integration to study the evolution of binaries in clusters due to interactions with a third passing PBH.
His Monte-Carlo analysis shows that the formerly predicted merger rates are reduced by orders of magnitude due to such interactions. 
 The robustness of the LIGO/Virgo bounds on PBH of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 10 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(10)\,M_{\odot} depends on the accuracy with which the formation of PBH binaries in the early Universe can be described.
Ballesteros et al. Ballesteros et al. 2018 revisit the standard estimate of the merger rate, focusing on the spatial distribution of nearest neighbours and the initial clustering of PBHs associated with the primordial power spectrum.
They confirm the robustness of previous results for a narrow mass function and infer f ∼ 0.001 ​ – ​ 0.01 f\sim 0.001\text{--}0.01 .
PBH clustering might tighten this constraint but only for very broad mass functions, corresponding to bumps in the primordial power spectra extending over several decades.
Korol et al. Korol et al. 2020 investigate the formation of binaries in small clusters of 30 30 PBHs in the absence of initial binaries.
They find that the binaries act as heat sources for the cluster, increasing its velocity dispersion and inhibiting mergers through two-body captures. 
 Several authors have considered the PBH merger rate as a function of the binary mass ratio.
In particular, Gow et al. Gow et al. 2020 consider the sensitivity of LIGO for five different PBH mass functions, showing that the empirical preference for nearly equal-mass binaries in current data is consistent with the PBH hypothesis once observational selection effects are taken into account.
Young and Byrnes  Young and Byrnes 2020 find that the initial PBH clustering due to primordial non-Gaussianity increases the present merger rate, although there is a large theoretical uncertainty when the clustering is large.
Diego Diego 2020 has constrained PBHs of 5 ​ – ​ 50 ​ M ⊙ 5\text{--}50\,M_{\odot} from the gravitational lensing of GWs at the LIGO frequency.
For the magnifications expected for observed GW events, he finds that f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) can be constrained at the percent level.
Indeed, if a small fraction of the DM is in the form of microlenses of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 10 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(10)\,M_{\odot}  , all GWs will show interference effects at sufficiently large magnifications. 
 Most of the observed coalesced black holes have effective spins compatible with zero.
Although the statistical significance of this result is low, it goes against a stellar binary origin Gerosa et al. 2018 but is a prediction of the PBH scenario García-Bellido 2017 .
Fernandez et al. Fernandez and Profumo 2019 also argue that the spin information favours a primordial rather than astrophysical origin for the LIGO/Virgo events and they discuss the number of events necessary to acquire information on the relative fraction of primordial and non-primordial binary black holes.
De Luca et al.   De Luca et al. 2020b ; De Luca et al. 2020c study the evolution of the PBH spin and the corresponding probabilities for GW events.
In order to realize efficient PBH accretion, they take the lowest relative velocity between the PBH and baryon fluid.
They compute the best-fit PBH mass distribution at formation compatible with current GW data and infer that the maximum DM fraction is around 10 − 3 10^{-3} .
However, the fact that at least one component of GW190412 is moderately spinning is incompatible with a primordial origin unless accretion or hierarchical mergers are significant.
With accretion, the current PBH constraints are significantly relaxed and the GW bounds is the most stringent ones in the relevant mass range. 
 Carr et al.   Carr et al. 2021a argue that three recent LIGO/Virgo events - GW190425, GW190814 and GW190521  Abbott et al. 2020a ; Abbott et al. 2020b ; Abbott et al. 2020c ; Abbott et al. 2020d - may support a scenario in which the PBHs form at the QCD epoch.
On the other hand, Takhistov et al.   Takhistov et al. 2021 argue that the black holes in the range 1.5 ​ – ​ 2.6 ​ M ⊙ 1.5\text{--}2.6\,M_{\odot} required to explain GW190425 and GW190814 could be produced via neutron star implosions induced by capture of small PBHs, with the mass distribution of such “transmuted” BHs following that of the neutron stars.
De Luca et al. De Luca et al. 2021a argue that GW190521 can only be explained within the PBH scenario without violating other constraints if the PBHs accrete efficiently before the reionisation epoch.
Inomata  Inomata 2021 argues that the power spectrum of scalar perturbations required to produce such PBHs through the non-linear interaction appearing in perturbations could avoid the BBN and CMB μ \mu -distortion constraints. 
 Miller et al.   Miller et al. argue that current searches for continuous waves from PBH binaries imply f < 1 f<1 for chirp masses in the range 4 × 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ 4\times 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{-3}\,M_{\odot} but that one could push this limit down to f < 10 − 2 f<10^{-2} with the Einstein Telescope.
Herman et al.   Herman et al. have discussed the detection of high-frequency GWs from planetary-mass PBH binaries with electromagnetic detectors.
This would allow the detection of PBH binary mergers with mass around 10 − 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-5}\,M_{\odot} if f ∼ 10 − 4 f\sim 10^{-4} .
Of course, the LIGO/Virgo events could be a mixture of PBHs and stellar black holes and Hütsi et al.   Hütsi et al. 2021 argue that PBHs alone are unlikely to explain the data. 
 III.6.3 Constraints from second order tensor perturbations 
 A different type of GW constraint on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) , first pointed out by Saito and Yokoyama Saito and Yokoyama 2009 ; Saito and Yokoyama 2010 , arises because of the second order tensor perturbations generated by the scalar perturbations which produce the PBHs, the associated frequency being 10 − 10 ( M / 10 3 M ⊙ ) − 1 / 2 Hz 10^{-10}\,(M/10^{3}M_{\odot})^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{Hz} .
Such a background could be detected by space-based interferometers in the planetary mass range and by pulsar timing in the intermediate mass range, these being the two ranges in which PBHs might provide the DM.
The limit on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) relates to the amplitude of the density fluctuations at the horizon epoch and the associated constraints are shown in Fig.  17 .
Conversely, one can use PBH limits to constrain the primordial GWB Nakama and Suyama 2015 ; Nakama and Suyama 2016 .
