 These effects have subsequently been studied by many other authors Bugaev and Klimai 2010 ; Assadullahi and Wands 2010 ; Bugaev and Klimai 2011 ; Alabidi et al. 2012 .
In particular, Orlofsky et al. Orlofsky et al. 2017 claim that PTA measurements can be used to probe whether the LIGO/Virgo mergers were from inflationary PBHs, while Ballesteros et al.   Ballesteros et al. 2020b claim that PBHs in the asteroid mass range might be detected by Earth-based interferometers.
The modifications required if the PBHs form in a matter-dominated rather than radiaton-dominated era have been discussed in Refs.  Alabidi et al. 2013 ; Kohri and Terada 2018a ; Inomata et al. 2019a ; Inomata et al. 2019b .
The spectrum of the induced GWB is also important.
For a wide class of perturbations giving rise to PBHs, the infrared behavior of the power spectrum of the GWB may have the general form Ω GW ∝ k 3 \Omega_{\mathrm{GW}}\propto k^{3} for a wide peak and Ω GW ∝ k 2 \Omega_{\mathrm{GW}}\propto k^{2} for a narrow peak Cai et al. 2020 .
However, Yuan et al.   Yuan et al. 2020 find a log-dependent slope, with the k 3 k^{3} or k 2 k^{2} parts being highly suppressed. 
 These analyses apply only if the PBHs are generated by Gaussian fluctuations.
Although this is questionable for the large-amplitude fluctuations relevant to PBH formation  Hidalgo ; Hidalgo 2009 , the non-Gaussian effects are not expected to be large  Saito et al. 2008 .
However, even if the scalar curvature perturbations have a subdominant non-Gaussian component, this can still be the dominant source of induced GWs  Unal 2019 , so this has motivated studies of the detectability of the GWB non-Gaussianity.
For example, if PBHs provide the DM in the window around 10 21 ​ g 10^{21}\,\mathrm{g} , Bartolo et al. Bartolo et al. 2019a ; Bartolo et al. 2019b show that LISA will be able to detect the GW power spectrum but not the bispectrum.
A related point has been considered by Cai et al. Cai et al. 2019a , who find that GWB induced by non-Gaussian curvature perturbations can exceed that induced by the Gaussian part.
If PBHs with masses of 10 20 ​ – ​ 10 22 ​ g 10^{20}\text{--}10^{22}\,\mathrm{g} provide the DM, they find that the corresponding GWs will be detectable by LISA, irrespective of the value of f NL f_{\mathrm{NL}}  .
However, Cai et al. Cai et al. 2019b argue that the PTA constraints can be relieved if the perturbations are locally non-Gaussian with f NL ≳ 𝒪 ⁡ ( 10 ) f_{\mathrm{NL}}\gtrsim\mathcal{O}(10) .
They conclude that the rate of PBH mergers is at least an order of magnitude smaller than previously suggested, so that GW constraints on the PBH contribution to the DM are relieved. 
 Chen et al. Chen et al. 2020 have obtained PBH limits by searching for induced GWs in the 11-year PTA data set of the North American Nanohertz Observatory for Gravitational waves (NANOGrav).
They infer f < 1 f<1 in the range 0.0004 ​ – ​ 2 ​ M ⊙ 0.0004\text{--}2\,M_{\odot} and f < 10 − 6 f<10^{-6} in the range 0.002 ​ – ​ 0.4 ​ M ⊙ 0.002\text{--}0.4\,M_{\odot} and this constraint is shown in Fig.  17 .
However, the NANOGrav collaboration have recently claimed a detection.
Six groups have suggested that this could be the second order background associated with PBHs but they advocate very different mass-scales:
Kohri and Terada  Kohri and Terada 2021 advocate 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ M ⊙ \mathcal{O}(1)\,M_{\odot} PBHs with f = 10 − 3 f=10^{-3} ;
De Luca et al.   De Luca et al. 2021b argue that f ∼ 1 f\sim 1 can be realised in the mass range M ∼ 10 − 15 ​ – ​ 10 − 11 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 10^{-15}\text{--}10^{-11}\,M_{\odot} if the PBHs have a broad mass spectrum;
Vaskonen and Veermäe  Vaskonen and Veermäe 2021 argue that 10 3 ​ – ​ 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{3}\text{--}10^{6}\,M_{\odot} PBHs can seed SMBHs and fit the GW signal but that a wider mass range of 10 − 1 ​ – ​ 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-1}\text{--}10^{6}\,M_{\odot} is also consistent with the data at the 2 ​ σ 2\sigma level for f < 0.01 f<0.01 ;
Domènech et al.   Domènech and Pi argue that a flat secondary GW spectrum fits the NANOGrav data and that this is expected if the PBHs form in a matter-dominated early Universe with mass in the range 10 − 5 ​ – ​ 10 − 1 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-5}\text{--}10^{-1}\,M_{\odot} ;
Atal et al.   Atal et al. argue that PBHs in the range 10 11 ​ – ​ 10 12 ​ M ⊙ 10^{11}\text{--}10^{12}\,M_{\odot} with f ∼ 10 − 3 f\sim 10^{-3} could explain the signal, with the μ \mu distortion limits being avoided if they arise from non-Gaussian fluctuations;
Sugiyama et al.   Sugiyama et al. 2021 argue that sublunar PBHs arising from inflation could generate the NANOGrav signal, pointing out that the PBHs could also be probed by the Subaru observations discussed in Sec.  III.2.2 .
Even if the NANOGrav detection is real, there is clearly considerable ambiguity in its interpretation. 
 Figure 17: Constraints on f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) from observations of various kinds of background radiation:
second-order gravitational waves from pulsar timing  Saito and Yokoyama 2009 and NANOGrav  Chen et al. 2020 , the CMB μ \mu distortion Nakama et al. 2018 and the n / p n/p ratio Inomata et al. 2016 . 
 IV Constraints for extended PBH mass functions and mixed dark matter 
 Our master diagram of all the β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) constraints for a monochromatic PBH mass function is shown in Fig.  18 .
For each mass range, this gives the strongest limit shown in Fig.  4 for evaporating PBHs and in Fig.  10 for non-evaporating ones, where we have used relation ( 57 ) in the latter case to convert from f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) to β ′ ​ ( M ) \beta^{\prime}(M) .
The lines below 10 9 ​ g 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} are shown dotted since they involve less secure assumptions (Planck relics, LSP particles, inflation) than the ones at higher mass.
The relic and entropy constraints come from Eqs. ( 55 ) and ( 24 ), respectively, while the LSP constraint comes from Eq. ( 49 ) and is shown in Fig.  4 .
The vertical ‘inflation’ line at M ∼ 1 ​ g M\sim 1\,\mathrm{g} corresponds to the maximum reheat temperature of 10 16 ​ GeV 10^{16}\,\mathrm{GeV} implied by the CMB quadrupole anisotropy, no PBHs being expected below this mass.
The vertical line at 10 9 ​ g 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} corresponds to the lower bound on the reheat temperature, T R > 5 ​ MeV T_{\mathrm{R}}>5\,\mathrm{MeV} , obtained from BBN  Hasegawa et al. 2019 , although this analysis applies only for β ′ ​ ( M ) > 10 − 14 \beta^{\prime}(M)>10^{-14} . 
 If the PBHs form from primordial inhomogeneities and the horizon-scale fluctuations have a Gaussian distribution with dispersion σ \sigma , then one expects the fraction of horizon patches collapsing to a black hole to be Carr 1975 
 β ≈ Erfc ⁡ [ δ c 2 ​ σ ] . \beta\approx\mathrm{Erfc}\!\left[\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{c}}}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma}\right]\,. 
 (101) 
 Here ‘Erfc’ is the complementary error function and δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} is the density contrast required for PBH formation.
This implies the limits on the primordial power spectrum 𝒫 ⁡ ( k ) \mathcal{P}(k) shown in Fig.  19 .
However, this does not include the constraints shown in Fig.  17 because these are not direct PBH limits.
For example, the μ \mu -distortion constraints imply limits on both PBHs and the power spectrum, whereas observational limits on PBHs directly constrain the power spectrum in the other cases.
There are many other constraints on 𝒫 ⁡ ( k ) \mathcal{P}(k) but the PBH limits provide the most stringent ones on small scales and indeed cover a range of k k not probed by any other type of observation. 
 Figure 18: Combined constraints on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) for a monochromatic PBH mass function.
For each mass range, this gives the strongest limit for either evaporating and non-evaporating PBHs.
As discussed in the text, the dotted lines below 10 9 ​ g 10^{9}\,\mathrm{g} involve less secure assumptions.
No PBHs form to the left of the ‘inflation’ line, so this is a theoretical rather than observational constraint. 
 If the primordial fluctuations can be described by a scalar spectral index and its running at the CMB scale  Li and Huang 2018 , then Fig.  19 implies constraints on these two parameters.
However, the situation may be more complicated than this since most models for PBH formation from inflation do not predict a simple power law with running and some require a spike in the power-spectrum.
Also Eq. ( 101 ) may not apply in a more detailed analysis  Akrami et al. 2018 .
In particular, Kalaja et al. Kalaja et al. 2019 have used results from numerical simulations and peak theory to study PBH formation for a range of perturbation profiles, including a careful treatment of non-linearities and smoothing and filtering scales.
Sato-Polito et al. Sato-Polito et al. 2019 incorporate uncertainties in the critical overdensity δ c \delta_{\mathrm{c}} associated with ellipsoidal collapse.
Other relevant results can be found in Refs.  Yoo et al. 2018 ; Young and Musso 2020 for peak theory, in Refs.  Germani and Sheth 2020 ; Suyama and Yokoyama 2020 ; Yoo et al. 2019 for some extensions of peak theory, in Ref.  Ando et al. 2018 ; Young 2019 for different choices of the window function and in Refs.  Yoo et al. 2018 ; Germani and Musco 2019 ; Kawasaki and Nakatsuka 2019 for various non-linear relations between the curvature and density perturbations.
Finally, Eq. ( 101 ) does not apply for PBHs which form in a matter-dominated era or through some other mechanism unrelated to primordial inhomogeneities. 
 Figure 19: Constraints on power spectrum 𝒫 ⁡ ( k ) \mathcal{P}(k) implied by Figs.  4 and 10 and Eq. ( 101 ) for Gaussian fluctuations. 
 The constraints shown in Fig.  18 assume that the PBH mass function is quasi-monochromatic (i.e. with a width Δ ​ M ∼ M \Delta M\sim M ).
However, in many scenarios one would expect the mass function to be extended, specific examples being discussed below.
In the context of the DM problem, this is a two-edged sword Carr et al. 2016a .
On the one hand, it means that the total PBH density may suffice to explain the DM, even if the density in any particular mass band is small and within the observational bounds discussed in Sec.  III .
On the other hand, even if PBHs can provide all the DM at some mass scale without violating the constraints there, the extended mass function may still violate the constraints at some other scale.
This problem is particularly pertinent if the mass function extends over many decades. 
 A detailed assessment of this problem requires a knowledge of the expected PBH mass fraction, f exp ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{exp}}(M) , and the maximum allowed fraction, f max ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{max}}(M) , in the monochromatic case.
However, the procedure is non-trivial even when these functions are known.
In particular, one cannot just plot f exp ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{exp}}(M) for a given model in Fig.  10 and infer that the model is allowed because it does not intersect f max ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{max}}(M) , even though this procedure is sometimes used in the literature.
If the PBHs span an extended range of masses, the differential mass function is written as d ​ n / d ​ M \mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}M where d ​ n \mathrm{d}n is the number density of PBHs in the mass range ( M , M + d ​ M ) (M,M+\mathrm{d}M) .
One can then define the mass density and DM fraction of PBHs with mass ‘around’ M M as 
 ρ ⁡ ( M ) ≡ M 2 ​ d ​ n d ​ M , f ⁡ ( M ) ≡ ρ ⁡ ( M ) ρ CDM . \rho(M)\equiv M^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\,,\quad f(M)\equiv\frac{\rho(M)}{\rho_{\mathrm{CDM}}}\,. 
 (102) 
 Alternatively, one can define n ⁡ ( M ) n(M) , ρ ⁡ ( M ) \rho(M) and f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) as integrated values for PBHs with mass larger or smaller than M M .
However, these are only simply related to the functions in Eq. ( 102 ) for a power-law spectrum. 
 One approach, adopted by Carr et al. in Ref.  Carr et al. 2016a , is to study the possible DM windows by breaking up each constraint into narrow mass bins and treating it as a sequence of flat constraints.
However, this is quite a complicated procedure and it has attracted some criticism from Green Green 2016 .
A more elegant methodology has been adopted by Carr et al. in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a .
In this, one introduces the distribution function for log ⁡ M \log{M} , 
 ψ ⁡ ( M ) ∝ M ​ d ​ n d ​ M , \psi(M)\propto M\,\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}M}\,, 
 (103) 
 normalised so that the total fraction of the DM in PBHs is 
 f PBH ≡ Ω PBH Ω CDM = ∫ M min M max d ​ M ​ ψ ​ ( M ) . f_{\mathrm{PBH}}\equiv\frac{\Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}}{\Omega_{\mathrm{CDM}}}=\int_{M_{\mathrm{min}}}^{M_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathrm{d}M\,\psi(M)\,. 
 (104) 
 The lower cut-off in the mass integral necessarily exceeds M ∗ ≈ 4 × 10 14 ​ g M_{*}\approx 4\times 10^{14}\,\mathrm{g} , the mass of the PBHs evaporating at the present epoch Carr et al. 2010a .
We follow the discussion of Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a below, although other approaches have been used by Bellomo et al.   Bellomo et al. 2018 and De Luca et al.   De Luca et al. 2020d . 
 IV.1 Possible PBH mass functions 
 Reference  Carr et al. 2017a considers four types of mass function, which we summarise briefly here. 
 1. 
 A lognormal mass function of the form 
 ψ ⁡ ( M ) = f PBH 2 ​ π ​ σ ​ M ​ exp ⁡ ( − log 2 ⁡ ( M / M c ) 2 ​ σ 2 ) , \psi(M)=\frac{f_{\mathrm{PBH}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma M}\,\exp\left(-\frac{\log^{2}(M/M_{c})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\,, 
 (105) 
 where M c M_{c} is the mass at which the function M ​ ψ ​ ( M ) M\psi(M) peaks and σ \sigma is its width.
This is a good approximation if the PBHs result from a smooth symmetric peak in the inflationary power spectrum.
It was first suggested in Ref.  Dolgov and Silk 1993 and then demonstrated numerically in Ref.  Green 2016 and analytically in Ref.  Kannike et al. 2017 for the slow-roll case.
For a given value of f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}}  , the mass function is therefore described by two parameters.
Note that the lognormal mass function used in Refs.  Green 2016 ; Horowitz ; Kühnel and Freese 2017 omits the M − 1 M^{-1} term in Eq. ( 105 ), in which case the function M ​ ψ ​ ( M ) M\psi(M) peaks at e σ 2 ​ M c e^{\sigma^{2}}M_{c} rather than M c M_{c}  .
The form ( 105 ) is more useful for our purposes.
However, Gow et al.   Gow et al. point out that a narrow peak in the power spectrum is more likely to produce a skew-lognormal mass function. 
 2. 
 A power-law mass function of the form 
 ψ ⁡ ( M ) ∝ M γ − 1 ( M min < M < M max ) . \psi(M)\propto M^{\gamma-1}\quad(M_{\mathrm{min}}<M<M_{\mathrm{max}})\,. 
 (106) 
 For γ ≠ 0 \gamma\neq 0 , either the lower or upper cut-off can be neglected if M min ≪ M max M_{\mathrm{min}}\ll M_{\mathrm{max}}  , so this scenario is effectively described by two parameters.
Only in the γ = 0 \gamma=0 case are both cut-offs important.
A mass function of this form arises naturally if the PBHs form from scale-invariant density fluctuations or from the collapse of cosmic strings.
In both cases, γ = − 2 w / ( 1 + w ) \gamma=-2w/(1+w) , where w w specifies the equation of state, p = w ​ ρ ​ c 2 p=w\,\rho\,c^{2} , when the PBHs form Carr 1975 .
In a non-inflationary model, w ∈ ( − 1 / 3 , 1 ) w\in(-1/3,1) and γ ∈ ( − 1 , 1 ) \gamma\in(-1,1) , so the exponent in Eq. ( 106 ) is always negative.
Inflation itself corresponds to w ∈ ( − 1 , − 1 / 3 ) w\in(-1,-1/3) and γ ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) \gamma\in(1,\infty) but Eq. ( 106 ) is inapplicable in this case since PBHs do not form during inflation but only afterwards from the inflationary fluctuations. 
 3. 
 A critical collapse mass function of the form 
 ψ ⁡ ( M ) ∝ M 2.85 ​ exp ⁡ ( − ( M / M f ) 2.85 ) , \psi(M)\propto M^{2.85}\,\exp(-(M/M_{f})^{2.85})\,, 
 (107) 
 where the exponent 2.85 assumes radiation-domination  Yokoyama 1998a ; Niemeyer and Jedamzik 1999 ; Musco and Miller 2013 ; Carr et al. 2016b .
This applies generically if the PBHs form from density fluctuations with a δ \delta -function power spectrum.
In this case, the mass spectrum extends down to arbitrarily low masses but there is an exponential upper cut-off at the mass-scale M f M_{f}  , which is roughly to the horizon mass at the collapse epoch.
Although the mass function is described by a single parameter, Eq. ( 107 ) must be modified if the density fluctuations are themselves extended  Carr et al. 2016a , as expected in the inflationary scenario.
Indeed, the lognormal distribution may then be appropriate, so two parameters may be required in the more realistic critical collapse situation. 
 4. 
 Special consideration is required in the w = 0 w=0 (matter-dominated) case  Polnarev and Khlopov 1985 , because both cut-offs in ( 106 ) can then be relevant.
For example, this may arise due to some form of phase transition in which the mass is channeled into non-relativistic particles Khlopov and Polnarev 1980 ; Polnarev and Khlopov 1981 or due to slow reheating after inflation Carr et al. 1994 .
Since Eq. ( 106 ) predicts γ = 0 \gamma=0 in this case, ρ ⁡ ( M ) \rho(M) should increase logarithmically with M M .
However, the analysis breaks down for this scenario because the Jeans length is much smaller than the particle horizon, so collapse is prevented by deviations from spherical symmetry rather than pressure and the probability of PBH formation becomes 
 β ⁡ ( M ) ≈ 0.056 ​ δ H ​ ( M ) 5 . \beta(M)\approx 0.056\,\delta_{H}(M)^{5}\,. 
 (108) 
 This is small for δ H ​ ( M ) ≪ 1 \delta_{H}(M)\ll 1 but much larger than the exponentially suppressed fraction in the radiation-dominated case.
The factor δ H ​ ( M ) 5 \delta_{H}(M)^{5} was first derived in Refs.  Khlopov and Polnarev 1980 ; Polnarev and Khlopov 1981 and is in agreement with the more recent analysis of Ref.  Harada et al. 2016 .
As discussed in Sec.  I.2.1 , there may be a correction factor which scales as δ H ​ ( M ) 3 / 2 \delta_{H}(M)^{3/2} but we will neglect that here.
We also need to consider the effect of spin  Kohri and Terada 2018b because this is inevitably produced during collapse in a matter-dominated period and can exponentially suppress PBH production for smaller density perturbations  Harada et al. 2017 .
If the matter-dominated phase extends from t 1 t_{1} to t 2 t_{2}  , PBH formation is enhanced over the mass range 
 M min ∼ M H ​ ( t 1 ) < M < M max ∼ M H ​ ( t 2 ) ​ δ H ​ ( M max ) 3 / 2 . M_{\mathrm{min}}\sim M_{H}(t_{1})<M<M_{\mathrm{max}}\sim M_{H}(t_{2})\,\delta_{H}(M_{\mathrm{max}})^{3/2}\,. 
 (109) 
 The lower limit is the horizon mass at the start of matter-dominance and the upper limit is the horizon mass at the epoch when the regions which bind at the end of matter-dominance enter the horizon.
For nearly scale-invariant primordial fluctuations, β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) is constant and f ⁡ ( M ) f(M) is logarithmic, but the mass function is still determined by two parameters, which can be taken to be t 1 t_{1} and t 2 t_{2} Carr et al. 2017b . 
 To compare with the lognormal case, we describe the mass function in the first three cases by the mean and variance of the log ⁡ M \log M distribution: 
 log ⁡ M c ≡ ⟨ log ⁡ M ⟩ ψ , σ 2 ≡ ⟨ log 2 ⁡ M ⟩ ψ − ⟨ log ⁡ M ⟩ ψ 2 , \log M_{c}\equiv\langle\log M\rangle_{\psi}\,,\quad\sigma^{2}\equiv\langle\log^{2}M\rangle_{\psi}-\langle\log M\rangle_{\psi}^{2}\,, 
 (110) 
 where 
 ⟨ X ⟩ ψ ≡ f PBH − 1 ​ ∫ d ​ M ​ ψ ​ ( M ) ​ X ​ ( M ) . \langle X\rangle_{\psi}\equiv f_{\mathrm{PBH}}^{-1}\,\int\mathrm{d}M\,\psi(M)\,X(M)\,. 
 (111) 
 For a power-law distribution these are 
 M c = M cut e − 1 / γ , σ = 1 / | γ | , M_{c}=M_{\mathrm{cut}}\,e^{-1/\gamma},\quad\sigma=1/|\gamma|\,, 
 (112) 
 where M cut ≡ max ⁡ ( M min , M ∗ ) M_{\mathrm{cut}}\equiv\mathrm{max}(M_{\mathrm{min}},M_{*}) if γ < 0 \gamma<0 or M max M_{\mathrm{max}} if γ > 0 \gamma>0 .
For the critical-collapse distribution, the exponential cut-off is very sharp, so the mass function is well approximated by a power-law distribution with γ = 3.85 \gamma=3.85 and M max ≈ M f M_{\mathrm{max}}\approx M_{f}  .
As it is relatively narrow, Eq. ( 112 ) implying σ = 0.26 \sigma=0.26 , even the monochromatic mass function provides a good fit.
Since critical collapse should be a fairly generic feature of PBH formation, σ = 0.26 \sigma=0.26 always provides a lower limit to the width.
However, critical collapse may not be relevant in the cosmic string or matter-dominated scenarios. 
 One might expect two parameters to suffice to describe the PBH mass function locally (i.e. close to a peak) since this just corresponds to the first two terms in a Taylor expansion.
However, the mass function could be more complicated than this and might have several peaks.
For example, Hasegawa et al. Hasegawa and Kawasaki 2018 have proposed a scenario in the minimally supersymmetric standard model which generates both intermediate-mass PBHs to explain the LIGO detections and lunar-mass PBHs to explain the DM.
Kusenko et al. Kusenko et al. 2020 envisage PBH production through the nucleation of false vacuum bubbles during inflation, this simultaneously accounting for the DM, the HSC lensing candidate, the LIGO/Virgo black holes and the seeds for SMBHs.
Carr et al.   Carr et al. 2021a have a scenario in which dips in the pressure during the thermal history of the Universe naturally generate four peaks in the PBH mass function, this explaining an even greater variety of cosmological conundra. 
 The following analysis does not apply in these situations. 
 IV.2 Constraints for extended PBH mass function 
 Reference  Carr et al. 2017a introduces a general method for dealing with extended mass functions.
Any astrophysical observable A ⁡ [ ψ ⁡ ( M ) ] A[\psi(M)] depending on the PBH abundance (e.g. the number of ML events of given duration in a given time interval) can be expanded as 
 A ⁡ [ ψ ⁡ ( M ) ] = A 0 + ∫ d ​ M ​ ψ ​ ( M ) ​ K 1 ​ ( M ) + ∫ d ​ M 1 ​ d ​ M 2 ​ ψ ​ ( M 1 ) ​ ψ ​ ( M 2 ) ​ K 2 ​ ( M 1 , M 2 ) + … , A[\psi(M)]=A_{0}+\int\mathrm{d}M\,\psi(M)\,K_{1}(M)+\int\mathrm{d}M_{1}\mathrm{d}M_{2}\,\psi(M_{1})\,\psi(M_{2})\,K_{2}(M_{1},M_{2})+\ldots\,, 
 (113) 
 where A 0 A_{0} is the background contribution and the functions K j K_{j} depend on the details of the underlying physics and the nature of the observation.
If PBHs with different mass contribute independently to the observable, only the first two terms in Eq. ( 113 ) need to be considered.
Explicit expressions are given for lensing and survival of stars in Ref.  Green 2016 , for evaporation in Ref.  Carr et al. 2016a and for neutron star capture and accretion in Ref.  Kühnel and Freese 2017 . 
 If a measurement puts an upper bound on the observable, 
 A ⁡ [ ψ ⁡ ( M ) ] ≤ A exp , A[\psi(M)]\leq A_{\mathrm{exp}}\,, 
 (114) 
 then for a monochromatic mass function with M = M c M=M_{c} we have 
 ψ mon ​ ( M ) ≡ f PBH ​ ( M c ) ​ δ ​ ( M − M c ) . \psi_{\mathrm{mon}}(M)\equiv f_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M_{c})\,\delta(M-M_{c})\,. 
 (115) 
 The maximum allowed fraction of DM in PBHs of mass M c M_{c} is then 
 f PBH ​ ( M c ) ≤ A exp − A 0 K 1 ​ ( M c ) ≡ f max ​ ( M c ) . f_{\mathrm{PBH}}(M_{c})\leq\frac{A_{\mathrm{exp}}-A_{0}}{K_{1}(M_{c})}\equiv f_{\mathrm{max}}(M_{c})\,. 
 (116) 
 Combining Eqs. ( 113 )–( 116 ) then yields 
 ∫ d ​ M ​ ψ ⁡ ( M ) f max ​ ( M ) ≤ 1 . \int\mathrm{d}M\,\frac{\psi(M)}{f_{\mathrm{max}}(M)}\leq 1\,. 
 (117) 
 Once f max f_{\mathrm{max}} is known, we can apply Eq. ( 117 ) to obtain the constraint for an arbitrary mass function ψ ⁡ ( M ) \psi(M) .
We first integrate Eq. ( 117 ) over the mass range ( M 1 , M 2 M_{1},M_{2} ) for which the constraint applies, assuming a particular function ψ ⁡ ( M , f PBH , M c , σ ) \psi(M;f_{\mathrm{PBH}},M_{c},\sigma) .
After specifying M 1 M_{1} and M 2 M_{2}  , this constrains f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} as a function of M c M_{c} and σ \sigma .
The procedure must be implemented separately for each observable.
Different constraints can be combined by using the relation 
 ∑ j = 1 N ( ∫ d ​ M ​ ψ ⁡ ( M ) f max , j ​ ( M ) ) 2 ≤ 1 , \sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\int\mathrm{d}M\,\frac{\psi(M)}{f_{\mathrm{max},j}(M)}\right)^{2}\leq 1\,, 
 (118) 
 where f max , j ​ ( M ) f_{\mathrm{max},j}(M) correspond to the different bounds for a monochromatic mass function, as defined by Eq. ( 116 ).
Most constraints rely on a single observable, such as the number of lensing events or the abundance of wide binaries. 
 The constraints on the allowed DM fraction in PBHs are shown in Fig.  20 , which is an updated version of Fig. 1 in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a .
The left panel shows the constraint for a monochromatic mass function and corresponds to a subset of the constraints in Fig.  10 for the mass range 10 − 18 ​ – ​ 10 5 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-18}\text{--}10^{5}\,M_{\odot}  .
The most restrictive forms for the constraints are used and some are depicted by the dotted lines.
The right panel shows the equivalent constraints for a lognormal mass function with σ = 2 \sigma=2 and a general value of M c M_{c}  .
We do not show the limits for a power-law mass function, since we only wish to demonstrate the methodology, but these are given in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a for an earlier form of the monochromatic constraints.
The important qualitative point is that the form of the constraints in this case is itself dependent on the PBH mass function.
One cannot just compare a predicted extended mass function with the monochromatic form of the constraints. 
 Figure  21 is an update of Fig. 2 in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a and shows the maximum allowed DM fraction, f max f_{\mathrm{max}}  , in the ( M c , σ M_{c}\,,\sigma ) plane for lognormal PBH mass function.
The constraints have been combined using Eq. ( 118 ).
The region where 100 % of DM can consist of PBHs is indicated by the dashed white contour.
The shape of the constraints in Fig.  21 makes it clear that the allowed mass range for fixed f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} decreases with increasing width σ \sigma , thus ruling out the possibility of evading the constraints by simply extending the mass function.
Moreover, Fig.  21 gives an upper bound σ ≲ 2.4 \sigma\lesssim 2.4 if all the DM is in the form of PBHs.
For a power-law mass function, Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a conclude | γ | ≳ 1 |\gamma|\gtrsim 1 , which effectively rules out PBH DM from the collapse of cosmic strings or scale-invariant density fluctuations.
This agrees with the conclusions of Refs.  Green 2016 ; Horowitz ; Kühnel and Freese 2017 .
The last paper provides a more comprehensive analysis, covering the mass range 10 − 18 ​ – ​ 10 4 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-18}\text{--}10^{4}\,M_{\odot}  , but it does not include the recent HSC constraint Niikura et al. 2019b and it uses the unnecessarily stringent Planck constraint from Ref.  Chen et al. 2016 . 
 Figure 20: Constraints on the fraction of PBH dark matter, f PBH ≡ Ω PBH / Ω DM f_{\mathrm{PBH}}\equiv\Omega_{\mathrm{PBH}}/\Omega_{\mathrm{DM}}  , as a function of the PBH mass M c M_{c} ; this is an updated version of Fig. 1 in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a (Raidal, Vaskonen & Veermae, private communication).
 Left panel : This assumes a monochromatic mass function and corresponds to a subset of the constraints in Fig.  10 .
The purple region is excluded by evaporations Carr et al. 2010a , the blue, violet, yellow, brown, purple and dark blue regions by the microlensing results from HSC  Niikura et al. 2019b , Kepler (K) Griest et al. 2014 , EROS Tisserand et al. 2007 , OGLE Niikura et al. 2019a , MACHO (M) Alcock et al. 2001a and supernovae (SNe) Zumalacárregui and Seljak 2018 , respectively.
The red and blue regions on the right are excluded by LIGO/Virgo Vaskonen and Veermäe 2020 and Planck data Ali-Haïmoud and Kamionkowski 2017 .
Various dynamical exclusions are indicated by broken lines, in orange for survival of stars in Segue I (Seg I) Koushiappas and Loeb 2017 and Eridanus II (Eri II) Brandt 2016 and in green for survival of wide binaries (WB) Monroy-Rodríguez and Allen 2014 .
The black solid lines show the combined constraint.
 Right panel: Equivalent constraints for a lognormal PBH mass function with σ = 2 \sigma=2 as a function of M c M_{c}  . 
 Figure 21: Observational constraints on M c M_{c} and σ \sigma for a lognormal PBH mass function; this is an updated version of Fig. 2 in Ref.  Carr et al. 2017a (Raidal, Vaskonen & Veermae, private communication).
The color coding shows the maximum allowed fraction of PBH dark matter.
In the white region f max < 10 − 3 f_{\mathrm{max}}<10^{-3} , while the dashed white contour correspond to f max = 1 f_{\mathrm{max}}=1 . 
 IV.3 Combined PBH and particle dark matter 
 If most of the DM is in the form of elementary particles, these will be accreted around any PBHs to form minihalos.
For weakly interacting massive particles (WIMPs) and light enough PBHs, this can even happen during the radiation-dominated era, since Eroshenko Eroshenko 2016 has shown that a low-velocity subset of particles will accumulate around PBHs as density spikes shortly after the WIMPs kinetically decouple from the background plasma.
For somewhat heavier PBHs, WIMP accretion is not important until after the time of matter-radiation equality ( t eq t_{\mathrm{eq}} ) and it then arises due to secondary infall.
Both cases lead to a similar halo profile and the subsequent annihilation of the WIMPs within the halos will give rise to bright γ \gamma -ray sources.
Comparison of the expected signal with Fermi-LAT data then severely constrains f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} for PBHs larger than about 10 − 12 ​ M ⊙ 10^{-12}\,M_{\odot}  .
Indeed, this leads to much stronger limits than any other constraint discussed in this review. 
 Figure  22 shows constraints on f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} for WIMP masses of 10 ​ GeV 10\,\mathrm{GeV} , 100 ​ GeV 100\,\mathrm{GeV} and 1 ​ TeV 1\,\mathrm{TeV} and the standard value for the velocity-averaged annihilation cross-section, ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ F = 3 × 10 − 26 ​ cm ​ s − 1 \langle\sigma v\rangle_{\mathrm{F}}=3\times 10^{-26}\,\mathrm{cm}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} .
The falling limit at low M M is associated with halos formed after kinetic decoupling and is taken from Ref.  Carr and Kühnel 2020 .
Boucenna et al.   Boucenna et al. 2018 have also investigated this constraint but for a larger range of values for ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ \langle\sigma v\rangle and m χ m_{\chi} .
The flat limit at larger M M is associated with halos formed by secondary infall and comes from the background generated by the integrated emission of many WIMP halos.
It corresponds to the ( M M -independent) constraint f PBH ≲ 10 − 10 f_{\mathrm{PBH}}\lesssim 10^{-10} for the assumed values of ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ \langle\sigma v\rangle and m χ m_{\chi}  .
Adamek et al.   Adamek et al. 2019 first derived this limit for solar-mass PBHs but the argument can be extended to much larger masses, as discussed in Ref.  Carr et al. 2021b . 
 More precisely, the flat limit on the DM fraction can be expressed as: 
 f PBH < 16 3 ​ Φ 100 ​ MeV Fermi ​ H 0 ρ DM ​ N ~ γ ​ ( m χ ) ​ ( 2 ​ m χ 4 ​ t 0 2 ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ ​ ρ eq ) 1 / 3 , f_{\mathrm{PBH}}<\frac{16}{3}\,\frac{\Phi^{\mathrm{Fermi}}_{100\,\mathrm{MeV}}\,H_{0}}{\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}\,\tilde{N}_{\gamma}(m_{\chi})}\,\left(\frac{2m_{\chi}^{4}\,t_{0}^{2}}{\langle\sigma v\rangle\,\rho_{\mathrm{eq}}}\right)^{1/3}\,, 
 (119) 
 where Φ 100 ​ MeV Fermi = 6 × 10 − 9 ​ cm − 2 ​ s − 1 \Phi^{\mathrm{Fermi}}_{100\,\mathrm{MeV}}=6\times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} is the Fermi point-source sensitivity above the threshold energy E th = 100 ​ MeV E_{\mathrm{th}}=100\,\mathrm{MeV} and ρ eq = 2.1 × 10 − 19 ​ g ​ cm − 3 \rho_{\mathrm{eq}}=2.1\times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{g}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3} is the density at t eq t_{\mathrm{eq}}  .
 N ~ γ \tilde{N}_{\gamma} is the average number of photons produced, 
 N ~ γ ​ ( m χ ) ≡ ∫ E th m χ d ​ E ​ d ​ N γ d ​ E ​ ∫ 0 ∞ d ​ z ​ H 0 H ⁡ ( z ) ​ exp ⁡ [ − τ ⁡ ( z , E ) ] , \tilde{N}_{\gamma}(m_{\chi})\equiv\int_{E_{\mathrm{th}}}^{m_{\chi}}\mathrm{d}E\,\frac{dN_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{d}E}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{H_{0}}{H(z)}\,\exp\left[-\tau(z,E)\right]\,, 
 (120) 
 where d ​ N γ / d ​ E \mathrm{d}N_{\gamma}/\mathrm{d}E is the number of γ \gamma -rays emitted from the annihilations per unit energy and τ \tau is the optical depth for photon-matter pair production, photon-photon scattering and photon-photon pair production  Cirelli et al. 2010 .
No bound can be placed above the mass where the lines intersects the cosmological incredulity limit 
 M IL = 2.5 × 10 10 ( m χ 100 ​ GeV ) 1.11 ( ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ F ) − 1 / 3 M ⊙ . M_{\mathrm{IL}}=2.5\times 10^{10}\,\left(\frac{m_{\chi}}{100\,\mathrm{GeV}}\right)^{1.11}\,\left(\frac{\langle\sigma v\rangle}{\langle\sigma v\rangle_{\mathrm{F}}}\right)^{-1/3}\,M_{\odot}\,. 
 (121) 
 These constraints are not included in Fig.  15 , since they depend on the assumption that the DM is dominated by WIMPs, but they have the important implication that there cannot be an appreciable amount of DM in both WIMPs and PBHs.
As discussed by Kadota and Silk  Kadota and Silk 2021 , an interesting application of this limit arises if the first bound cosmic structures are minihalos formed from PBH Poisson fluctuations (cf. Sec.  III.4.3 ). 
 Figure 22: Constraints on f PBH f_{\mathrm{PBH}} as a function of PBH mass, adapted from Ref.  Carr et al. , for a WIMP mass of 10 ​ GeV 10\,\mathrm{GeV} (dashed line), 100 ​ GeV 100\,\mathrm{GeV} (dashed-dotted line) and 1 ​ TeV 1\,\mathrm{TeV} (dotted line), with ⟨ σ ​ v ⟩ = 3 × 10 − 26 ​ cm 3 ​ s − 1 \langle\sigma v\rangle=3\times 10^{-26}\,\mathrm{cm}^{3}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1} .
Also shown is the incredulity limit (solid line), corresponding to one PBH per horizon. 
 V Conclusions 
 Although this review has focused on PBH constraints, our motivation has not been to exclude their existence, so the large number of limits discussed should not merely be regarded as ‘nails in the coffin’ of the PBH scenario.
All the constraints in Fig.  10 come with caveats, either because of uncertainties in the observations or because of flexibility in the models.
Some limits can be relaxed in various ways (e.g. by invoking PBH clustering for the lensing constraints or non-Gaussian fluctuations for the μ \mu distortion constraints), although these ‘let-outs’ may themselves be controversial.
Ideally, all the constraints should have 95 % confidence level but calculating a confidence level is difficult in some cases.
While we have tried to cover all the literature for historical completeness, we have represented constraints which are questionable or no longer believed by broken lines in our figures. 
 The constraints also have a positive aspect in that they could each in principle provide evidence for PBHs.
Indeed, historically some of them have already been thought to do so.
For example, microlensing of stars in the LMC once suggested that PBHs with M ∼ 1 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 1\,M_{\odot} could provide the dark matter in the Galactic halo  Alcock et al. 2000 ;
later observations seemed to exclude this but the source of the extra events remains unclear, so there could still be some PBHs.
There were also claims to have detected PBHs with M ∼ 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 10^{-3}\,M_{\odot} from microlensing of quasars  Hawkins 1993 , although this mass estimate was later revised upwards.
At one stage it was argued that PBHs with M ∼ 10 6 ​ M ⊙ M\sim 10^{6}\,M_{\odot} could generate the observed heating of the Galactic disc  Lacey and Ostriker 1985 , although this now seems unlikely.
It was once proposed that accretion by massive PBHs could generate the extragalactic X-ray background  Carr 1980b and that exploding PBHs with M ∼ 10 15 ​ g M\sim 10^{15}\,\mathrm{g} could explain some short-period gamma-ray bursts  Cline et al. 1997 . 
 Most of these claims have now been superseded but there is great current interest in whether PBHs can explain other cosmological conundra.
In particular, many authors have discussed the possibility that PBHs could provide the dark matter  Carr and Kühnel 2020 ; Green and Kavanagh 2021 .
In this context, Fig.  10 might suggest that the asteroid range 10 17 ​ g < M < 10 23 ​ g 10^{17}\,\mathrm{g}<M<10^{23}\,\mathrm{g} between the EGB and HSC microlensing limits is most plausible.
However, the intermediate range 10 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 2 ​ M ⊙ 10\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{2}\,M_{\odot} between the LMC microlensing and wide binary limits is still favored by some theorists, even though this appears to violate other constraints, because PBHs may naturally form in this range.
In principle, Fig.  10 suggests that a lot of dark matter could reside in “stupendously large black holes” in the range 10 14 ​ M ⊙ < M < 10 17 ​ M ⊙ 10^{14}\,M_{\odot}<M<10^{17}\,M_{\odot}  , although the lack of constraints there probably just reflects the fact that little attention has been paid to this possibility and obviously such SLABs could not provide dark matter inside galactic halos  Carr et al. 2021b .
A final possibility is the Planck-mass relics of Hawking evaporation in the range around M ∼ 10 − 5 ​ g M\sim 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{g} , although this is probably untestable unless the relics are electrically charged  Lehmann et al. 2019 . 
 PBHs could have important cosmological consequences even if f f is small.
For example, we have emphasised the possible association of PBHs with the LIGO/Virgo events, the merging black holes having masses in the range 8 ​ – ​ 50 ​ M ⊙ 8\text{--}50\,M_{\odot}  .
While the mainstream view may be that these are stellar remnants Belczynski et al. 2016 , this range is certainly larger than was originally expected for such remnants, so PBHs offer a plausible alternative.
On the other hand, it is unlikely that the LIGO/Virgo black holes themselves can provide all the dark matter, although PBHs with an extended mass function still might do so because one would then expect the PBH density to peak at a lower mass than the LIGO/Virgo events.
The viability of this proposal depends on whether the binaries form in the early Universe or much later in galactic halos.
In any case, it is clear that LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA observations will soon confirm or exclude this proposal.
As another example, we have seen that intermediate-mass PBHs could influence the development of large-scale cosmic structure for f ∼ 10 − 3 f\sim 10^{-3} and they could seed the SMBHs in galactic nuclei for f ∼ 10 − 5 f\sim 10^{-5} . 
 The three proposed roles for PBHs discussed above - accounting for dark matter, LIGO/Virgo events, seeds for large-scale structure - are essentially independent if the PBHs have a monochromatic mass function.
However, if they have a very extended mass function, they could in principle play all three roles.
Indeed, Ref.  Carr et al. 2021a suggests that the thermal history of the Universe could naturally produce a PBH mass spectrum with peaks at 10 − 6 10^{-6} , 1 1 , 30 30 and 10 6 ​ M ⊙ 10^{6}\,M_{\odot} and that this might provide a unified solution of a multitude of observational conundra:
(1) microlensing events towards the Galactic bulge generated by planetary-mass objects, these being much more frequent than expected for free-floating planets  Niikura et al. 2019a ;
(2) microlensing of quasars Mediavilla et al. 2017 , including ones that are so misaligned with the lensing galaxy that the probability of lensing by a star is very low;
(3) the unexpectedly high number of microlensing events towards the Galactic bulge by objects between 2 2 and 5 ​ M ⊙ 5\,M_{\odot} Wyrzykowski and Mandel 2020 , where stellar evolution models fail to form black holes Brown et al. ;
(4) unexplained correlations in the source-subtracted X-ray and cosmic infrared background fluctuations Cappelluti et al. 2013 ;
(5) the non-observation of ultra-faint dwarf galaxies below the critical radius associated with dynamical disruption by PBHs Clesse and García-Bellido 2018 ;
(6) the unexplained correlation between the masses of galaxies and their central SMBHs;
(7) the observed mass and spin distributions for the coalescing black holes found by LIGO/Virgo Abbott et al. 2019b .
There are additional observational problems which Silk has argued may be solved by PBHs in the intermediate mass range Silk 2017 . 
 Even if PBHs turn out to play none of these roles, their non-detection over various mass ranges still provides important constraints on the early Universe.
In particular, the limits on β ⁡ ( M ) \beta(M) can be used to constrain models involving inflation, an early matter-dominated phase or the collapse of cosmic strings.
They also restrict the form of the primordial inhomogeneities and their statistical distribution.
Although we have not discussed them here, the PBH limits could also constrain more exotic models of the early Universe, such as those involving a variable gravitational constant  Harada et al. 2002 or a different number of spatial dimensions  Guedens et al. 2002a ; Majumdar 2003 ; Guedens et al. 2002b ; Clancy et al. 2003 ; Sendouda et al. 2003 ; Sendouda et al. 2005 .
In the latter context, it is well known that the existence of extra dimensions which are large compared to the Planck length could permit the formation of black holes in accelerators  Giddings and Thomas 2002 .
Even though such black holes are not themselves primordial, the existence of the extra dimensions in the early Universe would have important implications for PBH formation.
